The Sūtra Called the Wise and the Foolish
Catalog
Toh 341 (Tōhoku catalogue; also cited as D 341 of the Derge edition) · Derge Kangyur · Sūtras (mdo sde) · Discourses (Sūtra)

About this text

The Sūtra Called the Wise and the Foolish, known in Tibetan as Dzanglun, is Toh 341 in the Kangyur, where it is placed in the General Sūtra section. Unlike most canonical sūtras it has no Sanskrit title line, and its own colophon notes that it appears to have been translated from Chinese; it corresponds to a collection compiled in Central Asia from stories heard by Chinese monks, and the Sanskrit title sometimes proposed for it is only a modern reconstruction. The work is a large anthology of fifty-one chapters of narrative, opening with the Buddha, newly awakened in Magadha, considering whether to pass into nirvāṇa and being moved to teach. Each chapter tells a story set in the Buddha's lifetime and then reveals its roots in past lives, showing through vivid tales of kings, merchants, monks and animals how generosity, faith and restraint ripen into happiness and liberation, while harsh words and ill will bring long suffering. Beloved in Tibet and Mongolia as a source of teaching stories, it illustrates the workings of karma in a way that is accessible to every listener.

Translation

The Sūtra Called The Wise and the Foolish. Fascicle One. The Chapter Presenting Various Examples.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One (the Buddha) was dwelling in the land of Magadha, at the place of complete victory. Not long after he had attained complete and perfect awakening, he had this thought: "These sentient beings have long been thoroughly corrupted by wrong views, and they are extremely difficult to teach and to correct. However long I remain in the world, it will bring them no benefit. Therefore I shall pass fully beyond sorrow into the nirvāṇa without remainder."

The very moment he had this thought, the gods of Brahmā's realm knew and understood the Blessed One's intention. They descended from the sky and came to where the Blessed One was. Having arrived there, they bowed their heads at his feet, joined their palms, and requested and urged the Blessed One to turn the wheel of Dharma. To them the Blessed One declared, "Gods of Brahmā's realm, these sentient beings are thoroughly stained by the defilement of wrongdoing. Attached to worldly pleasures, they have no mind endowed with wisdom. However long I remain in the world, the work will be pointless, and so I think that it would be happiness to pass into nirvāṇa."

Then the gods of Brahmā's realm again addressed the Blessed One: "Blessed One, the ocean of Dharma is completely full, the banner of Dharma has been planted, and the time for teaching and training has come. The sentient beings who will be liberated are also very many. Why then does the Blessed One intend to pass into nirvāṇa, and not become the protector and refuge of these who are so deeply blinded? Blessed One, in the past, beyond countless eons gone by, when you sought learning for the sake of sentient beings, you gave up your own body, your wives, your sons, and your daughters even for the sake of as little as a single verse. Why do you now intend to abandon these who have no protector?

"Long ago, in a past age, there arose here in Jambudvīpa a great king called Excellent Color, who held sway over this world. That king possessed eighty-four thousand vassal kings, and within sixty thousand great provinces he possessed eight hundred thousand cities. That king had twenty thousand queens and some ten thousand great ministers. The splendor and might of King Excellent Color were great beyond compare. Endowed with wealth, happiness, good harvests, and a great and teeming population of humans and other beings, he thought: 'I have benefited all beings with jewels and riches, but I have not benefited them with the true Dharma. This will be my failing. I shall seek out wherever the true Dharma is to be found, and liberate them all.' Having thought this, he issued a decree: 'Whoever in this Jambudvīpa possesses the true Dharma and teaches it to me, I will give him whatever he wishes, without going against his mind.' Yet though this was proclaimed, no one appeared who was able to teach it.

"At that time the king's mind was tormented with grief and he became joyless. The Reliever heard of this and, in order to rouse him, transformed his own body into a yakṣa. His color was a deep blue-black, his eyes were red as blood, his fangs jutted upward, his hair bristled on end, and flames blazed from his mouth. He came to the palace gate and said, 'Who wishes to hear the Dharma? Listen to me!'

"When the king heard these words, he was overjoyed. He went out to meet the yakṣa, bowed his head to him, and seated him on a high seat. He assembled his ministers and a great retinue and had them sit around him in a circle so that they might listen to the Dharma. Then the yakṣa said to the king, 'O king, hearing the Dharma is extremely difficult. If you wish to hear the Dharma, this alone is not enough.' The king joined his palms toward him and said, 'Whatever you wish, I will give it all without refusing.' Then the yakṣa said to the king, 'O king, if you let me eat whichever of your queens and sons you hold most dear, I will give you the true Dharma.' At that, the great king offered to the yakṣa the dearest among his queens and the foremost of his sons, and the yakṣa ate them in the midst of the great assembly while everyone looked on.

"At that time, when the king's ministers saw what had happened, they cried out and, rolling on the ground, pleaded, 'Great king, give up such improper deeds as these!' But the king was utterly steadfast in seeking the true Dharma, and they could not turn him back. Then the yakṣa, having eaten the king's queen and son, spoke this verse:

All formations are impermanent;
Whatever is born is bound up with suffering;
The five aggregates are empty, being without signs;
There is neither self nor anything belonging to a self.

"When he had spoken this verse, the king was overjoyed and delighted, and not even a hair's breadth of regret arose in his mind. Then he had it written down and proclaimed throughout all of Jambudvīpa, and made everyone study it. Then the Reliever changed back into his own body and praised and extolled the king, saying, 'Excellent indeed! How wonderful and marvelous!' And the king's queen and son were, as before, entirely unharmed.

"The king at that time was the Buddha. Blessed One, if in the past you undertook such hardships for the sake of the Dharma, why now abandon so many sentient beings and pass into nirvāṇa?" So they said.

"Blessed One, furthermore, in the past, beyond countless eons, there was here in Jambudvīpa a great king called Kanaśinipali, who ruled over many vassal kings. He held some eighty-four thousand cities, had twenty thousand queens and palace women, and also some ten thousand great ministers. That king was deeply compassionate, and he became like a father and mother to the many people and other beings. At that time the king thought thus:

'I have become the chief of all these many beings, and these many people also are devoted to me and love me. I shall benefit them all with the precious jewel of the true Dharma.' Having thought this, he had a minister proclaim a decree everywhere, commanding: 'Whoever possesses the true Dharma and teaches it to me, I will give him whatever he wishes, just as he desires.'

"At that time a brahmin called Leuduci came to the palace gate and said, 'I have the true Dharma.' When the king heard these words, he went out to meet him, paid him homage, and seated him on a seat that had been spread for him. When the brahmin had sat down on the seat, the king and his retinue joined their palms and urged him, 'Great teacher, the time has come to expound and teach the true Dharma to those blinded by delusion.' The brahmin said to the king, 'I did not learn this knowledge so easily. If you wish to hear it, this alone is not enough.' The king said, 'Tell me whatever you wish, and I will give it all just as you desire.' The brahmin said to the king, 'Great king, if you are able to make an offering by planting a thousand lamps in your body, I shall teach the Dharma.'

"The king was overjoyed and delighted. He then dispatched a messenger on an elephant that could travel a hundred thousand leagues, and had this proclamation made throughout all of Jambudvīpa. To the vassal kings and all the people it was announced: 'In seven days from now, King Kanaśinipali will pierce the flesh of his body for the sake of the Dharma and plant a thousand lamps in it.'

"When they heard this proclamation, each and every one of them was tormented with grief. They came to where the king was, paid homage, and all said this: 'All who live and breathe in this world rely on you, O king, just as a blind man relies on his guide, or a small child relies on its mother. If the king is no longer here, to whom shall we go for refuge? If you plant a thousand lamps in your body, you will certainly lose your life. Why would you abandon the beings of this world for the sake of a single brahmin?'

"At that time the queens, the sons, the crown princes, five hundred palace women, and some ten thousand ministers joined their palms and implored him. But the king was steadfast in his resolve and was not turned back. He said, 'When I attain full awakening, I will surely liberate you all.' Then the many people realized that the king was steadfast in his resolve. They were deeply distressed, cried out, and fell to the ground.

"The king's intention did not waver, and he said to the brahmin, 'The time has come to plant the thousand lamps in my body. Pierce it!' The brahmin pierced the king's body and smeared the lamp wicks with oil. When the great retinue saw this happen, they threw themselves to the ground like falling mountains.

"Then the king said to the brahmin, 'Great teacher, please be kind. First teach me the Dharma, and plant the lamps after that. If I should die, I would not get to hear the Dharma.' The brahmin then spoke these verses:

All that is permanent will come to an end;
What is lofty also falls at last;
Where there is meeting, parting will come;
Where there is birth, there will be death.

"The moment he had spoken these verses, the king was overjoyed and delighted, and without any thought of regret he made this pledge: 'This seeking of the Dharma of mine is for the sake of attaining buddhahood. When I later attain complete and perfect awakening, may the light of my wisdom shine brilliantly upon the beings blinded by delusion!' The moment he made this vow, heaven and earth shook violently. The shaking reached as far as the palaces of the gods of the Pure Abodes, and those gods descended from the sky and came to the place where he was making the offering of his body. When the gods saw the bodhisattva's body ruined in this way, they filled the whole sky above and wept. Their tears fell like rain, and they also rained down divine flowers and made offerings.

"At that time Śakra (Indra), lord of the gods, also came to where the king was, praised and extolled him in many ways, and said, 'Great king, tormented by such intense suffering as this, do you not feel regret?' The king said, 'I have no regret.' Śakra said, 'Great king, how can one believe your words that you have no regret, when your body is trembling like this and you are in such distress?' The king said, 'If from first to last I have had no thought of regret, may the wounds of my body heal as they were before!' The moment he said this, his body became just as it was before, without any wound.

"The king at that time, in that age, was the Buddha. Blessed One, if in the past too you endured unbearable suffering for the sake of sentient beings and so found the Dharma, why do you now intend to abandon all beings, bereft of the light of the Dharma, and pass into nirvāṇa?

"Furthermore, Blessed One, in a past age there was here in Jambudvīpa a great king called Byinglinggarli, who ruled over the vassal kings. He possessed eighty-four thousand cities and had twenty thousand queens and palace women, five hundred crown princes and princes, and ten thousand great ministers. That king was compassionate and regarded all his subjects as if each were his only son. At that time, because the great king was devoted to and delighted in the true Dharma, he commanded a minister to issue this decree: 'Whoever it may be who possesses a sūtra or the true Dharma and teaches it to me, I will give him whatever he wishes, just as he desires.'

"Then a brahmin called Leunguchu came to the palace gate and said, 'I have the true Dharma, and I shall teach it to whoever wishes to hear it.' When the king heard what he said, he was overjoyed and delighted. He went out to meet him, bowed his head at his feet, spoke pleasant words to him, and led him into the palace. He seated him on a seat that had been spread for him, joined his palms, and said this:

'Great teacher, please teach me the true Dharma.' The brahmin said, 'I experienced all kinds of suffering in my body and spent a very long time before I finally learned it. Great king, if you wish to hear it, this alone is not enough.' The king joined his palms and said, 'Tell me whatever you wish. Teacher, I will offer you everything, holding nothing back.' The brahmin said, 'If a thousand iron nails are driven into your body, I will give you the Dharma.' The king said, 'I shall do just that. Seven days from now I shall make these things ready.'

"At that time the king dispatched a messenger on an elephant that could travel some eighty-four thousand leagues, and had this proclaimed throughout Jambudvīpa: 'Seven days from now the great king Byinglinggarli will have a thousand iron nails driven into his body.' When the ministers and the many beings heard this proclamation, they all came before the king and said, 'All of us beings living in the four directions each enjoy happiness and well-being through the king's virtues and kindness. Great king, we now implore you for our sake: please do not have a thousand iron nails driven into your body.' At that time the queens, the palace women, the crown princes, the princes, and all the ministers also implored him: 'Great king, please have compassion on us. It is not right to die for the sake of one man and abandon all your subjects.'

"To this the king replied, 'For a long time I have wasted countless bodies in saṃsāra. At times, through attachment, aversion, and delusion, this body was killed; the white bones heaped up from that are higher than Mount Meru. The blood shed when my head was cut off is greater than the five great rivers. The tears I have wept exceed the four oceans. Though I have wasted body and life in all these various ways, not once was it for the sake of the Dharma. Now I shall have the nails driven in and so attain buddhahood. When I later attain full awakening and cut through the disease of your afflictions with the sword of wisdom, why do you obstruct my mind of awakening?' At that the great assembly fell completely silent.

"Then the king said to the brahmin, 'Great teacher, please be kind. First teach the Dharma, and after that drive in the iron nails. If I should die, I would not get to hear the Dharma.' The brahmin then spoke in verse:

All formations are impermanent;
All that is born is bound up with suffering;
All phenomena are empty and without an owner;
Nor is there anything that belongs to a self.

"Having spoken this verse, he drove a thousand iron nails into the king's body. At that time, when the vassal kings, the ministers, and the great retinue saw this happen, they threw themselves to the ground like great mountains toppling, wept, and rolled on the ground, losing all awareness. Then heaven and earth shook in six ways. The gods of the desire realm and the form realm were amazed. They all descended from the sky and came before the bodhisattva. When they saw him enduring great suffering in his body for the sake of the Dharma, they all wept, their tears falling like rain, and they made offerings with divine flowers.

"At that time Śakra, lord of the gods, came to where the king was and said, 'Great king, you have undertaken intense diligence and pay no heed to suffering. Is this for the sake of the Dharma? Or do you wish to become Śakra, or a universal monarch? Or do you wish to become the king of the māras, or the king Brahmā, or Maheśvara (Śiva)?' The king said, 'What I have done is not out of a wish to enjoy the happiness of the three realms. All the merit there is from it is for the sake of seeking buddhahood.' Śakra said, 'When I look at you, the king's body is trembling and in unbearable pain. How can one believe you when you say this and claim to have no regret?' The king said, 'If what I have said is true and I have no thought of regret, may my body become just as it was before, without any wound!' The moment he said this, it became just as before, and gods and humans were overjoyed and delighted.

"If at that time too you sought the Dharma in order to protect many beings, then now that the Blessed One has completely fulfilled the ocean of Dharma and is endowed with immeasurable qualities, why do you intend to abandon so many beings and pass into nirvāṇa without even teaching the Dharma?

"Furthermore, Blessed One, in a past age, beyond immeasurable and countless eons, there was here in Jambudvīpa a great king called Brahmadeva, who had a crown prince called Damgam. This prince was devoted to and delighted in the true Dharma, but though he searched for the true Dharma in every direction, he could not find it. He was tormented with grief and fell into despair. At that time Śakra, lord of the gods, fully knew and understood his thoughts. He transformed himself into a brahmin, came to the gate of the royal palace, and said, 'I have the true Dharma, and I shall teach it.'

"When the prince heard this, he went out to meet him, paid homage at his feet, led him into the palace, and seated him on a seat that had been spread for him. The prince joined his palms and said, 'Great teacher, out of your kindness, please teach the true Dharma.' The brahmin said, 'Studying and learning it is extremely hard, and it is not easy to attend on a master and come to understand it either. This alone will not suffice; it is not right merely to wish to hear the Dharma.' The prince said, 'Great teacher, tell me whatever you wish. Even if you want my body, my wife, and my sons, I will hold nothing back, and I will do as you command.' The brahmin said, 'If you fill a fire pit ten cubits deep with glowing embers and leap into it as an offering, I shall teach you the true Dharma.' At that time the prince made a great fire pit, just as the master had instructed.

"Then, when the king, the queens, the ministers, and the palace women heard what had come about, they were distressed in body and mind. They all came to the prince's palace and implored the prince. They also all earnestly surrounded the brahmin and pleaded, 'Venerable one, please have compassion. For our sake, please do not make this prince leap into the fire pit. If you want our retinues, our wives, our daughters, or even our own bodies, we will offer them.' The brahmin said, 'I am not forcing anyone to do anything; it is simply what the prince has undertaken. If he is able to do it, I shall teach the Dharma. If he is not able, I shall not teach the Dharma.' The retinue, knowing and understanding that the prince's pledge was firm, fell completely silent.

"At that time the king, too, dispatched a messenger on an elephant that could travel a hundred thousand leagues, sending him throughout all of Jambudvīpa with this proclamation: 'Seven days from now Crown Prince Damgam will leap into a fire pit out of his longing for the Dharma. If you wish to see him, come quickly!' Then the vassal kings and the many subjects, weeping and feeble, led one another along and came before the prince. They set their right knees on the ground, joined their palms, and all said with one voice, 'We rely on you, prince. You are like a father and mother to us. If you leap into the fire pit, all these subjects will be left without protector, refuge, or shelter. Do not, for the sake of a mere few people, leave all your subjects without an ally.'

"Then the prince addressed the many people with these words: 'For a long time, as I have wandered in saṃsāra, I have passed through countless bodies. When I was born among humans, never satisfied by desire, we harmed and injured one another. When I was born as a god in the heavens, when the god's lifespan ran out I experienced the suffering of being parted from what I desired. When I was born as a being in the hells, I was burned in fire, boiled in copper cauldrons, hacked and chopped by swords and weapons, plunged into poisonous waters, and made to climb ridges of sword blades; even in a single day I underwent countless deaths, and I experienced the sufferings of sickness and death, pain that pierced to the bone, beyond what words can tell. When I was born as a hungry ghost, I experienced all kinds of suffering that the body could not bear. In the birthplaces of animals, my very body was wasted: loaded with heavy burdens, eating nothing but grass, I experienced suffering hard to endure. Though I wasted body and life, never once did I give rise to even the slightest virtuous thought for the sake of the true Dharma. Now I shall make an offering of this foul and lowly body for the sake of the Dharma. Do not act to obstruct my generating the mind set on unsurpassed awakening! In order to attain buddhahood I shall give up this body entirely, and when I attain full awakening I shall give you the body of Dharma.' At that the great assembly fell silent.

"At that time the prince stood at the edge of the fire pit and said to the brahmin, 'Great teacher, first teach me the true Dharma. If I should die, I would not hear the Dharma.' The brahmin then spoke these verses:

Cultivate the mind of loving-kindness;
Abandon perceptions of aversion and wrath;
With great compassion, protect sentient beings;
Shed tears of tender love for all.
Cultivate great joy;
For others too, the Dharma that accords with your own:
To protect them with the mind of awakening
Is to be endowed with the conduct of a bodhisattva.

"The moment this verse had been spoken, as he was leaping into the fire pit, Śakra and King Brahmā each seized one of his hands and said, 'All the beings of Jambudvīpa, through your kindness, O king, have not the least unhappiness. If you now leap into the fire pit, it will be as if the father and mother of all your subjects had died. Why do you abandon all beings and leap into the fire pit?' The prince looked at the king of the gods and the great assembly and said, 'Do not act to obstruct my generating the mind set on unsurpassed awakening!' They all fell completely silent, and at once he leapt into the fire pit.

"At that moment heaven and earth shook violently. The gods of the sky wept, and their tears fell like rain. The fire pit turned into a pool of flowers, the prince was found seated on a lotus, and all the gods rained down flowers until they lay knee-deep.

"King Brahmadeva at that time, in that age, is King Śuddhodana, the father. His mother at that time, in that age, is Queen Māyā, the mother. The prince, the crown prince, at that time, in that age, was the Blessed One. Blessed One, if at that time, in that age, you sought the Dharma in order to protect many beings, why now, when it has been brought to completion, do you intend to pass into nirvāṇa without letting the rain of Dharma fall on the many sentient beings who are so tormented?

"Furthermore, Blessed One, beyond countless and immeasurable eons gone by, at that time, in that age, five hundred sages lived here in the land of Vārāṇasī. Their teacher, called Utpala, delighted in studying and meditating on the true Dharma, and he wandered everywhere, proclaiming, 'Whoever possesses the true Dharma and tells it to me, I will give him whatever he wishes, just as he desires, and I will be his servant.' A brahmin came there and said to him, 'I have the true Dharma. If you wish to hear it, I shall teach it.' When the sage heard this, he joined his palms and said, 'Out of kindness to me, please teach the true Dharma.' The brahmin said, 'Learning the Dharma is extremely hard; I learned the Dharma by undergoing much suffering. It is not right to wish to hear the Dharma for this alone. If you wish from your heart to hear it, do as I command.' The sage said, 'I will do as the great teacher commands, without disobeying.' Then the brahmin commanded him thus:

'If you flay your skin and make it into paper, make your bone into a pen, and make your blood into ink, and write down the Dharma that I teach, I shall teach you the Dharma.' When the sage heard these words, he was overjoyed and delighted. In order to honor the teaching of the Tathāgata, he flayed his skin and made it into paper, made his bone into a pen, made his blood into ink, and said, 'The time has come; speak quickly!' The brahmin then spoke these verses:

Restrain well the conduct of the body;
Do not kill, steal, or commit sexual misconduct;
Do not speak divisive words, lies, harsh words,
Or idle chatter.
Do not be attached to sensual pleasures,
And have no thought of aversion;
If you abandon all wrong views,
That is the conduct of a bodhisattva.

"The moment these verses had been spoken, he wrote them down and had them proclaimed throughout Jambudvīpa, and made all the many people study them and diligently practice them just as they had been taught. Blessed One, if at that time, in that age, you had no thought of regret in seeking the Dharma for the sake of many sentient beings, why do you now intend to abandon them all and pass into nirvāṇa without even teaching the Dharma?

"Furthermore, Blessed One, beyond countless and immeasurable eons gone by, there was here in Jambudvīpa a king called Śibi. The palace where that king lived was called Devabarta, and it was endowed with wealth, happiness, good harvests, and immeasurable riches. At that time this king ruled over Jambudvīpa. He held sway over eighty-four thousand vassal kings, eight hundred thousand cities within sixty thousand provinces, twenty thousand queens and palace women, about five hundred princes, and ten thousand great ministers. There was no one whom his loving-kindness and compassion did not reach.

"At that time Śakra, lord of the gods, had lost the five excellences of his body and was close to death, and so he was deeply unhappy. When Viśvakarman saw him grieving, he asked Śakra, 'Why are you grieving and unhappy?' Śakra said, 'I am close to death, and the omens of passing away have appeared. In this world the Dharma of the buddhas has declined, and no bodhisattva remains in the world either. I grieve because I do not know to whom I should go for refuge.' Viśvakarman said, 'Lord of the gods, in Jambudvīpa there lives a great king called Śibi who practices the conduct of a bodhisattva. He is utterly steadfast in his resolve and has undertaken great diligence, so he will certainly attain full awakening. If you go to him for refuge, he will certainly become your ally, and you will be freed from obstacles.'

"Śakra said, 'If he is a bodhisattva, we must test him first. Let us go and test him. You transform yourself into a dove. I shall transform myself into a hawk and swiftly follow after you. Go to where that king is, seek refuge, and we will test him, and so we will discover whether he is a fraud or genuine.' Viśvakarman said, 'Lord of the gods, a great being, a bodhisattva, is one worthy of offerings, not of harm. It is not right to torment him with so extremely hard a thing as this.' Śakra, lord of the gods, then spoke these verses:

I bear him no ill will;
Like gold, when tested, it becomes plain.
Since I wish to discover the truth,
I shall test the bodhisattva.

"Having spoken these verses, Viśvakarman transformed himself into a dove and Śakra transformed himself into a hawk. The hawk pursued the dove as if about to seize it, and the dove, terrified, flew into the king's armpit and begged the king to save its life. The hawk at once arrived too, perched near the palace, and said to the king, 'This dove is my food. It has come down here to the king, so give it to me quickly. I am tormented by hunger.' The king said, 'I have vowed never to turn away anyone who comes to me for refuge, so I will not give this one up either.' The hawk spoke again: 'If, as the king says, you are the refuge of all, then if you do not give me my food, I too will be deprived of life. Am I not also included among "all"?' The king said, 'If I give you some other meat, will you eat it?' The hawk said, 'If there is fresh meat, newly killed, I want it.'

"The king thought, 'If I provide fresh meat from some newly killed creature, it would not be right to kill one in order to save another; it would be pointless.' Having thought this, he thought further, 'Apart from my own body alone, all others that have life hold their lives dear.' Having thought this, he took up a sharp knife, cut flesh from his thigh, and gave it to the hawk, and so ransomed the dove's life. The hawk said, 'O king, you have become a generous patron and treat all alike. Though I am only a small bird, I ask that you treat me fairly too. If you are ransoming the dove's life, please weigh it on a scale and make the two equal.'

"The king commanded, 'Bring a scale!' The dove was placed in one pan of the scale, and the flesh he cut off was heaped in the other pan. Even when the flesh of his thighs was used up, it was lighter than the dove. Even when he had cut away all the flesh of both shoulders, of his right and left sides, and of his body, it did not equal the dove. Then the king himself rose and tried to climb into the scale, but he had no strength left; he fell to the ground and fainted.

"After a long while he regained consciousness and rebuked his own mind with these words: 'Since beginningless time, because of your incapacity, you have wandered in the three realms and experienced all kinds of suffering, yet it never became an accumulation of merit. The time has come to take up diligence; this is no time for laziness.' Having restrained himself with rebukes of many kinds, he then struggled with all his might and got into the pan of the scale, and a mind of great joy arose in him: 'Now it is well!'

"At that moment heaven and earth quaked in six ways, and even the palaces of the gods shook and swayed. The gods, up to those of the form realm, came down and stationed themselves in the sky above. When they saw these deeds of the bodhisattva, how he had practiced such extreme austerity and utterly ruined his body, paying no heed to body or life for the sake of the Dharma, their tears fell like rain and they wept, and they made offerings, raining down divine flowers like rain.

"Then Śakra changed back into his former body and spoke to the king: 'What does Your Majesty seek by performing such an extremely difficult deed as this? Do you seek to be a universal monarch? Or Śakra? Or the king of the māras? Or do you seek to be lord of the three realms? What is it that you seek?'

"The bodhisattva replied, 'What I desire is not the grand enjoyments of the three realms. Whatever merit I make, I desire unsurpassed awakening.'

"Śakra said, 'Your body has been broken and ruined like this, and you have experienced suffering that reaches to the bone. Do you not regret it?'

"The king said, 'I have no regret.'

"Śakra said, 'You say you have no regret, but when one sees how your body trembles and shakes, and your breath is all but cut off so that you cannot even speak, how can one believe that you have no regret?'

"The king said, 'If from first to last I have not had a thought of regret even as fine as the tip of a hair, then whatever I wish will come about without fail. If these words I have spoken are true, then by speaking these words of truth, may this body of mine become unwounded, just as it was before!'

"No sooner had he said this than the king's body became even more beautiful than before, and the whole world with its gods and humans was delighted, overjoyed and thrilled, and was filled with wonder.

"The King Śibi of that time and occasion is now the Buddha. Blessed One, if in past times too you did not regard your own life for the sake of many beings, then now, Blessed One, when your ocean of Dharma is complete, when you have raised the banners of Dharma, beaten the drum of Dharma, and made the lamp of Dharma shine brightly, and the time has come to work for benefit and happiness, why do you intend to abandon all beings and pass into nirvāṇa without teaching the Dharma?"

Then King Brahmā, his palms joined before the Tathāgata, praised and extolled him, recounting how the Tathāgata had sought the Dharma in his former lives and how, for the sake of beings, he had given up as many as a thousand heads.

Then the Blessed One granted what King Brahmā had requested, and going to the place in the land of Vārāṇasī where fearlessness was given to the deer, he turned the wheel of Dharma. Through his turning the wheel of Dharma in this way, the Three Jewels too became manifest in the world.

When he had spoken thus, all the gods, humans, nāgas, yakṣas, asuras and the rest of the eight classes rejoiced and followed his teaching.

This concludes the first chapter, the Teaching of Various Examples.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. Then, when the time had come for the alms round, the Blessed One put on his lower robe and Dharma robe, took up his alms bowl, and went for alms with Ānanda.

At that time in that city there was an old woman who had two sons who were always stealing. They were caught by the owner of the goods and taken before the magistrate, and when the law was applied to them it came to the point of execution. The low-caste executioners were leading them, terrified, to the place of execution, when the old woman and her sons, the three of them, saw the Blessed One coming from afar. They bowed down in the direction from which the Buddha was coming and said, "Chief among gods, have compassion on us and please become the refuge of these sons of mine, who have come to the point of death!"

The Blessed One heard them, and the Tathāgata, out of great compassion, felt tender love for them. In order to save their lives, the Blessed One instructed Ānanda and sent him to petition the king, and the king, in accordance with the Blessed One's word, released them.

Remembering the Blessed One's kindness, they felt great joy. They went to where the Blessed One was, bowed their heads at his feet, joined their palms, and said to the Blessed One, "Through the Blessed One's great kindness, what little remains of our lives has been spared. Chief among gods, have compassion on us, and would you grant us ordination into the Dharma?"

When the Blessed One said, "Welcome!" their hair and beards fell away of themselves and their clothes turned into saffron robes. Their minds became steadfast in deep faith, and when the Blessed One taught them the Dharma as suited them, their dust and stains were exhausted and they became arhats. Their old mother too, by hearing the Dharma, attained the state of no return.

Then Ānanda, seeing such wondrous things, praised him, saying, "So many are the qualities the Tathāgata possesses!" And he further thought, "What good did these three, the old woman and her sons, do in the past, that now they have met the Blessed One, have escaped from great peril, and have attained the bliss of nirvāṇa, gaining benefit and happiness in a single life? How excellent!"

The Blessed One knew this and said to Ānanda, "It is not only now, at this time alone, that I have saved this mother and her sons; in times past too they were saved through my kindness."

Ānanda said, "Blessed One, please explain how this mother and her sons were saved in times past as well."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "In times past, countless eons ago, there was in this Jambudvīpa a king called Mahāratha, who ruled over some five thousand vassal kings. That king had three sons. The eldest was called Mahāpraṇāda, the middle one was called Mahādeva, and the youngest was called Mahāsattva. From childhood the youngest son was endowed with loving-kindness and compassion, and regarded everyone as though they were his only child.

"At that time the king, together with his ministers, his queen and his sons, went out to stroll in the countryside. While they rested for a little while, the three sons went into a forest, and there they saw a tigress who had given birth not many days before. Tormented by hunger and thirst, she was on the point of eating her own cubs.

"The youngest prince said to his two elder brothers, 'This tigress is gravely afflicted by suffering. She is so weak and emaciated that she is near death, and she wants to eat even her newborn cubs.'

"The two elder brothers said, 'What you say is true.'

"The youngest brother then asked his two elder brothers, 'What does this tigress eat for food?'

"The two elder brothers said, 'Fresh flesh just killed and warm blood would satisfy her.'

"He spoke again, saying, 'Is there anyone at all who could save her life with such a thing and keep her from perishing?'

"The two elder brothers said, 'That would be extremely difficult, so there is no one.'

"Then the youngest prince thought to himself, 'For a long time I have wandered in saṃsāra and wasted countless bodies and lives. Sometimes it was for the sake of attachment, sometimes for the sake of aversion, sometimes for the sake of delusion. Though I have given up bodies, not once have I met with a field of merit for the sake of the Dharma. What use is this body?'

"Having thought this, he went on together with the other two, but before they had gone far he said to his two elder brothers, 'Brothers, go on ahead. I have some business to see to, and I will come after you.'

"Taking that same path back, he went quickly to where the tigress's den was and lay down in front of the tigress. But the tigress's jaws were clamped shut, and she could not eat. Then the prince drew blood from his body with a sharp splinter of wood and let the tigress lick it, and she opened her mouth and ate the flesh of his body, leaving none.

"The two elder brothers waited, and when he did not come for a long time, they went back along his tracks to search for him. When they considered what he had said earlier, they thought, 'Surely the hungry tigress has killed him.' They went there and looked, and saw that their youngest brother had been eaten by the tiger and lay in a heap, smeared all over with flesh and blood. They threw themselves to the ground and fainted. After a long while they came to, and wailing in lamentation they rolled on the ground and fainted again.

"At that time the queen, the mother, dreamed in her sleep that three doves were flying all around and playing, when a hawk carried off the smallest of them. As soon as she had dreamed this she awoke, and in terror she told the king, 'I have heard it said that doves are the life-spirit of sons. Since the smallest of the doves was carried off by a hawk, misfortune has surely befallen the dearest of my sons.' At once she sent people searching everywhere.

"Before long the two princes arrived, and she asked them, 'Has nothing happened to my dearest son, or what is it?' The two elder brothers were choked with sobs; for a long time they could not catch their breath or speak. Then, when they had recovered their breath, they said, 'He has been eaten by a tiger.'

"When the queen heard them say this, she fainted and fell to the ground. After a long while she recovered her breath, and together with the two sons, the king, and the palace retinue, she went quickly to the place where the prince had died. By then the tigress had finished eating all the prince's flesh, and they saw only bones and blood lying in a heap on the ground. The queen took hold of the head and the king took hold of the hands, and they wailed in lamentation and wept. There too they fainted, and after a long while they came to again.

"Prince Mahāsattva, having died there, was born in the abode of the gods of Tuṣita. He wondered, 'Why is this? What did I do to be born here?' and looked over the five realms of rebirth with his divine eye. He saw the bones of his own dead body lying in the forest, with his father and mother around them, heartbroken, pierced by the thorn of grief, suffering and lamenting.

"The god thought, 'Through this unhappy circumstance, my father and mother may come to harm in body and life. I will go to speak with them and cheer them.' Having thought this, he descended from the heavens, and from the sky above he cheered his father and mother with many kinds of sweet words.

"The father and mother looked up at the sky and said, 'God, tell us who you are!'

"The god said, 'I am the one called Prince Mahāsattva. Because I fed my body to the starving tigress, I have been born in the abode of the gods of Tuṣita. Great King, please understand this: all things bound up with craving come to destruction in the end. Where there is birth, there will certainly be destruction. If beings do evil, they fall into hell; if they do good, they are born in the higher realms. Since birth and destruction exist for all, why do you not see that you are falling into an ocean of grief merely for the sake of fondness? Exert yourselves in virtuous Dharma!'

"His father and mother said, 'Out of great compassion you fed yourself to the tigress and showed love to all, yet you abandoned us and died. Now, remembering you, we are tormented by suffering as though our flesh were being cut to pieces. Is it fitting that you, who practice great compassion, should act like this?'

"Then the god consoled them again with many kinds of sweet words and cheered them, and his father and mother too were somewhat cheered. They made a casket of the seven kinds of precious things, placed the bones inside it and buried it, and over it they built a stūpa. The god returned to his own abode, and the king and his large retinue returned to the palace."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "What do you think? Who do you suppose King Mahāratha was at that time, on that occasion? He is now my father, King Śuddhodana. The king's queen at that time, on that occasion, is now my mother Māyā. The eldest son at that time, Mahāpraṇāda, is Maitreya. The middle son, Mahādeva, is Vamisutra. As for the youngest prince at that time, on that occasion, Mahāsattva, do not think he was anyone else: he is now I myself. The tiger cubs at that time, on that occasion, are these two men. Long ago too I freed them from peril, saved their lives, and made them safe.

And now that I have attained perfect awakening, I have again freed them from peril, and they are completely liberated from the great suffering of saṃsāra."

Then Ānanda and all the large assembly rejoiced at what the Blessed One had said and praised it highly.

This concludes the second chapter, Mahāsattva Gives His Body to the Tigress.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada.

At that time, in the first watch of the night, two gods came to where the Buddha was. The light of those gods' bodies made the whole of Prince Jeta's Grove shine with a single golden hue. The Blessed One taught them the sublime Dharma as suited them, and their minds were liberated. Both of them attained the fruit of awakening, bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet, and went back to the abode of the gods.

The next day, as soon as dawn broke, Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "Last night two gods came and paid homage to the Blessed One, and the light of their bodies lit everything up and made it beautiful. What action did they do in the past to obtain such a result?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Long ago, after the Buddha Kāśyapa had passed into nirvāṇa and the sublime Dharma was close to vanishing, two brahmins took the eight precepts for one day and night. Of the two, one made the aspiration, 'May I be born as a god!' The other made the aspiration, 'May I become a king!' Then they went back to their homes.

"When one of them came home, his wife called him to eat, and the husband said to her, 'I have taken the precept from the Buddha's Dharma of not eating food in the afternoon, so I do not want any.'

"The wife said, 'You are a wandering ascetic (parivrājaka). Since we have the moral discipline of our own Dharma, why have you taken the precepts of another teacher? If you will not eat with me, I will tell the other wandering ascetics what you have said, and have you driven out, so that they will not allow you to gather or talk with them.'

"When she said this, the husband could not bear it; in fear and alarm he ate food with his wife at the wrong time.

"Those two men lived out whatever their lifespans allowed, and each of them died. The one who had aspired to be a king, because he had kept the precepts, was born into a royal family. The one who had aspired to be born as a god, because he had broken the precepts, was born as a nāga.

"At that time, a keeper of the orchard of the one who had been born as king brought him all kinds of fruit every day. Then at a later time, the keeper found in a spring an apple with fine color and fragrance, and he thought, 'Whenever I bring fruit and go back and forth, the gatekeeper always stops me and will not let me pass freely. I will give this apple to the gatekeeper.' So he gave it to the king's gatekeeper.

"The gatekeeper took the fruit and thought, 'Whenever I go to make a report or a request, the chamberlain always stops me, and I cannot go in with dignity. I will give this apple to the chamberlain.' So he gave it to the chamberlain. The chamberlain took it and offered it to the queen. The queen took it and offered it to the king.

"The king ate the apple and found it exceedingly sweet and especially fragrant, and he asked the queen, 'Where was this apple found?' The queen told him in detail how it had been obtained, and tracing it from one to the next they came at last to the orchard keeper. The king summoned the orchard keeper and said to him, 'If there is fruit like this in my orchard, why do you give it to others instead of offering it to me?' The orchard keeper explained in detail how he had first found it.

"Then the king said to the orchard keeper, 'From today on, always offer me apples like this one.'

"The orchard keeper replied, 'This apple did not grow on a fruit tree. I found it in a spring, so from now on I will not be able to find any; there will be none to be had.'

"To this the king said, 'If you do not find one from now on, I will have your body cut to pieces.'

"The orchard keeper went back to his place and grieved and wept. A nāga heard the loud sound of his weeping, and the nāga transformed himself into a man and asked him, 'Why are you unhappy and weeping?' The orchard keeper told the nāga in detail what had happened.

"The nāga vanished, and before long he filled a golden bowl with fine fruits and gave it to the man, saying, 'Take this fruit and offer it to the king, and give him this message as well: "The king and I are friends. In a former life we were both wandering ascetics, and we took the eight precepts for one day and night and each made an aspiration. Because the king kept the precepts he became a king, but because I broke the precepts I was born as a nāga. Now I wish to keep the precepts again and be freed from this body. Please give me the rite of the eight precepts for one day and night. If you do not grant it, I will turn the king's country into an ocean."' So he sent this message.

"Then the orchard keeper took the fruit and offered it to the king, and he also told the king the whole message the nāga had sent. The king became unhappy. Why was that? At that time no buddha was dwelling in the world, and since the sublime Dharma had vanished, not even a text of the eight precepts for one day and night could be found. Fearing that if he did not accomplish this the nāga would do him harm, he fell to brooding and sat there unhappy.

"Then the king said to his chief minister, 'A nāga is asking me for the text of the eight precepts. I will not allow you to fail to find it.'

"The minister said, 'Since there is no Dharma in the world, where am I to find it?'

"The king said to the minister, 'If you do not find it, you will be killed.'

"Most unhappy, the minister went off and reached his home. That minister had an aged and decrepit father.

"His father looked at him and said, 'Before, my son, you used to talk with a smile and a bright face. Why today, unlike before, do you speak with a dark and unsmiling face?' The minister told his father in detail what had happened.

"His father said, 'In my house there is a pillar from which light always shines. Look and see what is inside it.' So he took the pillar out and split it open, and from inside it there came forth the Sūtra of the Twelve Links of Dependent Origination and the text of the eight precepts for one day and night. The minister offered them to the king.

"When the king received them he was delighted, overjoyed and thrilled. He placed the text of the eight precepts in a golden casket and sent it to the nāga. When the nāga received it he was overjoyed, and he presented the king with many precious things as well. He kept the eight precepts for one day and night and urged others to keep them too. Then, when he died, he was born as a god. The king likewise kept the precepts, and when he died he was born as a god in the same abode.

"Those two came before me last night in the first watch of the night. When I taught them the Dharma as suited them, they attained the fruit of stream-entry and became completely free of the three lower realms. They will enjoy the pleasures of gods and humans and in the end will attain even nirvāṇa."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, all the large assembly rejoiced and were delighted.

This concludes the third chapter, The Wandering Ascetic Keeps the Precepts.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada.

At that time in that land a son was born to a certain householder. The boy was well formed and lovely to look at, and not long after he was born he could speak. The boy asked his father and mother, "Is the Blessed One alive at present?"

"He is alive at present," they said.

"Then are the venerable Śāriputra, Ānanda and the others alive?" he asked.

"They too are alive," they said.

The boy's father and mother thought, "This child, who could speak as soon as he was born, is not human," and in amazement and wonder they asked the Blessed One about it. The Blessed One said, "This son of yours is endowed with excellent marks. Have no doubts." The boy's father and mother were delighted, overjoyed and thrilled, and went back home.

Then the boy said to his father and mother, "Please invite the Buddha and the Saṅgha of monks."

His father and mother said, "If we invite the Buddha and the Saṅgha, provisions will have to be prepared, and they are not to be had at a moment's notice."

The son said, "Sweep and sprinkle the inside of the house and spread out seats. Arrange three high seats and spread them out as well. Food with a hundred flavors will come of itself. My parents from my former life are now living in the land of Vārāṇasī; summon them too."

His father and mother did as their son had said: they sent a man mounted on an elephant, who summoned his former parents and brought them. "Of the three high seats that have been set out," he said, "invite the Buddha to sit on one; let my parents from my former life sit on one; and let the parents who gave birth to my present body sit on one."

Then the Buddha came with his retinue of monks and sat on the seats that had been spread out, each in order. Everyone was filled and satisfied with fine food. Then the Blessed One taught the Dharma, and the boy's two sets of parents and everyone in the household, great and small, heard the Dharma and rejoiced greatly, and all of them attained the first fruit. Later, when the boy had grown up, he went forth, and by making diligent effort he attained arhatship.

Then Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "What good did this monk do in the past, that he was born into a great and high family, could speak from infancy, and after going forth swiftly attained the higher knowledges?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "In a former life this man was the son of a householder in the land of Vārāṇasī. When the householder died, he fell on hard times and became destitute. Though he had met with the Buddha, the Blessed One, dwelling in the world, he had no wealth with which to make offerings. Unhappy at this, he agreed to work for hire for a man of great and high family, and when a year had passed he had earned about a thousand gold coins.

"Then the man of great and high family asked him, 'Will you not take a wife?'

"'I do not want one,' he said.

"He asked again, 'Then what will you do with this gold?'

"He said, 'I must offer a meal of alms to the Buddha and the noble Saṅgha.'

"The man of great and high family said, 'If you want to invite the Buddha and the noble Saṅgha, I will put in gold and lay out the food and other provisions, so invite them to my house.'

"The poor man said, 'I will do so,' and food was laid out and he invited the Buddha and the noble Saṅgha. Through that cause and that condition, when he died he was born into the household of this householder, and now too, having invited the Buddha and heard the Dharma, he has attained awakening."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "The poor man of former times is this monk, the householder's son."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, all the large assembly rejoiced and were delighted.

This concludes the fourth chapter, Selling Himself to Make Offerings.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada.

At that time, in that same land, about five hundred merchants who were going to the ocean to fetch jewels discussed among themselves: "Let us take as our captain someone wise and knowledgeable among men." Having agreed on this, they took as their leader a layman who kept the five precepts, and set out on the ocean.

When they reached the middle of the ocean, the god of the ocean transformed himself into a rākṣasa, with a form most hideous, blue-black, with fangs jutting upward and flames blazing on his head. He came up to them and said, "Give me this ship!" And he asked the merchants this:

"Is there anything in the world more terrifying than I am?"

The layman said, "There is something far more terrifying than you."

The god of the ocean said, "Who is like that?"

The layman said, "In the world there are fools, full of delusion, who heedlessly commit nonvirtuous actions: killing, taking what is not given, sexual misconduct, lying, idle talk, slander, harsh speech, attachment, great hatred, and wrong views. When they die they are born in hell, and there they experience all kinds of suffering. The guardians of hell lead them away: some are hacked and chopped with weapons; some are pulled this way and that by chariots and torn limb from limb; some are pounded with pestles; some are bound with ropes; some are made to climb mountains of swords; some are boiled in chariots of fire, in boiling copper cauldrons, in molten metal and in vile filth. They experience such dreadful sufferings of all kinds for hundreds of thousands of years. That is far more terrifying than you."

As soon as the god of the ocean heard this, he vanished.

As they went on again over the ocean, the god of the ocean transformed himself into a man extremely emaciated and wretched, nothing but sinews and bones held together, and saying, "Give me this ship!" he asked this: "Is there anyone in the world more emaciated and wretched than I am?"

The layman said, "There are those far more wretched and emaciated than you."

The god of the ocean said, "Who is like that?"

The layman said, "Those who are foolish and deluded, by nature stingy and miserly, envious out of desire, and who know nothing of giving, are born as hungry ghosts when they die. Their bodies are as big as mountains, but their throats are as narrow as a needle. Their hair is matted, they are black and emaciated, and for hundreds of thousands of years they do not hear even the word 'water.' They are far more emaciated than you."

As soon as he had said this, the god vanished.

Then, as they went on and on, the god of the ocean transformed himself into a man of extremely fine appearance and said, "Give me this ship!" And he asked the merchants, "Is there anyone among the people of the world as handsome and beautiful as I am?"

The layman said, "There are those a hundred times, a thousand times more handsome than you."

The god of the ocean said, "Who is more handsome than I?"

The layman said, "In the world, the wise and intelligent who practice the ten virtues to perfection, whose actions of body, speech and mind are completely pure, who have deep faith in the Three Jewels, and who are minded to make offerings with whatever they have, are born as gods in the higher realms when they die. Their appearance is lovely, most attractive and most beautiful. They surpass you a hundredfold, a thousandfold. If you were compared with them, it would be like comparing a blind monkey with the goddess of good fortune."

Then the god of the ocean scooped up a double handful of water and asked the layman, "Is there more water in this double handful, or is there more water in the ocean?"

The layman said, "There is more water in the double handful; the water of the ocean is not much."

The god of the ocean said, "Are these words you have spoken true?"

The layman said, "These words are true, not false."

"How is it evident that they are true?"

He said, "The water of the ocean is plentiful, but without doubt there will be a time when it dries up. When the eon draws near its destruction by fire, two suns rise and dry up all the springs and ponds. When three suns rise, all the streams dry up. When four suns rise, all the rivers dry up. When five suns rise, even the great ocean shrinks from what it was before. When six suns rise, it shrinks by two-thirds. When seven suns rise, even the great ocean dries up, and even Mount Meru burns and blazes all the way down to its vajra foundation. But if someone with a mind of deep faith offers even a double handful of water to the Buddha, or presents it to the Saṅgha, or presents it to his father and mother, or gives it to the poor and destitute, or gives it to wild animals, that merit will not be exhausted even in many eons. Therefore you should know that the water of the ocean is little and the water in a double handful is much."

Then the god of the ocean was overjoyed. He presented the layman with many precious things, and he also sent supreme jewels to the Buddha and the Saṅgha. The layman and the merchants, satisfied with jewels, returned safely to their own land.

Then that layman and the five hundred or so merchants came to where the Buddha was and bowed their heads at the Blessed One's feet. Each of them brought jewels, and they offered to the Buddha and the Saṅgha the gifts the god of the ocean had sent as well. They knelt with their knees on the ground, joined their palms, and said to the Blessed One, "We too wish to go forth in the Teacher's doctrine."

When the Blessed One said, "Laymen, welcome!" their hair and beards fell away of themselves, and they became monks wearing Dharma robes. The Blessed One taught them the Dharma as suited them, and later, all their desires exhausted, they became arhats.

That large assembly rejoiced at what the Blessed One had said and were delighted.

This concludes the fifth chapter, The Questions of the God of the Ocean.

Fascicle Two.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying in the Bamboo Grove at Kalandakanivāpa.

At that time in that land there was a very wealthy great minister who had no son. At that time on the bank of the river Gaṅgā there was a shrine of a god called Maṇibhadra, and everyone bowed down and made offerings to it. That great minister also went to the shrine and made this request: "I have no son. I have heard, god, that you possess immeasurable qualities. If you, the protector of all beings, are able to fulfill the wishes of others, then please protect me too. If you grant me a son, I will cover your image, god, with gold and silver, and anoint the shrine where you dwell with incense and perfumes.

But if there is no clear sign, I will tear down your shrine, god, and smear your body with filth and excrement."

When the god heard this, he thought: "This minister is wealthy, powerful and mighty, so a being of mere lowly kind will not become his son. My power is small, and I cannot fulfill his wish. If I do not do as he wishes, he will certainly insult me in some ugly way." Having thought this, the god who guarded the shrine appealed to Maṇibhadra. Maṇibhadra too lacked such power, so he went to where Vaiśravaṇa was and appealed to Vaiśravaṇa. Vaiśravaṇa also thought, "By my power alone I cannot bring it about that he has a son." So Vaiśravaṇa went to where Śakra was and made this appeal: "My minister, who is called Maṇibhadra, has said this to me: 'A minister in Rājagṛha is asking me for a son. He has vowed that if I do as he wishes, he will make offerings and pay honor, but if I do not do as he wishes, he will tear down the shrine and do ugly things. If he does without fail as he has vowed, then, Śakra, lord of the gods, please bring it about that he has a son.'"

Śakra said, "This is extremely difficult indeed, yet I now see a way." At that time there was a son of the gods who had been parted from the five qualities and was close to the end of his life. Śakra said to him, "You are certain to die. When you die, be born as the son of that minister."

The son of the gods replied, "I long to go forth and practice the sacred Dharma. If I am born into a great and high family, it will be hard to go forth. If I become one of an ordinary family, it will be easy."

Śakra spoke again: "Divine one, be born there. If you long to go forth, I too will help you."

That son of the gods passed away from there and was born into that minister's family, in the womb of his wife. Later, when the time of birth came, a son was born who was well formed and beautiful to behold. They showed him to a soothsayer, and the soothsayer, having looked at him, asked, "Where did you first obtain this boy?" They said, "We asked for him from the god of the river Gaṅgā." And so they gave the boy the name Gaṅgādatta.

Later, when he had grown up, he said to his father and mother, "I ask to go forth."

His parents told him, "We are wealthy and powerful like this and possess great riches. You are our only son. You must uphold our family line and support your parents, so we do not permit it."

Because what he had hoped for did not come about, the boy was deeply unhappy, and he thought, "If I do away with this body and am born into an ordinary family, and then ask to go forth, it may be easier than this." Having thought this, he went out, and leapt from a high cliff. But though he leapt from the cliff and fell to the ground, he was neither hurt nor injured. Then he went to the bank of a great river and leapt into the water, but the water tossed him about and he came back out again, unharmed. He ate deadly poison, but the poison could not overcome him and did him no harm.

Then he thought again: "If I do something that breaks the king's law, the king may well have me killed." Just then the king's wives and palace women came out of the palace and went to bathe in a pool in the pleasure garden. They took off their clothes and ornaments and laid them on the trees. Gaṅgādatta, passing by, saw the clothes and ornaments laid on the trees in this way. He went into the pleasure garden, gathered the queens' clothes and ornaments into his arms, and came out at the gate. The gatekeeper seized him and delivered him to King Ajātaśatru.

When the king heard what had happened, he was furious. He took up a bow and arrow, and the king himself, with his own hand, shot at the boy. But the arrow he shot did not go to the boy; it turned back and fell in front of the king. This happened three times, and the king was frightened. He threw down the bow and said to the boy, "Are you a god, a nāga, a hungry ghost, a yakṣa, or what are you?"

The boy replied, "I have one request. If you grant it, I will tell you properly."

The king said, "I will do so."

The boy said, "I am neither a god nor a nāga. I am the son of a minister of Rājagṛha. I longed to go forth, but my father and mother would not permit it. So, thinking that I would kill myself so that this body of mine might be reborn elsewhere, I leapt from a high cliff, and I leapt into water, and I even ate deadly poison, yet I could not die. Then I thought that if I did something against the king's law I would be put to death. But even when Your Majesty was about to put me to death, I did not die, as you have seen. Now that things have come to this, may Your Majesty take pity on me and allow me to go forth."

The king declared, "I allow you to go forth." He took the boy and came before the Blessed One, and told the Blessed One in detail all that had happened. Then the Blessed One too allowed him to go forth, saying, "Let robes be upon your body," and he became a monk. The Blessed One taught him the Dharma in a way suited to him, and his mind was liberated. He became an arhat, endowed with the three knowledges, the six superknowledges and the eight liberations.

Then King Ajātaśatru said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, what root of virtue did this young boy accomplish in the past, such that though he leapt from a cliff he did not die, though he leapt into the water he came to no harm, though he ate deadly poison he did not die, and though an arrow was shot at him he was not wounded? And having met the Blessed One, he has been freed from the extreme of saṃsāra."

The Blessed One spoke to the king as follows: "Long ago in the past, countless eons ago, in the land called Vārāṇasī, there was a king called Brahmadatta. That king went out with his queens and the women of the palace to amuse himself in the pleasure garden. In that pleasure garden the palace women sang, and outside the wall a man took up the refrain in a loud voice. The king heard this, and when he heard it he grew angry and commanded, 'Seize that man and kill him!'

At that time a minister was coming in from outside, and he saw the man who had been taken captive. He asked the attendants, "What wrong has this man done, that he has been seized?" They told him in detail why he had been seized, and the minister said to them, "Wait for now, and do not kill him. I will speak to the king." He went into the palace and said this to the king: "The man who is being led away to be executed has done nothing that deserves death. Singing, or taking up a refrain, is not something seen in visible form. It is a rash and improper act, but it is not doing wrong to the queens. It would be right for Your Majesty to have compassion for his life and let him go." The king declared, "Let him go without killing him."

That man then entered the minister's service. Later, when many years had passed, he thought: "The wound dealt by desire is sharper than a sword. That I came to such misery was also because of desire." Having thought this, he asked the minister, "Will you allow me to go forth and to meditate on the path of liberation?" The minister told him, "If you meditate on the path of liberation, I will not stand in your way. But when you have become liberated, come back and meet me again."

Then the man went to a solitary place and brought the ultimate meaning to mind. By applying diligence he became a pratyekabuddha. Then he went back to the city and came to where the great minister was. The great minister was overjoyed, and offered him alms and the four kinds of requisites for living without interruption. Then the pratyekabuddha rose into the sky above, and displayed the eighteen kinds of miracles: water streaming from his body, fire blazing forth, great rays of light shining out, and so on. When the great minister saw such things, boundless delight arose in him, and he made this vow: "This man was able to live through my kindness. Wherever I am born, may I be powerful and wealthy and long lived, a hundred times, a thousand times more exalted than others, and may my wisdom and merit also become equal to his." So he made his aspiration.

The Blessed One said to the king: "The minister who at that time, on that occasion, saved a man's life and enabled him to attain the fruit was this one. By that cause and that condition, wherever he was born, his life was never cut short, and now, having met me, he has attained the supreme fruit."

The whole great assembly rejoiced at what the Blessed One had spoken and was delighted.

This is the sixth chapter, the chapter on the son of the gods called Gaṅgādatta.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time the chief queen of King Prasenajit, who was called Mallikā, gave birth to a daughter, and they gave the girl the name Vajrā. Her face was extremely ugly; the skin of her body was very rough, hard like a tiger's hide, and her hair too was coarse and bristling like a horse's mane. The king was displeased with the girl because she was ugly, and had her raised within the palace in a place where no one could see her.

Though the girl was as ugly as this, she had been born to the chief queen, and so, ugly as she was, she was raised where no one could see her. Later, when she had grown up and was of an age to be given in marriage, the king, deeply displeased, said this to a minister: "Find a man who was formerly of high lineage, belonging to the vaiśya class, but who has now fallen on hard times and has no wealth or possessions, and bring him to me." So that minister found a poor and destitute son of the vaiśya class, of high lineage but fallen on hard times, and presented him to the king.

The king took him in secret to a solitary place where there was no one else, and said, "I have a daughter whose face is ugly. She is of an age to be given in marriage, but no match has been found for her. You are of a great and high lineage, though you are poor and have nothing at all. I will give her to you as your wife; take her."

Then the young man set his knee on the ground and replied, "I will do as the king commands. Even if the great king were to give me a bitch, I would accept her. How much more so when you give me a princess of the royal line! As you command, I will accept her."

So the king gave his daughter as wife to the poor man. He had a house and so forth made for her, built it with seven successive gates, and gave this order to the man: "Whenever you go anywhere, close the gates yourself and take the key with you. Keep the gates firmly shut, so that no other person outside will ever see this very ugly daughter of mine." Having given this order, he gave him wealth, possessions and all the requisites he could need, as much as he wanted, and made him a minister.

Since he now possessed precious jewels and many possessions, at a later time he took part in feasting with others of great and high families. At the feasts husbands, wives, sons and daughters all gathered to enjoy themselves. All the others came accompanied by their wives, but only that minister's wife did not come, so his companions grew suspicious. They doubted, thinking, "Either this man's wife is exceedingly beautiful and fair of form, or else she is so very ugly that she must not be seen by others, and that is why he has not brought her." So they conferred about looking at his wife, and, in agreement, secretly made a plan: "Let us make this man drunk with liquor, and once he has lost his wits, steal the key, and send about five men to look at his wife."

At that time the man's wife thought, "What evil did I commit in the past, that my husband hates me, and I have been put in such a dark hole as this, where I cannot see even the sun and moon, nor see any other person?" She was deeply unhappy, and then she thought this: "The Buddha, the Blessed One, dwells in the world, and it is said that he acts as protector and refuge for those sentient beings tormented by suffering, and liberates them. I bow down to the Blessed One from afar. If only, out of compassion, he would come before me in person, and show me his face, even for a little while, how good that would be!"

Knowing that the girl's intention was utterly pure and that she was full of devotion, the Blessed One went to her house. Before the girl he manifested his head, marked by its sapphire blue hair, the fruit of his former deeds, and let her see it. When the girl saw the Blessed One's sapphire blue hair, she was all the more delighted and overjoyed, and her intention became utterly pure. Because her intention was so pure, the girl's own hair too became very soft and the color of sapphire. Then the Blessed One showed his face before her in person, and when the girl saw it, the instant she saw it she was so delighted and overjoyed that her face too became fair and beautiful, and its ugliness and coarseness vanished. The Blessed One then showed his body down to the waist, and made it shine with a radiant light like the color of gold. When the girl saw this, she was all the more delighted in the Buddha, and was filled with joy and gladness. Because she was so delighted and overjoyed, all her ugliness vanished, and she became so exceedingly beautiful and lovely that, like a daughter of the gods, she had no equal in the world.

Out of compassion for the girl, the Blessed One showed his complete body. The girl gazed at it without blinking, and was even more delighted and overjoyed than before. All the features of her body became surpassingly fair, without equal in the world, and not the slightest trace of the habitual tendencies of her ugliness remained. The Blessed One taught her the Dharma in a way suited to her. All her evil was exhausted, and she attained the fruit of stream-entry. Then the Blessed One departed.

Right after that, the five men opened the gates and came in, and they saw that the man's wife was exceedingly beautiful and fair beyond compare. They said to one another, "This man's wife is so very fair and beautiful, and that is why he does not bring her." Having seen his wife, they shut the gates again and came back to where the others were, and, without the man noticing, tied the key back on his body.

When the man had sobered up from his drunkenness, he returned home with the others. He saw that his wife was exceedingly fair and beautiful in form, a woman very rare among human beings, and he was overjoyed. He said to his wife, "Who are you?" and his wife said, "I am your wife." Her husband said, "You were very ugly. How is it that you have become so fair and beautiful?" His wife said, "Because I saw the Buddha, my body has become like this," and she told him the whole story in detail. His wife said further, "In my heart I now wish to meet my father, the king. Please tell the king that I say this."

Her husband told the king just that, and the king declared, "Do not say such things, husband. Go quickly, shut her in, and do not let her come out."

The husband replied, "Through the blessing of the Blessed One's compassion, the king's daughter has become as fair and beautiful as a goddess."

The king said to the husband, "If it has truly come about like that, bring her here quickly." Having said this, he had a chariot adorned and welcomed his daughter into the palace. When the king saw that his daughter had become so fair and beautiful, he was overjoyed, and the king, the queen, the husband and the daughter together went to where the Blessed One was. They bowed down to the Buddha and sat down to one side.

Then King Prasenajit set his right knee on the ground and said this to the Blessed One: "What merit did this girl make in the past, that she was born into a great and high family with many possessions and riches? And what evil deed did she commit, that her form was so very ugly and her skin and hair were like an animal's?"

The Blessed One declared: "Great king, your daughter's being fair and beautiful, and her being ugly, came about from the merit and the evil deeds she did in former lives. Long ago in the past, in the land called Vārāṇasī, there lived a householder of that land who was very rich, possessing measureless wealth and riches. That householder, who had no anger in him, used to make offerings and present all that was in his house to a certain pratyekabuddha. That pratyekabuddha's body was very rough, poor, ugly and gaunt. The householder had a small daughter, and day after day, whenever she saw the pratyekabuddha coming, the girl gave rise to a reproachful attitude. Scorning and reviling him, she would say reproachfully, 'How ugly he is, with his ugly face and the rough skin of his body!'

"The pratyekabuddha always came to that house and accepted offerings. When a long time had passed, he resolved to pass into nirvāṇa. For the sake of his patron he rose into the sky above, and displayed many kinds of miracles: fire blazed from his body and water streamed from it; he vanished in the east and appeared in the west, vanished in the west and appeared in the east, vanished in the south and appeared in the north, vanished in the north and appeared in the south; he lay down and sat in empty space, and so on. He let all the people of the house see these miracles, then came down from the sky and went into the house. The householder was all the more delighted and overjoyed. The girl too was filled with deep remorse, and she said this to the pratyekabuddha: 'I confess the reproachful wrongs I committed again and again before, so that they may not become actual evil for me.' And the pratyekabuddha accepted her confession."

The Blessed One declared: "Great king, the girl at that time, on that occasion, is this daughter of the king now. Because at that time, with a nonvirtuous mind, she committed the verbal wrong of reviling the noble pratyekabuddha, from then on she always experienced an ugly body. But later, because she saw the miracles and felt remorse, she afterwards came to have a very fair form and to be endowed with intelligence. Because she made offerings to the pratyekabuddha, wherever she was born she was of a great family with wealth and riches, and in the end she has also attained liberation.

Therefore, great king, all who belong to the family of sentient beings should guard with the utmost care against faults of body and speech, and should never scorn or revile others."

At that time King Prasenajit and his retinue, and the whole great assembly, having heard the Blessed One teach the fruits of karmic maturation, came to possess faith with utterly pure intention. Because their intention was so utterly pure, some attained the fruits from the first fruit up to the fourth. Some generated the mind set on unsurpassed awakening.
