Some came to abide on the ground of non-returning. That whole assembly rejoiced in what the Blessed One had spoken and was delighted.

This is the seventh chapter, the chapter on Vajrā, daughter of King Prasenajit.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada, together with a saṅgha of twelve hundred and fifty monks.

At that time, in that city, the wife of a certain householder gave birth to a son, and they named him Golden Treasure. He was well formed, fair and exceedingly beautiful, without equal in the world. Because of his former deeds, the boy was born with both hands clenched into fists. The boy's father and mother were greatly amazed, and thinking it auspicious, they opened the boy's two fists and looked. In each of the boy's palms there was a gold coin. The parents were overjoyed and took both gold coins, but in the place where they had been taken, there were more coins again, and they took them again and again. Though the parents went on taking gold coins from his palms in this way until a whole storehouse was full, the coins in the boy's hands never ran out.

Later, when the boy had grown up, he said to his parents, "I am going forth," and his parents said, "We allow it, as you wish." Then the boy Golden Treasure went to where the Blessed One was, bowed his head at his feet, and said this to the Buddha: "Blessed One, out of compassion for me, will the Teacher allow me to go forth?" The Blessed One declared, "Boy Golden Treasure, I allow you to go forth." Having been permitted by the Buddha in this way, the boy Golden Treasure shaved his hair and beard, put on the robes, and became a novice.

Later, when he had reached the full number of years and was ready for full ordination, he made his request to the Saṅgha with faith, and asked for full ordination. He then bowed down in turn to the fully ordained Saṅgha, and as he bowed down to each one in order, wherever his two hands touched the ground there was a gold coin. In the same way, at every bow he made in turn, there appeared a gold coin. Having received full ordination, he cultivated diligence and became an arhat.

Ānanda said to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, what merit did this monk Golden Treasure make in the past, that from the time he was first born until now he has held gold coins? Please, Blessed One, explain it."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Listen always, hold it well in mind, and I shall explain it to you." And Ānanda replied, "I wish to hear it just so."

The Blessed One declared: "Long ago in the past, ninety-one eons ago, a buddha called Kanakamuni came into the world, and the benefit he brought to sentient beings through the sacred Dharma was beyond telling and beyond measure. While that buddha was traveling through the land together with the Saṅgha, householders of great and high families, possessed of riches and wealth, set out food of many kinds and made offerings to the Buddha and to his close disciples, the noble Saṅgha. At that time there was a poor man who had no possessions at all, who always gathered firewood in the mountains and sold it. Later, as the price of firewood he had sold, he obtained two coins. That poor man saw the Buddha and the Saṅgha, who had been invited to the king's palace, as they were going, and was overjoyed. He offered the two coins he had to the Buddha and the Saṅgha, and the Blessed One, out of compassion, accepted the two coins."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda: "Because at that time that poor man offered two coins to the Buddha and the Saṅgha, for ninety-one eons he always held gold coins in his hands, and his wealth, jewels and the means of living became inexhaustible. The poor man at that time, on that occasion, is this monk Golden Treasure. Even if this man had not attained the fruit of an arhat, the future fruits of that maturation would still be beyond measure. Therefore, Ānanda, all sentient beings should exert themselves in the deed of giving."

At that time Ānanda and the great assembly, having heard what the Buddha had spoken, came to have faith and devotion. Some attained the fruit of stream-entry; some attained once-returning, non-returning and arhatship. Some generated the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Some came to abide on the ground of non-returning. The whole assembly was delighted with what the Buddha had spoken.

This is the eighth chapter, the chapter on Golden Treasure.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada, together with a saṅgha of twelve hundred and fifty monks.

At that time, in that land, the wife of a householder of a great and high family gave birth to a son who was well formed, lovely to behold, fair and beautiful. As soon as he was born, divine flowers fell like rain from the sky and filled the whole house, and so the boy was named Divine Flower. Later, when the boy had grown up, he went to where the Buddha was. When he arrived and saw the Buddha's complexion, and his major marks and excellent minor marks beyond compare, great joy arose in him, and he thought:

"Born into this world, I have met the Lord of the Noble Ones, so I will invite the Buddha and the saṅgha of noble monks." Having thought this, he said this to the Buddha: "Blessed One, I will prepare some poor alms at my house tomorrow at midday. Will you, together with the noble Saṅgha, come to receive alms?" Knowing that his intention was pure, the Buddha consented to come.

Then Divine Flower went back home, and the next day the Buddha and the Saṅgha came to his house. Divine Flower magically produced many jeweled seats inside a divine mansion, adorned with ornaments, and he invited the Buddha and the Saṅgha to be seated on those seats in order. Then Divine Flower thought of the many kinds of food that were needed, and through his merit they appeared just as he had thought, and he served alms to the Buddha and the Saṅgha. When the begging bowls had been taken away, the Buddha taught Divine Flower much Dharma at length, and all the people of Divine Flower's household attained the fruit of stream-entry.

Then Divine Flower asked his father and mother to let him go forth and go into the Buddha's presence as a disciple, and his parents allowed it. The boy went to where the Buddha was, and when he arrived he bowed his head at the Buddha's feet and asked to go forth in the Buddha's teaching. The Blessed One declared, "Welcome, monk!" and his hair and beard fell away of themselves, and he was clad in saffron robes. Following the Buddha's instructions, he practiced earnestly and became an arhat.

When Ānanda saw that this had come about, he went to where the Buddha was, and when he arrived he set his knee on the ground and said this to the Blessed One: "What merit did this monk Divine Flower make in the past, that divine flowers fall of themselves for him, and jeweled seats and many kinds of food appear by magic just as he wishes? Please, Blessed One, explain it."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda: "If you wish to hear, listen well. Long ago in the past, a buddha called Kanakamuni came into the world and brought vast benefit to sentient beings. At that time, while a great saṅgha was traveling through the villages, householders of great and high families made offerings to them. At that time there was a poor man who had no possessions at all, and a mind of great joy toward the Saṅgha arose in him. Thinking, 'I have nothing at all to offer,' he gathered many kinds of flowers from the meadows and grassy marshes and scattered them over the Saṅgha, and with pure intention he swept the place and went away."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda: "The poor man at that time, on that occasion, who scattered flowers, is this monk Divine Flower. Because long ago in the past, with the faith of pure intention, he gathered flowers, scattered them over the Saṅgha and made an aspiration, for ninety-one eons, wherever he was born, he was well formed and fair and lovely to behold, and food, drink, seats and so forth came to him and were his just as he wished, exactly as he thought of them. Through that merit he has now attained even the fruit. Therefore, Ānanda, let no sentient being despise a small gift, thinking it has no merit. Just as for Divine Flower, everything will come and be accomplished."

Then Ānanda and the great assembly rejoiced in what the Blessed One had spoken and were delighted.

This is the ninth chapter, the chapter on Divine Flower.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time the wife of a certain householder gave birth to a son. When the boy was born, the seven kinds of precious substances fell like rain from the sky and filled his whole house. Then they called a soothsayer and had him examine the boy's marks, and he told the householder, "The boy's marks are excellent." The householder was overjoyed and asked the soothsayer to name the boy. The soothsayer asked, "What omens occurred when this boy was born?" The householder said, "When this boy was born, the seven kinds of precious substances fell like rain from the sky and filled the whole house." So the soothsayer named him for his merit, and gave the boy the name Divine Jewel.

Later, when he had grown up, he was very learned and of wide knowledge, and his skill in the arts was without equal. Having faith in going forth, he took leave of his father and mother with a parting bow and went to where the Buddha was. When he arrived he bowed his head at the Buddha's feet and said this to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, I wish to go forth." The Blessed One granted it with the words "Welcome, monk!" and his hair and beard fell away of themselves, and robes came to be upon his body. When the Blessed One taught him the Dharma, he became an arhat.

Ānanda said to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, what merit did this monk Divine Jewel make in the past, that when he was first born jewels fell like rain from the sky, and clothing and food come to him spontaneously, so that he is never troubled by anything?"

The Buddha said to Ānanda: "Long ago in the past, a buddha called Kanakamuni came into the world, and the benefit he brought to sentient beings in the world cannot be fully told. At that time, while a great saṅgha was traveling through the villages, many householders living in the city invited the Saṅgha and made many kinds of offerings. At that time a poor man, out of faith, gave rise to a mind of rejoicing. But since he had no possessions or offerings at all, he scattered a handful of jewels made of white pebbles over the Saṅgha and made a great aspiration."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda: "The poor man at that time, on that occasion, who scattered a handful of jewels over the Saṅgha, is this monk Divine Jewel.

In a past time, merely by scattering a handful of jewels made of white pebbles over the Saṅgha with the faith of a pure intention, his merit was immeasurable for ninety-one eons: he had many precious jewels, clothing and food came to him spontaneously, and he was never in want of any enjoyment. Through nothing more than that faith of pure intention at that time, he met me when I came into the world, and he has attained the fruit, up to the very last."

Then the many in the assembly, having heard what the Buddha said, gave rise to faith of pure intention. Some attained the fruits, from the first fruit up to the fourth, and some generated the resolve to abide on the irreversible ground. All of that great assembly rejoiced at what the Blessed One had said, and were delighted.

This is the chapter of Divine Jewel, the tenth.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Rājagṛha, in the Bamboo Grove, at Kalandakanivāpa. At that time, not long after the Blessed One had become fully and perfectly awakened, the five monks, Kauṇḍinya and the others, were the first to go forth. After them a thousand monks, the Kāśyapa brothers, Nadīkāśyapa and the others, went forth. Then others besides them were also made to go forth, and those abiding in the Dharma became many. The people of Rājagṛha felt boundless joy, and they praised and extolled him, saying, "The coming of the Tathāgata into the world is wondrous and marvelous; he works the benefit of a great many ordinary people."

They also said this: "Those who first went forth, Kauṇḍinya and the followers of Nadīkāśyapa and the rest: what deeds did they share with the Tathāgata in the past, that they were the first of all to taste the nectar of the Dharma?"

When the monks heard them say this, they reported it in full to the Blessed One just as it had been said, and the Blessed One spoke to them as follows: "Long ago, in the past, I made a great vow for their sake, pledging, 'When I become fully and perfectly awakened, may I work for their benefit first of all.'"

Having heard this, the monks requested, "Out of your compassion, please explain in full how the Blessed One made that vow in the past."

The Blessed One said to the monks, "Listen closely and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you.

"Long ago, in the past, beyond immeasurable, countless, inestimable, inconceivable and inexpressible eons, there was a king called Kali in the land called Vārāṇasī. At that time, in that land, a sage called Patient One lived in a mountain dwelling with a following of five hundred disciples, and he cultivated patience.

"At that time the king, together with his queens, the women of his palace and his ministers, went to the mountain for pleasure. Being very weary, he fell asleep, and the palace women went on ahead, wandering about and looking at the sights and the flowers. They saw the sage Patient One sitting with his body upright, wholly absorbed in meditation. Faith in him arose in them, and they scattered all kinds of flowers over him. The queens then sat before him and listened to the Dharma.

"When the king awoke from his sleep, the queens were nowhere to be seen. He searched for them with four ministers, and when he saw the queens sitting before the sage, he said to the sage, 'Have you attained the four absorptions of the infinite sense sources?'

"He answered, 'I have not attained them.'

"The king said, 'Then have you attained the four immeasurables?'

"'I have not.'

"The king said, 'Then have you attained the four meditative concentrations?'

"He answered, 'Those too I have not attained.' At this the king grew angry. 'If you have not attained those qualities, you are just a base, common man. How can anyone trust you, sitting with women in a secluded and sheltered place? Who are you, always living here? What work do you do?'

"The sage said to the king, 'Here I cultivate patience.'

"Then the king drew his sword. 'If you say you cultivate patience, let me test whether you are patient or not!' He cut off both of the sage's hands and asked, 'Who are you?'

"He answered, 'I am the Patient One.'

"Then the king cut off both his feet, and still he said, 'I am the Patient One.'

"At that moment the place shook in six ways, and his five hundred or so disciples flew through the sky and arrived there. They said to their master, 'Experiencing such suffering, has your mind of patience not failed?'

"The sage said, 'My mind has not changed; it has not failed.'

"The king, alarmed and frightened, said to the sage, 'How could anyone believe that you are patient?'

"The sage said, 'The words I have spoken are not false. If they are true, then by that truth may the blood turn to milk, and may the severed limbs be restored and become just as they were before.' No sooner had he said this than the blood turned to milk, the limbs were restored, and they became just as they were before.

"When the king saw how patient he was, he was even more terrified, and he pleaded, 'Why did I harm the great sage? Out of compassion for me, please allow me to make amends.'

"The sage said, 'For the sake of women you took up a weapon and cut off my limbs. But my patience is like the earth, so later, when I become fully awakened, may I cut through your three poisons with the sword of wisdom.'

"At that time the nāgas and gods of that mountain, seeing that the king had harmed and injured the sage, each grew angry. They gathered clouds and brought down thunder, hail and thunderbolts, and were about to kill the king and his retinue. But the sage commanded them, 'Do not harm or injure him like this for my sake.'

"The king then made amends, and from then on he constantly invited the sage to the palace and made offerings to him.

"At that time about a thousand wandering ascetics who followed another teacher saw the king's faith and devotion toward the sage Patient One, and they became jealous. From a place where they could not be seen, they scattered dust, filth and defiling impurities over the sage.

"The thousand wandering ascetics who at that time scattered dirt over me are now the thousand monks, Nadīkāśyapa and the others. At that time I made a vow, pledging, 'By this unbroken cultivation of patience of mine, when I become fully awakened, may I wash away your dust and stains with the water of nectar, and may the filth of desire be completely purified.'

"Monks, if you wish to know who the sage Patient One was at that time, do not think it was anyone else: he is now I myself. King Kali and the four ministers are the group of five monks, Kauṇḍinya and the others. Because, while cultivating patience at that time, I pledged, 'I shall work for their benefit first,' after becoming fully awakened I first freed them completely from suffering."

The monks rejoiced at what the Buddha had said and were delighted.

This is the chapter on patience, the eleventh.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time Ānanda, having done his round for alms, sat in meditation in a grove, and this thought occurred to him: "The coming of the Buddha, the Blessed One, into the world is a great wonder, and he has established many sentient beings in perfect happiness. What roots of virtue did the group of five monks, Kauṇḍinya and the others, accumulate, that they entered as soon as the door of the Dharma was opened, that they were the first to hear as soon as the drum of the Dharma was beaten, and that they were the first to be sated with the nectar of the Dharma?"

When this thought had occurred to him, he rose from his seat and went to where the Buddha was. Arriving there, he put it to the Blessed One just as he had thought it.

The Blessed One said, "In the past, too, I gave the monks Kauṇḍinya and the others their fill of flesh and blood when they were hungry and thirsty, and made them happy. That is why, after I became fully and perfectly awakened, I established them first in liberation. It is not without cause and condition."

The venerable Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "Please explain how, in the past, you relieved them from hunger and thirst and made them happy. When this great assembly has heard it, they will understand."

The Blessed One said, "Long ago, in the past, countless eons ago, here in Jambudvīpa there was a king called Maitrībala, who ruled over some eighty-four thousand minor kings of Jambudvīpa. That king had twenty thousand queens and ten thousand ministers. The king was endowed with loving-kindness and compassion; he practiced the four immeasurables and, being tender toward all beings, he governed by the ten virtues. He was honored by all. In his land there were no enemies, the harvests were rich, and the people enjoyed happiness and well-being.

"There were pretas that spread fevers, who stole and fed on the blood and vitality of human beings. But at that time all the people of the land guarded their body, speech and mind and abided in the ten virtues, so the savage pretas found nothing to eat. All of them went hungry and became powerless.

"At that time five yakṣas came before the king and said this: 'Our food depends on the vitality and blood of human beings; by it we sustain our bodies and our lives. But because the king has commanded it, everyone abides in the ten virtues, and we find neither food nor drink. We are exhausted and close to death. Will the king not have compassion on us too?'

"Out of compassion for them, the king cut five veins in five of the limbs of his body, and had each of the yakṣas hold out a vessel and drink its fill of the blood. Sated with blood, they were overjoyed, pleased and content. When they had had their fill of blood, he urged them, 'Practice the ten virtues as far as you are able.' And he said, 'Just as I have now drawn blood from my body and sated you and made you happy, so later, when I have become fully and perfectly awakened, may I remove your three poisons by the moral discipline, meditative absorption and wisdom of the Dharma body, and may I establish you, who are tormented by desire, in nirvāṇa, which is supreme happiness.'

"Ānanda, King Maitrībala at that time, on that occasion, is now I myself. Those five yakṣas are the five monks, Kauṇḍinya and the others. I made the aspiration that wherever I was born, I would liberate them first. That is why they were liberated as soon as I first taught the Dharma."

The venerable Ānanda and the great assembly rejoiced at what the Buddha had said and praised it highly.

This is the chapter on the generosity of King Maitrībala, the twelfth.

The third section.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Rājagṛha, at Kalandakanivāpa in the Bamboo Grove, together with a great Saṅgha of one thousand two hundred and fifty monks. At that time King Bimbisāra, having attained the first fruit, became even more intent than before on faith and on serving and honoring the Buddha, and he constantly offered the four kinds of requisites to the Blessed One and the Saṅgha of monks. Many people too, in harmony with him, took delight in virtue.

In that land the six teachers, Pūraṇa and the others, had appeared earlier. They taught wrong views falsely and led many beings astray, and those who were seen to have been corrupted trusted in those wrong views, so that the faction of the wicked grew numerous and widespread.

A younger brother of the king also trusted in the six teachers and was attached to wrong views. Taking what is not the path to be the path to liberation, he spent all the wealth he had in making offerings to them. Although the Buddha, the Blessed One, like the rising sun, made everything blaze with the light of wisdom, he was obstructed by wrong views and had no devotion toward him.

Because King Bimbisāra held him especially dear, he skillfully urged him to serve and honor the Buddha, but attached as he was to wrong views, he did not do as he was told. When the king urged him again and again, "Make offerings to the Buddha," he replied, "I have a teacher of my own. I will not serve and honor Gautama."

Yet since it was not proper to disregard the king's command, he arranged a great feast, declaring, "I shall give alms to whoever comes of their own accord without being invited." He set out the articles of offering, spread the seats, and sent an invitation to the six teachers. They arrived at once and sat at the head of the rows.

Since the Buddha and the Saṅgha did not come of their own accord, he went to where the king was and said this: "You have told me again and again to invite the ascetic Gautama. I have arranged the offering, and now that it is midday, why does he not come?"

The king said, "Even if you cannot go to invite him yourself, send a man to invite him, saying, 'It is midday.'"

So the king's younger brother sent someone to invite him, and the Blessed One came together with the noble Saṅgha and sat down in the order of the rows. Through the Blessed One's power, the six teachers, who had been sitting at the head of the rows, found themselves at the very end. Ashamed and embarrassed, the six teachers rose and sat at the head of the rows, but without noticing it they ended up at the tail of the rows again. Three times in this way they went to the top of the rows, and still they found themselves sitting at the bottom. Powerless, they sat at the end of the rows in shame.

When the donor was about to pour water for the elder, the Blessed One said, "First pour it for your own teachers." The donor took the water vessel and poured water for the six teachers, but the mouth of the vessel was blocked and no water would come out. When he then offered it to the Buddha and the others in turn, the water came out, and they washed their hands.

When they had washed their hands, the donor took the alms food and asked for a dedicatory prayer. The Blessed One said this to the donor: "In the first place this was not done for our sake, so ask your own teachers to make the prayer." So the great donor went before the six teachers and asked them to make the prayer, but the six teachers' mouths were shut and they could not speak. They gestured with their hands, signaling, "Ask the Buddha." Then the Blessed One made the prayer in a voice like the voice of Brahmā.

When the prayer had been made, the donor was about to serve the alms food to the elder, but the Blessed One said, "First serve it, beginning with your own teachers." When the servers went to serve the six teachers, the dishes of food rose up into the sky above, and it was impossible to serve them. Then, when they set about serving the Buddha and the others instead, the food came into each one's hands. They served it in order, and when the Buddha and the Saṅgha had finished their meal and washed their mouths and hands, they sat on their seats again, and the donor requested a teaching of the Dharma.

Then too the Blessed One said, "Ask your own six teachers to teach." But the six teachers' mouths were shut, and they gestured with their hands, signaling, "Ask the Buddha." Then the Blessed One spoke in an exceedingly gentle and soft voice, analyzing and teaching the characteristics of phenomena to the great assembly. The great assembly was gladdened, and having heard what the Buddha said, all of them understood and comprehended it. The younger brother of Bimbisāra gained the pure Dharma eye with regard to phenomena. Others in the assembly attained from the first fruit up to the third, went forth, and exhausted the defilements. Some generated the resolve for unsurpassed awakening and came to abide on the irreversible ground. Since each was fulfilled just as they had aspired, each of them knew and understood what is genuine. They had faith and devotion toward the Three Jewels and lost their faith in the six teachers, and no longer served and honored them.

Then the six teachers, utterly agitated and furious, went off to a secluded spot and fell to scheming.

Then Māra, the Evil One, thought: "For a long time I have tried to obstruct the ascetic Gautama, but I have not found an opening. Now I will obstruct him by any means."

With that thought, Māra the Evil One manifested himself in the form of Pūraṇa and went to where Maskarin Gośālīputra, Sañjayin Vairaṭṭīputra, Ajita Keśakambala, Kakuda Kātyāyana and Nirgrantha Jñātiputra were. On arriving, he set about displaying miracles before them: blazing fire, streaming water and flashing lightning. They asked, "Pūraṇa, have you attained so many qualities as these?"

He said, "I have."

Each of them thought to himself, "Apart from me alone, all of these display great magical power and great might."

Then later on the six teachers, Pūraṇa and the others, gathered in a meeting hall, and in the course of their talk they said things like this: "In former times kings, ministers, brahmins, wealthy householders, merchants and caravan leaders attended upon us, honored us and made offerings to us, and we received clothing, food, bedding and seats, medicine for illness, and requisites. But now they attend upon and honor this ascetic, the son of the Śākyas, and we no longer receive clothing, food, bedding and seats, medicine for illness, or requisites. So now we will challenge the ascetic Gautama in miracles of superhuman qualities. If the ascetic Gautama sets out to display one miracle of superhuman qualities, we will display two. If the ascetic Gautama displays two, we will display four. If the ascetic Gautama displays four, we will display eight. If the ascetic Gautama displays eight, we will display sixteen. If the ascetic Gautama displays sixteen, we will display thirty-two. However many miracles of superhuman qualities the ascetic Gautama displays, we will display double or triple that number of miracles of superhuman qualities."

Then the six teachers, Pūraṇa and the others, went to where King Bimbisāra was. Arriving before him, they said to King Bimbisāra, "May you be victorious and live long!" and then they said this:

"Your Majesty, we possess miraculous powers and profess to have knowledge, and the ascetic Gautama too professes that he himself possesses miraculous powers and has knowledge. Since it is right that one who professes knowledge should compete with another who professes knowledge in miracles of superhuman qualities, set us against him!"

At this King Bimbisāra laughed and said, "How foolish you are! The Buddha has great qualities and great magical power, and his miracles are unimpeded. For the likes of you to compete with him is like a firefly competing with the sun, or the water in a cow's hoofprint vying with the ocean, or a jackal competing with a lion, or an anthill competing with Mount Meru in height. The difference in greatness is plain to see. You holders of wrong views are utterly foolish!"

The six teachers spoke again: "The king has not yet seen the evidence that will come forth. If we compete in great miracles, it will become evident; after the contest it will be evident who wins."

The king said, "If you wish to compete, it is up to you to compete, but I fear you will be put to shame. If you do compete in miracles, let us see your miracles too."

The six teachers said, "Seven days from now we will compete in miracles. Have the place where the contest is to be held swept clean." So the six teachers spoke.

After they had gone back, King Bimbisāra went to where the Buddha was. Arriving there, he said this: "The six teachers say they will compete with the Blessed One in miracles. I tried to stop them but could not. May the Blessed One display the power of miracles, turn them back from wrong views, and establish them in virtue. And please let us see the display of miracles too."

The Blessed One said this to King Bimbisāra: "I myself know the time. Have a spacious place cleaned where the contest is to be held."

King Bimbisāra gave orders to many ministers. They swept a spacious and pleasant place, set up a lion throne, raised victory banners and pennants, and adorned it most beautifully. Since the time for the contest of miracles had thus been set, everyone hoped to see it on that day.

At that time the Tathāgata, together with the Saṅgha, left Rājagṛha and went to Vaiśālī. In Vaiśālī the Licchavis and many people welcomed the Tathāgata. Only then did the people of Rājagṛha learn that the Blessed One had gone to Vaiśālī. When they realized it, the six teachers proclaimed: "When we said, 'The ascetic Gautama cannot match us in miracles,' you did not believe us. He has fled from the time set for the contest of miracles and run off to Vaiśālī." The six teachers grew even more arrogant; each of them became elated, and they went off in pursuit of him.

At that time King Bimbisāra too had five hundred carriages made ready, and about a hundred and forty thousand of the retinue of the king and his ministers took provisions for the journey and went after the Blessed One to the land of Vaiśālī.

The six teachers also went to the land of Vaiśālī, and they said this to the Licchavis: "If you permit us to compete with the ascetic Gautama in miracles, we will hold a contest of miracles seven days from now."

Then the Licchavis went to where the Blessed One was and said this: "The great fools, the six teachers, think they have good qualities and say they will compete with the Tathāgata in miracles. May the Blessed One subdue them."

The Blessed One said, "I myself know the time."

Then the Licchavis, with the people of the land, arranged the requisites just as King Bimbisāra had. But on the day before the contest was to take place, the Blessed One, together with the Saṅgha, went to the land of Kauśāmbī. Udrayana, the king of Kauśāmbī, together with his ministers, welcomed the Tathāgata.

When the people of Vaiśālī came the next day to pay homage to the Tathāgata, they heard that he had gone to Kauśāmbī. The six teachers also heard that the Blessed One had gone, and they grew even more arrogant than before and again went to where the Blessed One was. At that time the Licchavis too had five hundred carriages made ready, and with a retinue of seventy thousand, together with Bimbisāra's retinue, they went to Kauśāmbī to watch the Buddha and the six teachers compete in miracles.

When the six teachers reached Kauśāmbī, they said the same things as before to King Udrayana: "This ascetic Gautama could not stand his ground and has fled from us. Let the king set a time for him to compete with us in miracles." Udrayana said this to the Buddha: "The six teachers say they will compete with the Blessed One in miracles. Do you know of this?"

The Blessed One said, "I myself know the time."

Then Udrayana made preparations and arrangements just as King Bimbisāra had, and the Blessed One, together with the Saṅgha of monks, went to the land of Vargi. Śuncida, the king of Vargi, welcomed the Tathāgata together with many people. The people of Kauśāmbī only then heard that the Tathāgata had gone to the land of Vargi, and the six teachers again went after him. At that time King Udrayana, together with a retinue of eighty thousand and with King Bimbisāra and all the others, went to the land of Vargi.

Then the six teachers said to King Śuncida, "Let us compete with the ascetic Gautama in miracles." That king also made the same request, and the Blessed One said, "I myself know the time." That king too made the preparations and arrangements, and the Blessed One, together with the Saṅgha of monks, went to the land of Tikicaśiri. The king of the land of Tikicaśiri, called Indravami, welcomed the Tathāgata together with a large retinue.

At that time King Śuncida, with a retinue of fifty thousand, accompanied by King Bimbisāra and all the others, went to the land of Tikicaśiri. The six teachers too went to that land, and they clamored loudly to King Indravami, "Let us compete with the ascetic Gautama in miracles!" and he could not turn them away. Then Indravami went to where the Blessed One was and made the same request, and the Blessed One said, "I myself know the time."

Then that king too arranged the requisites, but when the time came, the Blessed One, together with the Saṅgha of monks, went to the land of Vārāṇasī. The king of that land, called Brahmadatta, welcomed the Tathāgata together with a large retinue. Only then did the people of Tikicaśiri learn that the Tathāgata had left, and the six teachers went after him. King Indravami, with a retinue of sixty thousand, accompanied by King Bimbisāra and all the other kings, went to that place. The six teachers went there after him too and made the same request to that king; that king made the same request in turn, and the Blessed One said, "I myself know the time."

Then that king too prepared and arranged the requisites, but the Blessed One, together with the Saṅgha of monks, went to the land of Kapilavastu. The Śākya clan of the land of Kapilavastu, each spreading the word to the next, all gathered together and welcomed the Tathāgata. The people of the land of Vārāṇasī only then realized that the Tathāgata had left, and the six teachers went after him. King Brahmadatta, with a retinue of eighty thousand, and all six kings, Bimbisāra and the others, together with their retinues, went after the Blessed One wherever he had gone.

When the six teachers arrived there too, they spread loose talk among the Śākya clan, saying, "When it was arranged that we compete with the ascetic Gautama in miracles, he fled from our greater power." Then the Śākyas made the same request to the Blessed One, and the Blessed One said, "I myself know the time." Then the Śākyas too prepared and arranged the requisites, and the Blessed One, together with the Saṅgha of monks, went to the land of Śrāvastī.

At that time Prasenajit, the king of Śrāvastī, together with his retinue, welcomed the Tathāgata. The Śākyas only then realized that the Tathāgata had left, and the six teachers again went after him in pursuit. The Śākyas, with a retinue of ninety thousand, and the kings, King Bimbisāra and the others, with their retinues, filled the whole region of Śrāvastī.

When the six teachers arrived there too, they said this to King Prasenajit: "A time was set for us to compete with the ascetic Gautama in miracles, but when the time came, he slipped away and fled. Now a great assembly has gathered in the king's land, so, great king, let us compete!"

King Prasenajit laughed and admonished them, saying, "The wondrous qualities of the Blessed One are inconceivable. Why would base people like you, who know nothing at all, compete in miracles with the great king of Dharma?"

Yet the six teachers could not contain themselves and were puffed up with pride, so King Prasenajit went to where the Blessed One was and said this: "The six teachers are pressing again and again for a contest of miracles. May the Blessed One subdue them and show the distinctions of miracles."

The Blessed One said, "I myself know the time." King Prasenajit then gave orders to his ministers. They leveled and swept the place for the contest of miracles, set out an abundance of flowers and incense, set up a lion throne, and adorned it with pennants, victory banners and the like, and a great assembly gathered.

Then, on the first day of the first month of spring, the Blessed One went to the place of the contest of miracles. King Prasenajit set out articles of offering and food, and offered the Blessed One a tooth stick. When the Blessed One had used the tooth stick, he planted it in the ground. As soon as it was planted in the ground, it grew, with spreading branches and leaves, to a height of five hundred leagues. Its branches and leaves also spread out to cover five hundred leagues all around. At once flowers the size of chariot wheels grew on it, and its fruits became as large as jars holding five measures. Its roots, branches, leaves and all its parts turned into the seven precious substances, shining and blazing in all kinds of colors, outshining even the light of the sun and moon. For whoever ate its fruit, the taste surpassed even that of nectar. Its fragrance pervaded everywhere, and all who were touched by its scent became happy and content. When a fragrant breeze touched its branches and leaves, there arose the most delightful sounds of the Dharma. All the people felt the greatest faith in the tree. Then, when the Blessed One taught the Dharma in harmony with the minds of all, very many attained the fruits and were reborn as gods.

The next day King Udrayana invited him and arranged an offering. At that time the Tathāgata emanated two mountains of jewels to his right and left. They were very high and beautiful, glittering with the colors of all kinds of jewels. All kinds of trees grew on those mountains, laden with flowers and fruit, and the sweetest sounds arose from them. On the summit of one mountain, fruits with a hundred flavors, soft and sweet, grew in abundance, and the many people ate them to their heart's content. On the summit of the other mountain grew grass, very sweet and soft, and he so arranged it that the beings born as animals ate it to their heart's content. Since the Blessed One taught them the Dharma in whatever way suited each, their minds were liberated, and because they generated the resolve for unsurpassed awakening, the number reborn as gods in the higher realms was very great.

The next day King Śuncida invited him and arranged an offering.

When the Blessed One rinsed his mouth, the rinsing water he let fall back onto the ground turned into a jewel pool. Its circumference was two hundred leagues, it was adorned with the seven precious substances, and it shimmered in many different colors. The water of that pool possessed the eight qualities, and the floor of the pool was spread with sand of the seven precious substances. It was filled with great lotuses of many kinds, each the size of a chariot wheel: blue lotuses, yellow, white and red ones, orange-red ones, butter-colored ones, and others. The fragrance of those lotuses spread everywhere and was delightful, and each excellent lotus shone with rays of light according to its own color. Their light illuminated all of heaven and earth, and the whole great assembly gazed at that jewel pool, filled with joy, and praised it. Then the Blessed One provided the necessities for the great assembly and taught the Dharma in many ways, so that they understood and realized it. Some generated the resolve for unsurpassed awakening; some attained the fruits and were reborn as gods in the higher realms; and those who generated meritorious karma were beyond measure.

The next day King Indravami set out the articles of offering and invited the Buddha. That day, on each side of the jewel pool, there were eight great channels, whose water flowed around and back into the pool itself. From the sound of the flowing water came the sound of the Dharma in its many forms: the five faculties, the five powers, the seven limbs of awakening, the noble eightfold path, the three doors of liberation, the six superknowledges, the six perfections, great compassion, great loving-kindness, the four immeasurables, and the rest. Having understood and realized that sound of the Dharma, very many attained the fruit of becoming buddhas, were reborn as gods in the higher realms, or generated meritorious karma.

The next day King Brahmadatta invited him and set out the articles of offering. That day, from the Blessed One's mouth there radiated rays of light the color of gold, which illuminated the whole of the great thousandfold world system. All the sentient beings touched by that light were freed of the three poisons and the five obscurations, and their bodies and minds were supremely blissful, just like monks in the third meditative concentration. The great assembly became devoted to the qualities of the Buddha, the Blessed One taught them the Dharma, and those who generated the resolve for unsurpassed awakening, who were reborn as gods in the higher realms, or who generated meritorious karma were countless.

The next day the Licchavis invited him and arranged the articles of offering. That day the Blessed One caused every member of that great assembly to know the thoughts of every other, and made each realize his own virtuous and non-virtuous actions as well. Each of them rejoiced and praised the qualities of the Buddha. Because they listened to the sacred Dharma from the Blessed One, they understood and realized it, and those who made aspirations to buddhahood, who attained the fruits, or who aspired to be reborn as gods in the higher realms were countless.

The next day the Śākya clan invited the Buddha. That day the Blessed One transformed each member of that assembly into a universal monarch, magically endowing each with the seven precious possessions and a thousand sons, and each saw himself honored by vassal kings and ministers. In every one of them arose wonder and marvelous delight, and in that delight the Blessed One analyzed and taught the Dharma, so that each one's mind was satisfied. Those who generated the resolve for unsurpassed awakening, some who attained the fruits, and those who were reborn as gods and humans became countless.

The next day Śakra, that is, Indra, invited the Buddha and set out a lion throne. When the Blessed One sat upon it, Śakra made offerings on his left, and Brahmā, the king, made offerings on his right. As the great assembly sat there in silence, the Blessed One pressed the lion throne with his hand, and from the lion throne there came a great sound like the trumpeting of an elephant. Immediately five great rākṣasas appeared, dragged off the thrones of the six teachers, and smashed them. Vajrapāṇi, holding a vajra with fire blazing at its tip, brandished it over the heads of the six teachers. The six teachers, terrified, scattered and fled in shame, and some leapt into the water and died. Ninety thousand of the followers of the six teachers went for refuge to the Buddha and asked to go forth. When the Blessed One said, "Welcome!", their hair and beards fell away of themselves and they became monks. The Blessed One taught them the Dharma, each as suited him, and all their defilements were exhausted; freed from the afflictions, they became arhats.

Then the Blessed One radiated rays of light from the eighty thousand pores of his body and filled the whole of space. At the tip of each ray of light he emanated a great lotus, and upon each of those lotuses he emanated an emanated buddha with a retinue, teaching the sacred Dharma. He let the whole great assembly see such emanations, and an exceptional faith arose in them. To them the Blessed One taught the Dharma, each as suited him, and among them, from some who generated the resolve for unsurpassed awakening to some who attained the fruits, those who accumulated the merit to be reborn as gods and humans were countless.

The next day Brahmā, the king, invited the Buddha. The Blessed One manifested his body seated, majestic and radiant, so exceedingly tall that it reached up to the Brahmā gods, and with great rays of light he lit up and made bright all of heaven and earth. All of them saw him and heard his voice as well, and to them the Blessed One analyzed and taught the Dharma in its many forms, each as suited them. Of the great assembly, from those who generated the resolve for unsurpassed awakening to those who attained the fruits, those who generated the roots of virtue for rebirth as gods and humans were countless.

The next day the kings of the Four Great Kings invited him. At that time the Blessed One caused the whole great assembly to see the body of the Blessed One filling everything, from the realm of the Four Great Kings up to the Peak of Existence. From those bodies four great rays of light emerged, and he caused the assembly to hear clearly and to see him teaching the sacred Dharma to each as suited them. An exceptional joy arose in that assembly, and because he taught them the Dharma, each as suited them, those who generated the resolve for unsurpassed awakening, those who attained the fruits, and those who generated the roots of virtue for rebirth as gods and humans were countless.

The next day the householder Anāthapiṇḍada invited the Buddha. That day the Blessed One entered the meditative absorption of loving-kindness and, while remaining seated on the lion throne like the color of gold, made his body invisible. He radiated great rays of light, and with an exceedingly gentle voice he analyzed and taught the sacred Dharma, letting the whole great assembly hear it. They generated the resolve for unsurpassed awakening and abided on the ground of non-regression. Some attained the fruits, and those who generated the merit for rebirth as gods and humans were countless.

The next day the householder Cinda invited the Buddha. That day the Blessed One entered the meditative absorption of loving-kindness and radiated rays of light the color of gold, filling the whole great thousandfold world system with light. In all the sentient beings touched by that light, every thought of the three poisons was pacified and a mind of compassion arose, so that all beings became as dear and loving to one another as fathers and mothers, or elder brothers, or younger brothers. After that he taught the Dharma in its many forms, and those who generated the resolve for unsurpassed awakening and abided on the ground of non-regression, those who attained the fruits, and those who generated the roots of virtue for rebirth as gods and humans were countless.

The next day King Śuncida invited the Buddha. That day the Blessed One sat on the lion throne and sent forth two rays of light from his navel. They rose about seven fathoms above his body, and at the top of each there was a lotus. On each lotus he emanated an emanated buddha, and from the navels of those emanated buddhas rays of light came forth, divided in two, and rose about seven fathoms; again there were lotuses, and upon the lotuses sat emanated buddhas. In the same way, step by step, he emanated others like them as far as the great thousandfold world. The whole great assembly, seeing such things, was filled with wonder, and the Blessed One taught them the Dharma, each as suited them. Those who generated the resolve for unsurpassed awakening, those who abided on the ground of non-regression, those who attained the fruits, and those reborn as gods and humans were countless.

The next day King Udrayana invited the Buddha. King Udrayana scattered flowers over the Blessed One, and at once the scattered flowers became one thousand two hundred and fifty jeweled chariots, which lit up the whole great trichiliocosm, the three-thousandfold great thousandfold world system, and were seen by all. Then the Blessed One taught them the sacred Dharma, each as suited them, like giving medicine to the sick. Some generated the resolve for unsurpassed awakening and abided on the ground of non-regression, and those reborn as gods and humans were countless.

The next day King Bimbisāra invited the Buddha. Beforehand the Blessed One had said to the king, "King, prepare vessels for the food." When the time for the midday meal came, all the vessels filled by themselves with food of a hundred flavors in many varieties, enough for the whole great assembly, and when they had eaten it their bodies and minds were supremely at ease.

Then the Blessed One struck the ground with his hand, and in the eighteen hells immeasurable sentient beings were seen undergoing suffering, and each of them was saying, "What kind of evil did we commit in the past, that we must undergo suffering such as this?" He let the whole great assembly hear and see this, and in all of them a mind of tender compassion arose; they were so shaken and frightened that their hair stood on end. Then the Blessed One taught them the Dharma, each as suited them, and their minds were satisfied. Some generated the resolve for unsurpassed awakening, some abided on the ground of non-regression, and those who generated the roots of virtue for rebirth as gods and humans were countless. Those hell beings, through the condition of seeing the Buddha and hearing the Dharma, gave rise to a mind of deep faith, and when they died they were all reborn as gods and humans.

Then King Bimbisāra set both knees on the ground and said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, you who possess the thirty-two wondrous marks: the other marks of your body can be seen, but please show the assembly the mark of the wheel on the soles of the Tathāgata's feet." When he had asked this, the Blessed One stretched out his feet and showed the whole great assembly the mark of the wheel on his soles. The whole assembly saw the mark of the wheel on the underside of his feet, as clear as if it had been drawn, and could not tire of gazing at it. An exceptional joy arose in them, and again the king said to the Blessed One, "What merit did the Blessed One make in the past, that he came to possess such a mark of the wheel?"

The Blessed One declared, "In times past I practiced the ten virtuous actions and also urged others to practice them; because of this I came to possess such a mark, so very clear."

Then the king again said to the Blessed One, "Please, out of your loving concern, teach how the Blessed One practiced the ten virtuous actions and how he urged others to practice the ten virtues."

The Blessed One said to the king, "Listen very attentively and keep it in mind, and I shall teach it."

"Long ago, countless and immeasurable eons in the past, here in Jambudvīpa there was a king called Rathanemi, who ruled over eighty-four thousand vassal kings. He had a hundred thousand cities and ten thousand great ministers. Although that king had twenty thousand queens, he did not have even a single son, and so the king was deeply unhappy. Thinking, 'I have no one to continue my line,' he was overwhelmed with grief. He made offerings to all the gods and spirits, and later, at another time, the chief queen, called Sulipala, became pregnant. From then on the queen became upright and wise, gentle and kind, and she delighted in establishing many beings in virtue. Later, when the months were complete, she gave birth to a baby boy, handsome in form and lovely to behold, excellent beyond the ordinary, with light shining from every pore of his body. The king, too, loved the boy so dearly that he gazed at him without ever tiring. He showed him to the soothsayers, and when the soothsayers examined his marks, good and bad, they rejoiced, saying, 'This boy is truly wondrous!' and they told the king, 'Judging by the marks of this boy, he will rule over the four continents.'

"The king, overjoyed, said to the soothsayers, 'Give the boy a name.' The soothsayers asked the king, 'When this boy entered his mother's womb, what wondrous omens occurred?' He answered, 'The boy's mother became exceptionally upright and kind, and she delighted in establishing others in virtue.' So the soothsayers named the boy Prajñāprabha, Light of Wisdom. He was consecrated as crown prince, and, endowed with a wise intelligence, he surpassed all others.

"Then, some time later, the great king died, and the ministers asked the prince to be consecrated as king. The prince told the ministers, 'I am not able to be king.' The ministers said, 'Now the great king has passed away, and apart from the prince there is no other king at all. If the prince will not be king now, whom are we to set up as king?' The prince said, 'The people of this world practice improper and evil ways. If I carried out killing and punishment, my wrongdoing would be no small thing. But if all of you, the people of the land, are able to practice the ten virtuous actions, then I too am able to be king.' The ministers replied, 'This is good. Please ascend the throne and rule, and we shall decree that all the paths of the ten virtuous actions be practiced.' At once the prince ascended the throne, and all the people agreed to practice the paths of the ten virtuous actions and did as he commanded.

"At that time the king of the māras grew jealous, and in order to ruin the king's reign he wrote a forged letter and sent it to all the vassal kings: 'The decree issued earlier, "Practice virtue," has shown no sign of benefit even though virtue has been practiced; there is no benefit in it against my own afflictions. From now on, practice the ten non-virtues heedlessly, just as you please.' When this forged letter reached the hands of the vassal kings, they were astonished, thinking, 'Why would the great king urge us to commit evil deeds? This is not right.' Not believing it, they asked the king about it. When the king heard what they said, he was shaken and astonished. 'I gave no such command, so how could this have come about?' he thought in alarm, and the king went in person throughout all the lands to see whether the many people had changed from before or not.

"Then the king of the māras conjured by the roadside a man inside a great, fiercely blazing fire pit, crying out piteously in suffering, a sound the king was made to hear. The king went over to him and asked, 'Who are you, and what wrong have you done?' The man said, 'Because in the past I established these people in the ten virtuous actions, I must now undergo such unbearable suffering as this.' The king said, 'For one to undergo such suffering for urging others to do virtue is not in accord with the Dharma. If you undergo such suffering, then did those whom you urged to do virtue, and who did virtue, obtain the fruit of virtue?' He replied, 'They did obtain the fruit of virtue; only the one who urged them to do virtue undergoes suffering like this.' When the king heard this he was overjoyed and said, 'If others will obtain the fruit of virtue, then even if I must undergo suffering, so be it; I have no regret.' When the king of the māras heard this, he immediately made all of it vanish.

"Then the king went throughout all the lands and proclaimed the paths of the ten virtuous actions everywhere. All his subjects took delight in virtue by way of body, speech and mind, and all praised the king's qualities. At that time he came to possess the golden wheel and the seven precious possessions, and he traveled throughout the four continents, devoting himself wholly to establishing beings in virtue.

"The great king of that time is now my father Śuddhodana. The prince's mother of that time is now my mother Māyā. King Prajñāprabha, who established many people in the ten virtues, was I myself. At that time too I myself practiced the ten virtues and established others in the ten virtues as well, and because of this the soles of my feet came to bear wheels with a thousand spokes."

Then King Bimbisāra again said to the Blessed One, "The six teachers, together with their intoxicated and corrupted followers, did not know their own measure. Attached to gain and fame, they gave rise to jealousy and challenged the Blessed One to a contest of miracles, saying, 'If the Blessed One performs one, we will perform two.' Yet although the Blessed One displayed inconceivable miracles, the six teachers could not display even a single miracle; ashamed and humiliated, they leapt into the water. I think that those who perished were utterly intoxicated and deluded."

The Blessed One declared, "Great king, it is not only now that these six teachers, wanting to compete with me for the sake of gain and fame, have thrown away their lives. In times past too, wanting to compete with me, they were parted even from body and life."

Then King Bimbisāra set both knees on the ground and said to the Blessed One, "Please tell us how, in times past, the Blessed One competed with the six teachers."

The Blessed One said to the king, "Great king, keep this constantly in mind. In times past, immeasurable and countless eons ago, here in Jambudvīpa there was a king called Mahāśakuli, who ruled over five hundred vassal kings. Although that king had five hundred queens, he had not a single son to continue his line, and so the king thought, 'I am old and frail, and I have not even one son to continue my line. If, after I have passed away, the vassal kings will not heed one another and lead out their armies, beings will die without having done any wrong, and the realm too will fall into turmoil.' Thinking this, his mind was tormented with grief and he sank into despondency.

"At that time Śakra, lord of the gods, that is, Indra, saw from afar that the king was unhappy and despondent. He came down from the sky, transformed himself into a physician, went to the king and asked, 'King, why are you so dejected and unhappy?' The king told him everything as before. The conjured physician said to the king, 'King, do not grieve. For your sake I shall go to the snow mountains, gather many kinds of medicines from the snow mountains, and have the queens eat them, and as soon as they have eaten them all the queens will become pregnant.' When the king heard him say this he thought, 'These words dispel my grief,' and he said to the physician, 'If you can do this, that will be excellent.'

"Then the conjured physician went to the snow mountains, gathered many kinds of medicines, carried them back as a load, and returned to the king's palace. He boiled the medicines in milk and gave them to the chief queen. But the chief queen, finding the medicine foul-smelling and having no faith in it, would not drink it, and the conjured physician went back to the abode of the gods. The other, lesser queens drank that medicine, and not long after they had drunk it, each of them became pregnant. They told the chief queen so, and the chief queen, regretful and unhappy, asked, 'Is any of that medicine left?' They said, 'It is used up.' When she asked, 'Then are there at least some dregs of the medicine?' they said, 'There are dregs of the medicine.' Then the chief queen boiled the dregs of the medicine in milk and drank it, and not long after she became pregnant.

"When their months were complete, the other queens who had drunk the medicine earlier each gave birth to sons, every one of them fine and lovely to behold. The king was overjoyed and thought only of when the chief queen would give birth to her son. The chief queen too, when her months were complete, gave birth to a boy, but he was ugly in appearance, like a block of wood. The king and queen were unhappy and named the boy Stump.

"Then they raised him, and when he had grown up all his elder brothers took wives; only Prince Stump did not take a wife. At another time a hostile king led out his army and drew near. The five hundred princes too marshaled their troops and went out to attack the enemy army, but they were defeated in battle and came fleeing back to the palace. Prince Stump said to his elder brothers, 'Why have you fled in such terror?' His elder brothers said, 'We went out with the army to attack the enemy army, but we were defeated in battle and, struck down by the enemy army, we fled.' Prince Stump said, 'An enemy of that size cannot get the better of us. Bring the bow and conch of our grandfather that are now in the shrine, and I will go out to attack them.'

"That prince's grandfather had been a universal monarch. Many people together, with great effort, lifted the bow of the universal monarch and presented it to the prince. The prince took the bow and drew it, and the sound of the twanging bowstring roared like thunder and could be heard for forty leagues. Then the prince, carrying the conch and the bow, went out alone to attack the enemy. First he blew the conch, and the sound of the conch rang out like a falling thunderbolt; merely hearing it, the enemy army was terrified and all of them scattered.

"Then the prince returned, and only now did the king and queen come to love him, and they thought of taking a wife for the prince. At that time a king called Liśipaca had a daughter, said to be rarely fine in all the world. Hearing of her, the king sent messengers to ask for the girl as a bride. Showing them a handsome one among the princes, they said, 'This is the bridegroom,' and that king agreed to give his daughter. Then the king, overjoyed, arranged the preparations for welcoming the bride, and he sent Prince Stump himself to welcome his wife, instructing him, 'Do not meet your wife by day; meet her by night,' and sent him off.

"Then at another time the girl met with the other sisters-in-law, and each of them spoke of her husband's good qualities. The wife of Prince Stump also praised her husband: 'My husband is brave and his strength is equal to a wrestler's, and the flesh of his body is very soft.' The sisters-in-law said, 'Say no such thing! Your husband is extremely ugly; he is like a tree stump, so if you were to see him by day you would be horrified.' When Stump's wife heard her sisters-in-law say this, she kept it in mind. At night she lit a lamp, and after her husband had fallen asleep she brought out the lamp and looked. Seeing him to be extremely ugly, she was horrified and frightened, and at once she fled back to her parents' home."

"Prince Stump only noticed it when dawn came, and, unhappy, he took his bow and conch and set off to his wife's country to seek his wife. Having arrived in that country, he stayed with a minister, and while he was staying there, at another time, the kings of six vassal states, having heard that King Liśipaca had a fair daughter, each came with an army to ask for the girl.

"At that, King Liśipaca was unhappy. He gathered his ministers and took counsel: 'If I give this girl to one king, the others will be displeased and bear a grudge. What can be done so that these enemies will be no more?' One minister said, 'Cut this girl into pieces and give a piece to each, and they will be satisfied.' Some ministers said, 'It would be fitting to issue this decree: "Whoever is able to turn back these armies, to him the girl will be given as wife, and half the country too will be given as a reward."' The king heeded that counsel and issued the decree.

"Then King Stump took his bow and conch and went outside the city to where the enemies were. He blew the conch and merely twanged the bowstring, and all the enemy armies were so terrified that they could not even stir to battle. Then he went into their armies, cut off the heads of all six kings, took their diadems, and gathered those armies into his own following. King Liśipaca was overjoyed. He gave him the girl as his wife and made him a great king, uniting the realms of the six kings into one. Then, with his troops, and accompanied by his wife, he returned to his own country.

"When the king heard that his son had returned, he went out to welcome him and gave the kingdom to the prince, but the prince would not accept it and handed it back to his father. He lived with his wife as before, and he asked his wife, 'Why did you abandon me in the night and flee?' She said, 'You are so very ugly that I thought you were not human, and I was afraid and fled.' Then the prince took a mirror, and looking into the mirror he saw that his own appearance was extremely ugly. He grew so weary of it that he did not even wish to look at his body, and he went into a grove and wept.

"Śakra, lord of the gods, saw him from afar, came to him and asked, 'Why are you weeping?' The king told him what had happened, and Śakra cheered him. He also gave him a precious jewel and said, 'Always wear this jewel on the crown of your head, and you will become fine and handsome like me.' So he instructed him, and as soon as the prince wore the jewel on the crown of his head, he felt that his body had become better than before. He went back into the palace, took his bow and conch, and was going off to amuse himself, when his wife, not recognizing him, said, 'Who are you? Do not touch these, lest my husband come and harm you.' He said to his wife, 'I am your husband.' But his wife did not believe it and said, 'My husband is extremely ugly. You are extremely handsome, so how can you be my husband?' As soon as the prince untied the jewel from the crown of his head, she saw him ugly again and was overjoyed. When she asked, 'Why has this happened?' the prince told her in detail why it had come about. From then on husband and wife loved each other, and the prince's name, Stump, was changed after him, and he was named Prince Sulanśan.

"Then, at another time, that prince thought, 'Now I will set out with my troops and build a palace.' Having thought this, he went to a place where the ground was broad and level and gave the command, 'Build a palace here.' Four nāga kings, transforming themselves into men, asked, 'If you build a palace here, what will you build it from?' Prince Sulanśan said, 'It shall be made of earth.' The four nāga kings said, 'Why not build it of jewels?' The king said, 'If a great palace must be built, where could one find so many jewels?' The nāga king said, 'We will offer them.' They conjured a spring on each of its four sides and said, 'If you draw from the spring on the eastern side and build with it, everything will turn to beryl. If you draw from the spring on the southern side and build with it, everything will turn to gold. If you draw from the spring on the western side and build with it, everything will turn to silver. If you draw from the spring on the northern side and build with it, everything will turn to crystal.' The prince drew from the springs just as they had said, and it turned out just so.

"Then he made a palace wall four hundred leagues in circumference, and within it he built a palace forty leagues across. The multistoried houses along the streets, the gardens and the bathing pools were also made of the four kinds of jewels. In excellence he made it like a palace of the gods. As soon as it was finished, the seven precious possessions also appeared, and he ruled over the four continents and made virtue flourish.

"Great king, King Mahāśakuli at that time is now my father, King Śuddhodana. The queen at that time is now my mother Māyā. The ugly prince Stump is now I myself. His wife was Prajāpatī. That wife's father was Kāśyapa. The six kings who led out armies wanting the girl are these six teachers. At that time too they contended with me over a beauty, and I killed them all and gathered their troops into my following. Because of this, down to the present they are jealous over gain and fame, and though they wanted to compete with me they had no miracles; since things did not turn out as they had planned, they died in the water. In this way their following of nine hundred thousand was gathered into my following and became my close disciples."

Then King Bimbisāra said to the Blessed One, "What merit did King Stump make in the past, that he came to have such great might and strength and yet was so extremely ugly?"

The Blessed One said to the king, "There is a cause and there are conditions for it, and I shall explain them."

"Long ago, countless, immeasurable eons in the past, here in Jambudvīpa, in the land called Vārāṇasī, there lived a pratyekabuddha called Sage Mountain. That pratyekabuddha suffered from a wind disorder, and for the wind disorder he needed to drink sesame oil as medicine. So he went to the house of an oil presser and begged for sesame oil. The oil presser grew angry with him and reviled and abused him, saying, 'Look at this one, with a head like a stump of wood and arms and legs like sticks of kindling! He is unfit to do any work, and he cannot even earn a single coin; he lives by begging alone!' Nevertheless, he gave him the dregs of the oil.

Having received the dregs of the oil, the pratyekabuddha came out to the door, and as he was leaving he met the oil presser's wife at the doorway. A feeling of faith in the pratyekabuddha arose in her, and she asked, 'Sir, where have you come from, and what will you do with these oil dregs?' The pratyekabuddha told her plainly just what had happened. The wife then called the pratyekabuddha back, led him into the house, took his alms bowl, filled the bowl with sesame oil, and offered it to him. Then she said to her husband, 'Offering the dregs of the oil to this man was a grave wrong on your part. Confess the fault of your words!' At her urging, the man too felt remorse and made confession, and then husband and wife, of one mind, said to the pratyekabuddha, 'Venerable one, from now on, whenever you need oil, always come to us.'

From then on the pratyekabuddha always took his oil from their house. And to repay their kindness he displayed miracles before them: rising up into the sky above, he let water stream down and fire blaze, and displayed many kinds of miracles. When the husband and wife who had offered the oil saw such miracles, they rejoiced all the more, and faith arose in them.

The man said to his wife, 'Whatever merit there is in your having offered the oil, let it be shared with me, and let us be husband and wife.' The wife replied, 'You spoke harsh words to this great being and offered him the dregs of the oil without any faith, so wherever you are born you will come out extremely ugly. How could I be your wife?' The husband said, 'I am always toiling away, doing nothing but the work of pressing oil. Why will you not dedicate the fruit of the giving together with me? I will not hear of it; you must be my wife.' The wife said, 'If I do become your wife, since you are so very ugly, I will abandon you and run away in the night.' The husband said, 'Even if you run away, I will chase after you, catch you, and bring you back.' So the husband and wife spoke to each other, and they made confession to the pratyekabuddha with their whole body and life.

Then the pratyekabuddha said to the husband and wife, 'Because you two have given me oil, my illness is cured. So, husband and wife, say what you wish for, and it will be accomplished just so.' When he had said this, the husband and wife were overjoyed. Kneeling on the ground, they made this aspiration: 'Wherever we two are born, whether as gods or as humans, may all be accomplished as we wish.' Such was the aspiration they made.

Great king, the oil presser of that time was Prince Stump. His wife was none other than Stump's wife. Because at that time he spoke harsh words to the pratyekabuddha, saying he was like a stump, and gave him the dregs of the oil in anger, he became extremely ugly in all his births. Because he later made confession and offered fine oil, in all his births he afterward became fair and handsome. Because he offered the oil, in all his births he was endowed with might and strength and was victorious everywhere. Through that merit he became a wheel-turning king (cakravartin), ruling the four continents and enjoying the five sense pleasures as he wished. The ripening of virtuous and nonvirtuous karma never comes to nothing. Therefore, be diligent in guarding the actions of body, speech, and mind."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, King Bimbisāra and the other kings, the ministers, the fourfold assembly, and the gods, nāgas, and others who heard it attained various results. Some attained the fruit of stream-entry. Some attained the fruits of once-returner, non-returner, and arhat. Some produced roots of virtue for becoming pratyekabuddhas. Some generated the mind set on unsurpassed awakening and came to abide on the irreversible ground. The whole assembly rejoiced at what the Blessed One had said and was delighted.

This was the thirteenth chapter, on the subduing of the six teachers.

Fascicle Four.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Rājagṛha, on the mountain Vulture Peak. At that time the body of the Blessed One was seized by a chill ailment. The physician Jīvaka compounded thirty-two kinds of medicine with ghee, and each day the Blessed One took thirty-two measures of the medicine.

The wicked Devadatta, his mind filled with jealousy, had proclaimed, "I am the Buddha's equal." When he heard that the Blessed One was taking medicine, he wanted medicine too, so as to be like the Buddha, and he ordered the physician Jīvaka, "Prepare the medicine that is given to the Blessed One for me as well, and give it to me." So Jīvaka prepared the medicine and gave it to Devadatta, instructing him, "Take four measures of the medicine each day." Devadatta asked, "How many measures does the Blessed One take each day now?" Jīvaka answered, "The Blessed One now takes thirty-two measures each day." Devadatta commanded, "Then give me thirty-two measures each day as well." Jīvaka said, "Your body is not like the body of the Tathāgata. If you consume a large amount, you will not digest it, and without doubt it will later turn into illness." Devadatta said, "I have the digestive heat to digest however much I eat. What difference is there between me and the Buddha?" And so, just like the Buddha, he took thirty-two measures of the medicine in a single day. The medicine did not digest; stabbing pains shot through all his channels, he was in torment, and in extreme suffering he cried out and wailed.

Then the Blessed One, moved by loving compassion for him, stretched out his hand from afar and stroked Devadatta's head. At once all the medicine was digested, and he was freed from the suffering of his illness. Devadatta recognized that it had been the hand of the Blessed One, and he said, "Prince Siddhārtha has studied medicine too as one of his skills, and he poses as a learned physician as well. I want none of it."

At that time Ānanda heard him say this and was deeply displeased. Kneeling on the ground, he said to the Blessed One, "This Devadatta has no gratitude for kindness. Even though the Blessed One, moved by loving compassion for him, completely cured his illness, he has turned around and, with an unwholesome mind, spoken uncalled-for words, and he constantly reviles the Blessed One."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "It is not only now that Devadatta has given rise to an unwholesome mind toward me and intends to harm me. In times long past, too, he gave rise to an evil mind toward me and killed me."

Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "Please explain what Devadatta did in times past."

The Blessed One said, "Long ago, countless, immeasurable eons in the past, here in Jambudvīpa, in the land called Vārāṇasī, there was a king called Brahmadatta who was wrathful, fierce, and pitiless, given to great attachment and aversion, who oppressed people with evil deeds and delighted in the work of killing.

At that time the king, in a dream, saw a wild beast whose body hair was like gold, a beast from the tips of whose hairs a golden light streamed out, making everything to its right and left appear golden in color. As soon as he had dreamed this he awoke, and he thought, 'Such a thing as I have seen in my dream must surely exist. I will have the hunters search for its skin.' So he gathered the hunters and commanded them, 'In my dream I saw a wild beast the color of gold, from the tips of whose hairs a golden color, as it were, streamed out. Such a creature surely exists in this land, so search for it everywhere. Flay its skin and present it to me, and I will make your children and grandchildren happy and comfortable for seven generations to come. But if you do not search wholeheartedly, or you are lazy about it and do not find it, I will wipe you out, root and lineage.'

The hunters were deeply dismayed. They all gathered in a secluded place and took counsel together, saying, 'No one has ever seen, from ancient times on, the beast the king dreamed of. If we do not find it, then since the king's decrees are harsh, we will be killed.' Having taken counsel, they grew even more dismayed, and they said further, 'In these forests there are many venomous snakes and savage beasts. We cannot all travel long roads, staking our lives and not sparing them, to get this done. So let us send one capable man from among us to search. If that man sees it, he can then report to us. That would be best.' Having said this, they spoke to one man: 'Go and search everywhere, at the risk of your life. If you find it, we will collect wealth from our common funds and give you a great deal. And even if you do not come back, we will give it to your son and your wife.'

The man thought, 'For the sake of so many people, it is worthwhile even if I lose my body and life.' So he prepared provisions for the journey and set out to search everywhere through the fearsome mountain forests. After a long time had passed he was utterly exhausted. In the hot season of summer, he came to a place where the burning sand was knee-deep, and he collapsed from fatigue. Tormented by thirst and on the brink of death, he cried, 'Is there anyone with compassion who can be a refuge for my wretched body and life?'
