At that time, on that mountain, there lived a wild beast called Kunta, the hair of whose body was the color of gold, and from the tips of whose hairs a golden light streamed out. From afar he heard the words the man had spoken, and a mind of deep tender love arose in him. He plunged his body into a place where there was a cool spring, came to the man, and held him close in his embrace. When the man had revived a little, Kunta led him to the water's edge, washed him, gathered fruits, and had him eat.

When the man had recovered, he thought, 'This beast is exactly the one the king wants. When I was about to lose my life, he became the refuge of my life. How could I conceive the intention of killing him without having repaid even a little of his kindness? Yet if I do not get him, the hunters, together with their relatives, will all be caught up in punishment.' As he sat there unhappy, the beast asked him, 'Why are you so unhappy?' He shed tears and told him everything in detail.

Kunta said, 'Do not be unhappy about this. This skin of mine is very easy to obtain. When I reflect, though I have given up countless bodies in former lives, I never did a deed by which my body turned into merit. With the skin of this body I will save their lives.' Having said this, he felt great joy and said, 'Flay my skin without taking my life, and take it away. I give it to you without any regret.'

Then the hunter slowly flayed off the skin, and Kunta made this aspiration: 'By this merit of having let my skin be flayed and given away, and of having saved the precious lives of many people, which I dedicate to all sentient beings, may I become fully and perfectly awakened in unsurpassed awakening, free all sentient beings from the suffering of saṃsāra, and establish them in the bliss of nirvāṇa.' Such was the aspiration he made.

At that moment the great trichiliocosm, the world system of a billion worlds, shook in six ways, and all the palaces of the gods rocked and trembled. The gods, alarmed, looked down and saw that the bodhisattva had made a gift of the skin of his body. They descended from the sky, came to where Kunta was, strewed divine flowers over him in offering, and shed tears that fell like rain.

After the skin had been flayed off and the hunter had gone, blood oozed from all over that raw flesh. Eighty thousand flies, ants, and other creatures gathered on his body, and all of them fed on him. For fear that they would flee, he did not move his body at all. Having made the gift of his body, he died right there. The flies and insects that had eaten the bodhisattva's flesh also, when they passed away, were all reborn as gods in the higher realms.

Then the hunter took the skin and presented it to the king. The king was overjoyed and said, "There is nothing to equal the softness of this!" He spread it out as the mat on which he always slept, and he slept on it.

"Ānanda, understand it in this way. The wild beast Kunta of that time and that occasion is I myself now. King Brahmadatta was Devadatta. The eighty thousand insects were the eighty thousand sons of the gods who attained the fruit when I first became fully awakened and turned the wheel of Dharma. Devadatta killed me at that time too, and down to the present he still thinks, with an evil mind, of killing me."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, the venerable Ānanda and the great assembly became intent on wholesome qualities. Some attained the fruit of stream-entry, some that of once-returner, some those of non-returner and arhat. Some produced roots of virtue for becoming pratyekabuddhas. Some generated the mind set on unsurpassed awakening and came to abide on the irreversible ground. They rejoiced at what the Blessed One had said and were delighted.

This was the fourteenth chapter, on the wild beast Kunta's gift of his body.

The merit of anyone who goes forth is great indeed. Whether one permits one's son, or daughter, or any of one's servants to go forth, or goes forth oneself, the merit of that is immeasurable. The merit of one who practices giving is such that one will enjoy wealth for up to ten lifetimes; but compared with being reborn a thousand times over as a god in the six levels of heaven, the merit of letting a single person go forth, or of going forth oneself, is supremely greater than the former. Why is that? The merit of giving has an end where it is exhausted, but the merit of going forth is immeasurable and endless.

Moreover, through the merit of guarding moral discipline one may become a sage endowed with the five superknowledges and enjoy the pleasures of the higher realms up to the world of Brahmā; but the merit of going forth in the teaching of the Buddha is inconceivable, and the merit does not run out until nirvāṇa.

Even if someone were to build a stūpa of the seven precious substances so tall that it reached the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, the merit of it would not match the merit of going forth. Why is that? A stūpa of the seven precious substances can be destroyed by wicked fools, but the merit of going forth cannot be destroyed. When one seeks the wholesome Dharma, apart from the Dharma of the Buddha there is no other Dharma superior to it.

For example, suppose a skilled physician opened the eyes of a hundred blind people and made them all see at once; and further, suppose that when a hundred people were about to have their eyes gouged out, a person of power freed them from having their eyes put out. The merit of these two people would be immeasurable; nevertheless, it would not match the merit of letting another go forth or of going forth oneself. Why is that? The giving of eyes to those people by these two persons only lets them see worldly benefit and happiness, and the nature of the eye of flesh is a perishable nature. But when one lets another go forth or goes forth oneself, then, as one person teaches and leads another, the realm of sentient beings will come, until the end of the eon, to have the unsurpassed eye of wisdom, and the nature of the eye of wisdom is not to perish throughout the eon. Through that merit, one experiences among gods and humans, as one wishes, pleasures and enjoyments that never run out and have no end, and in the end one also becomes fully awakened. Why is that? The Dharma of going forth does away with the retinue of Māra and keeps up the lineage of the Buddha. It does away with evil, unwholesome qualities and gives rise to wholesome qualities. It does away with the stain of wrongdoing and gives rise to deeds of unsurpassed merit. Therefore the Blessed One has said, "The merit of going forth is loftier than Mount Meru, deeper than the ocean, and vaster than the sky."

If anyone obstructs and hinders another's going forth, so that it does not happen as that person intended, that very grave wrongdoing is like a person who goes into a dark place and sees nothing at all. The ripening of that person's evil is just like that: he falls into the hell of utter darkness, the great black darkness where nothing can be seen. Just as all the rivers and streams flow down into the ocean, so it is with the ripening of that person's evil: all faults gather in his body. Just as Mount Meru, when the fire at the end of the eon arises, is burned up without remainder, so he too is burned up by the fire of hell without remainder; it is just like that.

If one permits another to go forth, or goes forth oneself, the merit of that is very great. Going forth becomes the cause of nirvāṇa: with the utterly pure water of the sūtras one washes away all the stains of the afflictions and completely clears away the suffering of saṃsāra. With the feet of the Vinaya one walks upon the utterly pure ground of moral discipline; with the eye of the Abhidharma one sees the virtue and evil of the world; and one travels as one wishes on the noble eightfold path and reaches the city of nirvāṇa. Therefore, whether old or young, if one goes forth, or permits another to go forth, or goes forth oneself, the merit of that is supremely great.

At that time the Blessed One was staying at Rājagṛha, in the Bamboo Grove at Kalandakanivāpa. At that time in Rājagṛha there was a householder called Śrījāta, who was a hundred years old. He heard that the qualities of going forth were immeasurable in this way, and he thought, "Why should I not go forth in the teaching of the Buddha?" Having thought this, he told his wife, his sons, and his servants, "I am going forth."

Because the man was decrepit with age, everyone in his household was weary of him, and thinking, "He is of no use," when they heard him say, "I am going forth," they were glad and said, "It is high time you went, and quickly!"

So the householder left his home and went to the Bamboo Grove to request ordination from the Blessed One. When he arrived at the Bamboo Grove he asked the monks, "Where is the Buddha, the Blessed One, the one of great compassion, who works extensively for the benefit and welfare of gods and humans?" The monks said, "The Buddha, the Blessed One, is traveling about for the benefit of many beings and has gone elsewhere." The householder then asked the monks, "Who is foremost in learning and wisdom among the retinue of the Buddha, the great Teacher?" The monks pointed him out, saying, "It is the venerable Śāriputra."

Leaning on his staff, the old man went to where Śāriputra was. When he arrived, he set down his staff, bowed, and said, "Venerable one, please let me go forth." The venerable Śāriputra examined him and thought, "This householder is old, so he will fall short in the three activities, study, meditative concentration, and the work of the Saṅgha, and will not be able to manage them." Having thought this, he said, "Go back. You are decrepit with age, and your time has passed. It is not proper for you to go forth." So he told him.

Then he went in turn to Mahākāśyapa, Upāli, Aniruddha, and all the other arhats and made his request. Every one of them asked him, "Have you not already asked someone before?" "Earlier I asked the venerable Śāriputra." "Then what did he say?" "He said, 'You are decrepit with age and your time has passed, so it is not proper for you to go forth.'" Then those monks said, "If even Śāriputra, foremost in learning and wisdom, did not permit it, we too are of the same view and do not permit it. For example, when a skilled physician, expert in the healing art, examines a patient and gives him up without treating him, the other, lesser physicians will not treat him either, because they understand that he has the signs of certain death. Since even Śāriputra, who is endowed with intelligence, did not permit it, the other monks too are of the same view and do not permit it." So they told him.

Since the monks would not permit him to go forth, the householder left the Bamboo Grove, sat down on the threshold of the gate, raised a loud cry, and sat there lamenting: "Since I was first born I have committed no great wrong, so why will they not let me go forth? Even people like Upāli, who comes from the barbers, and the sweeper who throws away filth, and Aṅgulimāla, who killed countless people, and Asika, and other such wicked bandits have gone forth. What fault do I have, that they will not let me go forth?"

Then the Blessed One went to him. When he revealed his form, it shone forth brilliantly with rays of light; adorned with the major and minor marks, he appeared like the banner of the seven precious substances of Śakra, lord of the gods. He said to the householder Śrījāta, "Why are you weeping?"

At that time, when the householder heard the Buddha's voice, which was like the voice of Brahmā, he was overjoyed, delighted, and elated, as glad as a child who sees its mother. Touching the five points of his body to the ground, he bowed to the Buddha and said, "Among sentient beings, even base people who kill, who commit robbery, who tell lies, and who slander others go forth. What real sin do I alone have, that I am not permitted to go forth in the teaching of the Buddha? Everyone in my household too has grown weary of me because I am decrepit with age, and they say I am of no use. So if I am not permitted to go forth in the Buddha's teaching, even if I go back home they will surely not let me into the house. I have nowhere at all to go, and this very day I shall certainly lose my life."

Then the Blessed One said to the householder Śrījāta, "Who is it that, raising his hand to the sky, declares with certainty, 'This one may go forth; this one may not go forth'?"

The householder said, "Blessed One, it was Śāriputra, foremost in learning in the retinue of the king who turns the wheel of Dharma, the greatest after the Buddha, the caravan leader of the world, who would not permit me to go forth in the teaching."

Then the Blessed One, with great compassion, just as parents speak gentle words to a deeply devoted son, encouraged the householder Śrījāta and said, "Do not grieve. I will let you go forth. It was not Śāriputra who practiced hardships with diligence for three countless eons and made merit for a hundred eons. Nor was it Śāriputra who, in all his lives, practiced what is so very hard to do, giving away as gifts his head cut off, his eyes torn out, his bones, his marrow, his flesh, his blood, his skin, his feet, his hands, his ears, and his nose cut off. It was not Śāriputra who gave his body to a tigress, leapt into a fire pit, had a thousand iron nails driven into his body, and had a thousand lamps set into his body. It was not Śāriputra who performed acts of giving of lands, cities, wives, sons, male servants, female servants, elephants, horses, and the seven precious substances. It was not Śāriputra who, in the first countless eon, served eighty-eight thousand buddhas; in the middle countless eon, served ninety-nine thousand buddhas; and in the last countless eon, served one hundred thousand buddhas, and having gone forth, brought the perfection of moral discipline to completion. Since Śāriputra is not master of the Dharma, why does he say, 'This one is unfit to go forth'? I alone am master of the Dharma. Upheld by the six perfections and wearing the armor of patience, I sat on the vajra seat at the foot of the tree of awakening, subdued the hosts of Māra, and became fully awakened. There is no one equal to me."

"Therefore, follow after me, and I will let you go forth." When the Blessed One had encouraged him in this way by various kinds of skillful means, all his grief subsided, great joy arose in his mind, and he went to the monastery following after the Buddha.

Then the Blessed One said to Mahāmaudgalyāyana, "Let this householder go forth." Why was that? Sentient beings are trained by the one with whom their karma accords. Those whose karma accords with the Buddha are not trained by anyone else, and those whose karma accords with another are not trained by the Buddha either. Those whose karma accords with Śāriputra are not trained by Maudgalyāyana, Kāśyapa, Aniruddha, Nabun, or any of the other close disciples. In the same way, whoever's karma accords with a particular one is not trained by anyone else.

At that time Maudgalyāyana too thought, "This householder is decrepit with age and will not be able to learn recitation, practice meditative concentration, or do the work of the Saṅgha; he will fall short in the three activities." Yet since the Blessed One, the king of Dharma, had said, "Let him go forth," he thought, "It would not be proper to refuse," and so he let him go forth and gave him full ordination.

In his former lives this man had produced the cause for becoming liberated. Caught by the hook of the Dharma, just as a fish that has taken the hook is certain to be drawn out, he had produced the cause of liberation and had also gathered a store of virtue. So he applied himself with diligence day and night, trained in reading and recitation, and came to understand and realize the sūtras, the Vinaya, and the Abhidharma extensively. But because he was decrepit with age, he was unable to properly perform the services, the acts of respect, the receiving and seeing off, the speaking courteously to his elders, and the bowing.

Thereupon some young monks who had gone forth earlier and so held higher seniority thought, "This old monk is proud of his recitation, his reading, and his learning, and he shows us no respect," and they harassed him constantly. Then the old monk thought, "When I lived at home I was tormented by my wife and sons. I thought that once I had gone forth I would be free of such things. Now once again I am tormented by these young monks. What wrong did I do in the past, that I am afflicted by suffering like this? Now I might as well die."

Having thought this, he went to the bank of a great river that flowed beside that grove. He took off his Dharma robes and hung them on the branch of a tree, knelt on the ground, shed tears, and made this vow: "I am not forsaking the Buddha, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha; I am giving up this body alone. By the merit of the giving I have practiced, the moral discipline I have guarded, the diligence I have undertaken, and the sūtras I have recited, when I have parted from this body, may I be born into a family that is powerful and rich, endowed with wealth, and with a harmonious household; may no one obstruct my entering into wholesome Dharma; may I always meet with the Three Jewels; may I go forth and apply myself to the Dharma; may I meet a learned teacher; and may he show me how to pass into nirvāṇa." Such was the vow he made. Then, where the water flowed fiercely, deep and swirling in eddies, he made ready to leap boldly in.

At that moment Maudgalyāyana, wondering, "What is my old disciple doing now?", looked with his divine eye and saw that his disciple was leaping into the water. Before he reached the water, Maudgalyāyana caught him up by the power of his miraculous ability, set him down on the riverbank, and asked, "Son of the Dharma, what are you doing?"

The old man was now ashamed and embarrassed, and he thought, "How shall I answer? If I tell a lie and deceive my teacher, then because I have deceived my teacher I shall carry, in all my lives, the fault of being without the faculty of the tongue. And even if I tell a lie, my teacher has superknowledge, so he will see through it. In the world, one who has learned wisdom and is by nature honest and free of guile will be honored by the gods. One who has learned wisdom but has some guile is not fit to be a teacher of others, though he will be honored by humans. One who has no learning but also no guile and is honest, though he cannot work for the benefit of others, will work for his own benefit. One who is a dull fool, full of delusion, and has guile as well is a vicious enemy to all; whatever he says, people say, 'This is a lie; it is a deception.' Even when he speaks a true word, no one believes him. Therefore it would be wrong and unseemly for me to lie to my teacher and deceive him; I will speak the truth." Having thought this, he said to his teacher, "Weary of the household, I went forth from home into homelessness. If I may tell the truth, I have now become unhappy again, and I thought to kill myself."

When Maudgalyāyana heard him say this, he thought, "Unless I frighten him with the terrors of birth and death, his going forth will come to nothing." Having thought this, he said to him, "Hold fast to the hem of my Dharma robe and do not let go." Then he rose up into the sky above, as easily as the wind blows away a feather, as easily as one carrying a single hair in one's hand by miraculous power, and so he went. And again, just as a hawk carries off a sparrow chick, he rose into the sky by Maudgalyāyana's miraculous power, and in the time it takes a man to bend his outstretched arm or stretch out his bent arm, he reached the shore of the ocean.

On that ocean shore lay the corpse of a woman who had just died, very lovely and fair, finely adorned, and endowed with every fine feature. A snake was entering its mouth and coming out through its nose, entering through its eyes and coming out through its ears. Maudgalyāyana examined it closely.

Then the monk Śrījāta asked, "Who is this woman, endowed with such fine features?" Maudgalyāyana said, "When the time comes, I will show you," and they went on again. There a woman had set up a great copper cauldron, poured water into it, and kindled a great fire beneath it. When the water boiled, she took off her clothes and went into it. At once all her hair and body hair came away, her flesh was cooked, and her bones too came apart, and the boiling water cast the bones out. When the wind blew upon those bones again, at once she became a person once more, and Śrījāta saw her take the flesh out of the cauldron and eat it.

The monk Śrījāta was terrified, and his hair stood on end. He asked, "Master, who is this who eats her own flesh?" Maudgalyāyana said, "When the time comes, I will show you."

Going on further, he heard a great tree being devoured by a multitude of worms, so many that between its leaves and branches there was not even a needle point's worth of empty space, and from it came the sound of loud weeping and wailing, resounding everywhere like the sound of the hells. He asked, "Who is this, with such a dreadful sound?" Maudgalyāyana said, "When the time comes, I will show you."

Then again he saw a man surrounded in a ring by many hungry ghosts with human bodies and the heads of beasts of prey. They held bows and arrows in their hands, and all of them shot arrows like blazing fire at that man, so that he was one single mass of flame. He asked, "Who is this, who undergoes such unbearable suffering and yet has no place to flee?" The master told him, "Wait a while. When the time comes, I will show you."

Then, going on further, after a long while, at the foot of a great mountain swords and sharp weapons had been planted with their edges pointing upward. A man ran down from the summit of the mountain and, as he went, he was pierced and slashed by the swords and weapons; yet the swords and weapons stayed planted in the ground, and he kept running like that without respite. Seeing this, Śrījāta asked, "Who is this?" The master told him, "When the time comes, I will show you."

Then, going on again, there was a great mountain of bones, seven hundred yojanas high, which blocked out even the sun and darkened the ocean with its shadow. Maudgalyāyana climbed it and went back and forth upon a single rib bone. Seeing that he himself had gone along following behind, Śrījāta thought, "Lest I become separated from the master, I shall ask about those things I saw before." So he said, "Master, please explain to me those things I saw before."

Maudgalyāyana replied, "Now the time has come, and I shall explain them to you. If you wish to know about the woman you saw first, she was the wife of a man of Rājagṛha called Great Sarphak. Because she was beautiful, her husband loved her exceedingly. At that time, when Sarphak was setting out onto the great ocean, he could not bear to leave his wife behind, so he took her with him and went out to sea with about five hundred merchants. Then his wife would constantly bind three sticks together at the top to make something like a spear stand, set a mirror on it, and look into the mirror; seeing how beautiful she was, she conceived intense attachment. Then a great turtle from the ocean crushed the ship, the ship broke apart, and all the five hundred or so merchants died. By the very nature of the ocean, a corpse does not remain in it overnight: the water cast her out and she came to the ocean shore. When beings die, they are born wherever they are attached."

Then the question was put, "In that case, who would delight to be born in the hells, that so many come to be born in the hells?"

He said, "Those beings who steal the wealth of the Three Jewels, or steal their parents' possessions, or commit the grave misdeed of taking human life, fall into the great Blazing Hell. Such a person, seized by a wind disorder or a cold disease, longs in his mind for fire. As soon as he thinks of going into the fire, he dies and goes to the Blazing Hell.

"Those who steal the lamps or jewels offered to the Buddha, or steal the lamps or firewood of the Saṅgha, or tear down the dwellings of the Saṅgha, or tear down a hall where the Dharma is taught, or snatch other people's clothing in the cold of winter, or, having the power to do so, strip the clothes from male or female servants or other people out in the freezing wind and pour water over them, or strip and seize others' clothes, fall through this wrongdoing into the Chabdrom Hell. Such a person, tormented by a fever, constantly thinks of a cool and cold place, and because he longs for cold, as soon as he dies he is born in that hell. The Utpala, Padma, Kumuda and Puṇḍarīka hells are just the same. When beings undergo suffering in the cold hells, the flesh of their bodies cracks and splits into mouthful-sized pieces like grain bursting as it is roasted, and their heads and bones too crack and split into a hundred thousand fragments.

"Those beings who are miserly, who cut off others' livelihood and abandon them to hunger, and who do not give food at the proper time, fall into the realm of the hungry ghosts. Such a person is struck by an illness in which food will not stay down and he is nauseated. When his nurses or friends prepare many kinds of food and earnestly show them to him, saying, 'This one is sweet, this one is sour, this one is delicious, this one is easy to digest,' the sick man feels nauseated and thinks, 'Will there ever come a time when my eyes do not have to see this food?' As soon as he dies at that time, he falls among the hungry ghosts.

"Those beings who are dull and deluded, who have no faith in the Three Jewels, and who denigrate the noble ones are born in the animal realm. When such a person is tormented by illness, he writhes and cannot lie down, cannot lie down even for a moment, and he does not want to hear pleasing talk of virtue. His nurse, knowing that he will certainly die, urges him, 'Listen to the Dharma! Take up the moral precepts! Look upon the sacred images and the Saṅgha, and make offerings to them!' But he has no faith and is displeased, and when he is urged insistently he grows angry and says, 'What harm would there be if I went somewhere I would never even hear the names of the Three Jewels and of virtue?' As soon as he dies, he is born in the animal realm."

"As for those who strive in virtue that is a cause for birth as gods and humans, they are not severely tormented even by illness, and when they die their minds are not disturbed. When their nurses and friends realize that they are dying, they urge them, saying, 'Do you wish to hear the sacred Dharma? Do you wish to see the sacred images? Do you wish to listen to sūtras or verses from the monks? Would you be glad to take up the moral precepts? Would you have the faith to offer your possessions to the Buddha and the Saṅgha?' If the person answers that he has faith in all of these, they speak further to the sick person: 'Moreover, if you make offerings to an image of the Buddha, you will attain awakening. If you venerate the Dharma and offer your possessions to it, then wherever you are born, in every place, you will be endowed with understanding, and you will grasp and realize the characteristics of the Dharma. If you make a gift of your possessions to the Saṅgha, then wherever you are born you will be endowed with jewels and great wealth, just as you wish.' The sick person is overjoyed, delighted and content, and makes the aspiration, 'In all my births may I meet the Three Jewels and hear the Dharma, and having heard it, may I grasp and realize it.' When he dies, he is born among humans.

"Whoever has developed vast virtue that becomes a cause for birth as a god, a person who practices generosity, whose moral discipline is pure, who delights in listening to the Dharma, and who practices the ten virtues, when he dies he lies at ease and sees images of the Buddha, the palaces of the gods and the daughters of the gods, and hears the music of the gods. His complexion is very fine, he raises his hands upward, and when he dies at that time he is born in the abode of the gods.

"The wife of the householder Sarphak was attached to her own body, so as soon as she died she was reborn as a snake in that very woman's body, and when she dies from that snake's body she will undergo the immeasurable sufferings of the great hells."

The monk asked the master again, "Who is that woman who eats her own flesh?"

Maudgalyāyana said, "In Śrāvastī there was a laywoman who had a maidservant. That laywoman invited a monk of pure moral discipline to receive her offerings for the three months of the summer rains. She made a dwelling for him in a secluded spot and housed him there, and every day she prepared all kinds of food with a hundred flavors and fine fragrance, and at the midday mealtime she sent the maidservant to bring it to him. When the maidservant had gone to a lonely place, she ate the delicious food and offered the leftovers to the monk. Later the laywoman saw that the maidservant had a fine complexion and had put on flesh, and she asked her, 'Have you not been making leftovers of the food offered to the monk?' The maidservant said, 'My lady, I too have at least a little faith in my heart. Since I hold no wrong views, why would I make leftovers of the food offered to the monk? I eat only the leftovers the monk has not finished. If I have eaten it, then wherever I am born, may I eat my own flesh!' Because of that cause and that condition, she first undergoes this terrible ripening, and then she will go on to undergo immeasurable suffering in the great hells."

The monk asked, "Who is that great tree, devoured by many worms, from which a great and dreadful sound resounds?"

He said, "That is the monk called Lita, who was the Saṅgha's administrator. He made use of the Saṅgha's property, ate its flowers and fruit, and gave its drinks to the side of the householders. Because of this, he undergoes this terrible ripening, and as soon as he dies he will be born in the great hells. The worms devouring the tree are the people who received what that monk gave them."

The monk asked again, "Who is that man surrounded by many people, struck with arrows until his body is one mass of flame?"

Maudgalyāyana said, "That was a man who went hunting and killed many wild animals, and so he has come to experience suffering like this. Then, when he passes from that life, he will be born in the great hells, and the time of his release from them will be very hard to reach."

The monk asked again, "Who is that who runs on the great mountain onto the points of swords and weapons?"

Maudgalyāyana said, "That was a very brave minister of a king, who would at times charge onto the points of sharp weapons, swords, spears and javelins. Then, when he passes from that life, he will undergo suffering for a long time in the great hells."

The monk asked again, "Whose is that great mountain of bones?"

Maudgalyāyana said, "If you wish to know, those are the bones of your own former bodies."

When the monk heard this, he was terrified. All his hair stood on end and sweat broke out on him, and he said to the master, "Before my heart bursts, please explain the causes and conditions of my past karma."

Maudgalyāyana said, "Birth and death in saṃsāra have no end and no limit, and good and evil karma do not perish and come to nothing, so one will certainly experience the ripening of whatever karma one has done.

"Long ago, in times past, here in Jambudvīpa there was a king called Dharmodgata. He delighted in generosity and moral discipline, was devoted to listening to the Dharma, had a mind of great compassion, was by nature not fierce, and did not take the lives of living beings. He bore the marks of a king and ruled according to the sacred Dharma. When he was about twenty years old, on one occasion, while he was playing chess with someone, a man committed a grave offense. The ministers asked him, 'A man has committed a grave offense against the law. What should be done?' The king was absorbed in the chess game, and so he ordered, 'Deal with it according to the law.' The ministers applied the law, it came to execution, and they killed the man. When the king had finished playing chess, he said to the ministers, 'Where is the man who committed the offense? I shall judge his case.' The ministers said, 'We applied the law and have put him to death.' When the king heard them say this, he fainted and fell to the ground. The ministers sprinkled him with water, he got up again, and, shedding tears, he said this:

"The palace grounds, the elephants, the horses, the seven precious things and all the rest will be left behind here, and I alone shall go on to undergo the sufferings of the hells. Even before I became king, kings ruled from this palace and dwelt in it, and if I die before long, others will likewise rule from it. Since I have killed a man, I am a king of ignoble kind. Where will I go in all my births? Now I no longer want to be king." Having said this, the king gave up the royal office and lived in the mountains, guarding himself.

When that king died, he was born as a sea monster in the great ocean, with a body seven hundred yojanas long. Whether king or minister, whoever gains power and might and takes the lives of living beings, or does deeds against others' wishes, or does deeds that harm living beings, is for the most part born as a sea monster as soon as he dies, and many worms live in its body. Because its body itches, it rubs and scrapes itself against a mountain of crystal, and the blood of the many worms that die turns the ocean red for about a hundred krośas. Through that evil karma, when those worms pass from that life, they fall into the great hells.

When that sea monster falls asleep, it wakes only once in a hundred years. Tormented by hunger and thirst, it opens its mouth, and the ocean water pouring into its mouth is like a great river flowing into the sea. At that time about five hundred merchants had set out on the ocean to fetch jewels, and they met the sea monster with its mouth gaping. Their ship sped very swiftly toward the mouth of the sea monster, which wanted to devour it. The merchants cried out loudly and wept, and said to one another, "We are certainly going to die. Let each of you call to mind whatever he relies upon." When this was said, some of them recited the names of the Buddha, the Dharma and the Saṅgha. Others called out the names of the gods of the world, of mountains and rivers, of asuras, of father and mother, wife, son, daughter, brothers and kinsfolk, and said, "After seeing it this last time today, we shall never see Jambudvīpa again."

Then, when they were on the very point of going into the sea monster's mouth, all of them at once cried out, "Homage to the Buddha!" As soon as the sea monster heard the sound of the words "Homage to the Buddha," it closed its mouth, the inflow of water was cut off, and those merchants were saved from death. The sea monster died right there of hunger and was born as a worm in Rājagṛha. The sea monster's corpse was cast up on the ocean shore, scorched by the sun and soaked by the rain, and when its flesh was gone, its bones became this mountain.

"Śrījāta, understand it this way: at that time, on that occasion, you were King Dharmodgata. Through the condition of having killed a man, you were born as a sea monster in the great ocean. Now that you have once again obtained a human body, if you do not turn your back on saṃsāra, then as soon as you pass from this life you will go to the hells, and it will be very hard to be freed."

The monk Śrījāta saw his own former body, and when he heard this explanation he was terrified of saṃsāra. He gradually brought to mind the Dharma he had been diligently practicing and settled his mind in one-pointedness. By seeing his former body he came to understand the truth of impermanence, realized what it is to turn one's back on saṃsāra, exhausted all the defilements of the afflictions, and became an arhat.

Maudgalyāyana too was overjoyed and said, "Son of the Dharma, you have accomplished everything to be done. Coming here before, you came through my power. Now you will go back by your own power." Having said this, Maudgalyāyana rose into the sky above, and the monk Śrījāta came along behind the master, like a fledgling following its parent bird, and they came to where the Bamboo Grove was.

At that time, since the young monks did not know that he was an arhat, they ridiculed Śrījāta as before, yet his deportment did not waver and he said nothing at all. The Blessed One, knowing how things stood, so that the young monks would not come to harm through this, and in order to make the elder monk's qualities known, told the monk Śrījāta in the midst of a great assembly, "Come here," and asked, "Did you go to the ocean shore?"

Śrījāta replied, "I went to the ocean shore."

"Tell what you saw on the ocean shore," the Blessed One said, and the monk related in detail everything he had seen. The Blessed One gave his approval, saying, "Excellent, excellent! Śrījāta, what you saw is true. Now that you have completely abandoned the suffering of saṃsāra and attained the bliss of nirvāṇa, you have become an object of veneration for the world with its gods and humans. Monk, you have fully completed all that was to be done."

When the young monks heard the Blessed One say this, they were filled with deep remorse and unhappiness, and they said, "Lacking wisdom, we have harmed and ridiculed such a learned and wise noble one. The ripening of this misdeed will come upon us." The young monks went to where the monk Śrījāta was, prostrated with the five points of the body touching the ground, and said this: "Holy beings are born together with compassion, and you, venerable one, were born together with great compassion. So, out of kindness toward us, we confess to you."

The monk replied, "I hold no displeasure even toward my own body. May you be purified."

Then the monk Śrījāta, recognizing that those young monks were deeply remorseful and had minds full of respect, taught them the sacred Dharma. Having heard the Dharma of turning one's back on saṃsāra, they practiced with diligence and effort, exhausted all the defilements of the afflictions, and became arhats.

Then the householder Śrījāta too became very famous in Rājagṛha. Everyone was amazed and said, "Even an old householder who knew nothing at all went forth in the Buddha's teaching, became an arhat, and teaches such sacred Dharma as this!" At that time most of the people living in Rājagṛha gave rise to minds of pure intention. Some gave permission for their sons, daughters, male servants, female servants and others to go forth, and some requested to go forth themselves. All of them were overjoyed and rejoiced.

Therefore the qualities of going forth are immeasurable and beyond counting. If even someone like Śrījāta, going forth at a hundred years of age, came to possess such great qualities, what need is there to speak of a young person in the prime of youth who goes forth finding the great, supreme and excellent fruit? Those who strive in the Dharma should go forth.

This is the fifteenth chapter, In Praise of the Qualities of Going Forth.

The fifth section.

Those who guard moral discipline would rather lose body and life than ever let their moral discipline decline. Why is that? Moral discipline is the root of entering awakening, the supreme undefiled thing, the path that leads in bliss to nirvāṇa. The merit of guarding moral discipline purely is immeasurable and beyond reckoning. Just as the great ocean is immeasurable and boundless, so too the merit of guarding moral discipline is immeasurable and boundless.

Just as the ocean is the abode of asuras, turtles, sea monsters and great creatures belonging to the class of water beings, so too is moral discipline: many great beings who abide in the three vehicles dwell in it.

Just as in the great ocean there are many jewels such as gold, silver and beryl, so too in the ocean of moral discipline there are many jewels: the ground of impermanence, the thirty-seven factors of awakening, meditative concentration, samādhi and the rest.

Just as the floor of the great ocean is vajra and its shore is encircled by a band of vajra, so that although the four great rivers flow into it, it neither diminishes nor overflows, so too is the ocean of moral discipline: the Vinaya, like vajra, forms its floor, the band of the Abhidharma encircles its shore, and although the four collections of sūtras flow into it like rivers, it neither increases nor diminishes.

If one asks why the ocean does not overflow, it is because the fire of the Avīci hell dries the ocean up; that is why the waters of the ocean do not overflow. Because the rivers flow into it constantly, it does not diminish. So too the ocean of moral discipline does not swell, because of heedfulness, and does not diminish, because it is endowed with good qualities. Therefore one should understand that the qualities of whoever guards moral discipline are exceedingly many.

After the Blessed One had passed into nirvāṇa, in a country called Anta there lived a monk who lived on almsfood, who delighted in dwelling in the wilderness and was endowed with fine deportment. A monk who lives on almsfood is praised by all the buddhas, and does not live among the crowd. Why is that? A monk who lives on almsfood has few desires and is content. He hoards nothing, eats a single meal a day, lives without a roof, keeps only the three robes, and practices the like. He is venerable; he is foremost. A monk living in the midst of the Saṅgha who has many desires, is not content, is miserly and is attached to everything does not win great renown.

That almsfood monk was endowed with good qualities and conduct. He had attained the fruit of a monk and abided in the six superknowledges, the three knowledges and the eight liberations, and because his deportment was beautiful in every respect, he became renowned everywhere.

At that time, in the country called Anta, there lived a layman endowed with good qualities, who had great faith in the Three Jewels and guarded the five precepts: not taking life, not taking what is not given, not engaging in sexual misconduct, not telling lies, and not drinking liquor, the intoxicating drink. That layman thought, "I will come to experience the merit of providing that almsfood monk with his sustenance for a whole lifetime." He thought further, "If I invite him to my house, he will be troubled on the road by cold and heat, and that will become an obstacle to his meditation; and later, when I experience the ripening, I would obtain it only by thinking about it and striving for it. If I deliver his sustenance to his dwelling, then later I will obtain the ripening as well without needing to strive for it." Having thought this, the layman, with a mind of faith, set out all kinds of food with a hundred flavors, fine fragrance and color, and had it delivered every day.

To judge whether monks are good or bad is difficult, in four ways, just as it is difficult to judge whether a mango is ripe or unripe. Some monks have very beautiful deportment, walk with slow steps and look no further than a yoke's length ahead, yet inwardly they are full of desire, anger and delusion, practice what is not Dharma, and have corrupted moral discipline. They are like a mango that is ripe outside but unripe inside. Some monks are coarse in outward deportment and lack fine deportment, yet inwardly they possess the conduct and qualities of a renunciant and are endowed with meditative concentration, samādhi and wisdom. They are like a mango that is ripe inside but unripe outside. Some monks are coarse in deportment, commit misdeeds and have corrupted moral discipline, and inwardly too they are full of desire, anger, delusion, miserliness and jealousy. They are like a mango that is unripe both outside and inside. Some monks have beautiful deportment and are endowed with moral discipline, and inwardly too they possess the qualities of a renunciant, moral discipline, samādhi and wisdom. They are like a mango that is ripe both outside and inside. The almsfood monk, endowed with both the outer and the inner, was like this; because his qualities and conduct were complete, everyone served and honored him.

At that time in that country there was a householder with great faith in the Three Jewels who had a young son. He thought, "Let this son of mine go forth, and I shall seek out a learned teacher for him." Why is that? Because by relying on a virtuous friend, virtue increases.

"Relying on an evil companion gives rise to evil qualities. For example, although the nature of the wind does not change, if it comes from a grove of sandalwood or a grove of campaka trees, that wind carries a sweet fragrance. If it comes from the direction of a putrid, filthy charnel ground, that wind too is foul. Likewise, if a new garment is put into a vessel of incense, the garment too smells of incense; if it is put among foul, rotting things, the garment too becomes foul. If one relies on a virtuous friend, virtue grows and increases; if one relies on an evil companion, evil arises and increases. Therefore I shall offer this son of mine to this venerable monk, and let him go forth."

Having thought this, he went to where the monk was and said this: "I have faith that this son of mine should go forth. Venerable one, please consider him with kindness and let him go forth. If you do not permit it, I will take him back home again."

Then the monk examined with the eye of miraculous power and saw that if this boy went forth, he would guard moral discipline purely and the Buddha's Dharma would flourish. So he let him go forth and made him a novice.

At that time the layman who provided the monk's sustenance had a householder friend. That householder prepared a seasonal feast and invited the layman, his wife, his sons, his daughters, his male servants, his female servants and all the people of his household. The next day, when they were to go as guests, the layman thought, "If all of us go to be guests, who will guard this house? If I insist on leaving someone behind, he will miss the occasion and lose his share." So he said, "Whoever is willing to stay, I will repay the kindness and reward him as soon as we return from the feast."

The layman had a daughter, and she said this to her parents: "Let all the people of the household go to be guests; I will guard the house."

Her father said, "Excellent, excellent! If you guard the house, you are no different from your mother, and I have no fear that anything will be lost or that any mishap will occur." Then all the people of the household went off to be guests, and the daughter barred the doors securely and stayed alone in the house.

That day the layman was so busy that he forgot to send the almsfood. Then the monk thought, "The sun is nearly past noon. The householder has many affairs and may perhaps have forgotten. It is not right to go without eating; I shall send for it." So he sent the novice to fetch the almsfood.

The master instructed the novice, "Restrain your deportment well, and receive almsfood in the village as the Blessed One taught. Just as bees do no harm to the color and fragrance of flowers, receive almsfood from the house without becoming attached. Guard your senses, without attachment to forms, sounds, smells, tastes and textures. If you guard moral discipline, you will certainly attain the fruit. Someone like Devadatta, though he could recite many sūtras from memory, did wrong, corrupted his moral discipline, and was born in the Avīci hell. Kokālika, having slandered others and corrupted his moral discipline, was likewise born in hell. Someone like Cūḍapanthaka, though he knew no more than a single verse, became an arhat because he guarded moral discipline. Moral discipline leads in through the gate to nirvāṇa and becomes the cause of supreme bliss.

"For example, in the brahmins' religion a feast is held for three or four months, and to it they invite learned brahmins who are endowed with knowledge, guard moral discipline and practice pure conduct, but not everyone. Whoever comes to the feast has a seal stamped in keule on his wrist. There was a brahmin who had good qualities but was greedy by nature, and because he was attached to honey and sweet things, he licked off the seal that had been stamped on his wrist until it could no longer be seen. The next day, when they gathered for the feast and the gatekeeper checked the seals, that wandering ascetic had none, so the gatekeeper would not let him in. When asked, 'Where is your seal?' he said, 'It was sweet, so I licked it off.' In this way, because that wandering ascetic was attached to a tiny bit of sweetness, he lost the four months' feast, with its food of fine color and fragrance, its many kinds of sweet scents, and its many precious benefits.

"You too, do not let the seal of moral discipline be broken for the sake of attachment to some trifle like that. Otherwise you would lose the delicious taste of the five sense pleasures of gods and humans, the undefiled state, the thirty-seven factors of awakening, the bliss of nirvāṇa, and the immeasurable jewels of the Dharma. Do not break the moral discipline of the buddhas of the three times, and do not disgrace the Three Jewels, your master, or the lineage of the Saṅgha."

When he had been instructed in this way, the novice bowed his head at his master's feet and went to the layman's house. When he arrived there, he knocked on the door, and the girl called out, "Who is it?"

The novice said, "I have come to receive my master's almsfood."

The girl was overjoyed, thinking, "Now my hope is fulfilled," and she opened the door. When the novice had come inside, the girl, who was no more than sixteen years old, finely featured, lovely to look at and beautiful, thought, "I will seduce him with desire." She made many kinds of enticements in front of the novice and, burning with desire, pressed herself upon him.

The novice thought, "Has a wind disorder arisen in this girl, or is she mad? Or else is this girl tormented by desire, and is she going to burn up my moral discipline with the fire of desire?" Having thought this, he kept his deportment tightly restrained and remained there without even his complexion changing.

The girl prostrated with the five points of the body touching the ground and said this to the novice: "What I have always hoped for has come about today. There is something I have to tell you; I have always kept it in mind, but I never found the chance."

"If you too have always been thinking of me, then please fulfill my hope. Inside this house there are jewels, gold, silver, and many storehouses, like the treasure of Vaiśravaṇa, yet there is no master. Please become master of the house and all it holds. I will be your servant and attend you with respect and service. Do not go against this; please fulfill my hope."

The novice thought, "What evil deed have I done, that I have met with such a circumstance as this? Better that I lose my body and my life than break the moral discipline laid down by the buddhas of the three times. In former times, monks who had gone into a prostitute's house would rather leap into a pit of fire than break their moral discipline. Again, monks who had been robbed by bandits and tied up with grass, though they were buffeted by the wind, scorched by the sun, and eaten by many insects, would not cut the grass and leave, in order to keep their moral discipline. A monk who saw that a goose had made a jewel disappear endured suffering, yet would not say a word, in order to keep his moral discipline. When a ship was wrecked, a monk clung to a plank, yet in order to keep his moral discipline he gave that plank to a fellow monk senior in rank, and died in the ocean. Were those alone the ones who kept moral discipline as close disciples of the Buddha, and not we? Is the Tathāgata the Teacher of those alone, and not our Teacher? For example, when campaka flowers are pressed together with sesame, the sweet fragrance of the campaka comes to pervade the sesame oil. Am I not the same? Since I am fully held in the care of a spiritual friend, why should I now commit evil, unwholesome deeds? Better that I lose my body and my life. Never shall I break my moral discipline and bring disgrace upon the Buddha, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha, upon my parents, my teacher, and the line of my masters."

He thought further, "If I run away, this girl, tormented by desire and without any shame or embarrassment, will catch me outside and slander me, and if the villagers see it, they will denounce me. I shall surely give up my life in this very house." Having thought this, he said to the girl, "Shut the doors tightly. I have a small task to do inside a room; when I have done it, I will meet with you."

When he had told her this and the girl had gone out to shut the door, the novice went into a room and bolted the door tightly. Finding a razor inside, he was overjoyed. He took off the robes he wore on his body and hung them on the clothes rail. Joining his palms and facing toward the place in the city of Kuśinagara where the Buddha passed into nirvāṇa, he knelt with his knees on the ground and made this vow: "I do not abandon the Buddha, the Dharma, and the Saṅgha. I do not abandon my preceptor and my teacher. I do not abandon moral discipline. It is in order to keep moral discipline that I abandon this body. Wherever I am born, may I go forth in the teaching of the Buddha, may I lead a perfectly pure holy life, may I exhaust the defilements, and may I attain the fruit." Having made this aspiration, he cut his own throat and died. He was covered in blood, and his body too turned red with blood.

At that time the girl thought, "He is taking a long time," and she went to the door and called, but he said nothing at all. When the girl opened the door and looked, she saw that he was already dead. Since things had not turned out as she intended, her desirous mind subsided. Tormented by great unhappiness, the girl tore her hair, scratched her face with her nails, and rolled on the ground, weeping.

When the girl's parents came back from the feast, they knocked on the door and called, but the girl said nothing at all, so they sent a man inside to open the door. When they saw the girl in such a state, they said to her, "What is wrong with you? Has someone made sport of you and brought disgrace upon your family?" When they asked this, the girl said nothing, but thought, "If I speak honestly, it will be deeply shameful. But if I say that the novice made sport of me, then, by slandering a noble one, a being falls into hell and comes to experience much suffering. I shall speak honestly, without deceit." Having thought this, she said, "While I was here alone guarding the house, that novice came to collect his teacher's alms. Burning with the fire of my desire, I pressed the novice to destroy the moral discipline he had kept, but his mind could not be changed. The novice found a way, went into a room, and gave up his life. With this impure body of mine I have defiled a perfectly pure vessel, and on top of that I have committed a grave wrong. That is why I am unhappy."

When she had said this, the girl's parents, hearing it, were amazed. Why was that? Because they understood that this is how things are by the very nature of affliction. They told the girl, "All phenomena are impermanent; do not grieve." Then they went into the room and looked, and seeing the novice's remains stained with blood like red sandalwood, they prostrated and praised him, saying, "Excellent! In order to keep the moral discipline of the Buddha, you did not regard even your body and your life!"

At that time, by the king's law of the land, if a monk died in a householder's house, a thousand gold coins had to be offered to the court. So that layman took a thousand gold coins and went to the palace, and petitioned, "Great king, a fault has occurred on my part. Please accept these coins." The king said to him, "In my realm, the one who has faith in the Three Jewels, who has devotion for the true Dharma and so is upright by nature, and whose conduct is well disciplined, is you alone. What fault could have occurred on your part?" The layman reproached his daughter and praised the virtue of how the novice had kept his moral discipline, and reported in full everything that had happened.

The king said, "The novice simply disregarded his body and his life in order to keep his moral discipline. You have committed no fault, so take the coins and go back home. I too will come to your house to make an offering to that novice." Having said this, he had the golden drum beaten and gave the command: "Let the whole retinue assemble and come along in attendance. We are going to the house of that layman."

Then the king went to that house. Seeing the novice's remains stained with blood like red sandalwood, he prostrated and praised his virtues. The remains were placed on a chariot adorned with all kinds of jewels and taken to a level, pleasant place where everyone could see, and there they were cremated with fragrant wood and offerings were made.

That girl, moreover, was exceedingly beautiful, of a beauty rare in the world. The king had the girl too placed on a high spot where everyone could see her, and let the whole great assembly have a good look at her. Then he declared to the many people, "Who that is not free of desire would not become attached to a girl as fair of form, as lovely and as beautiful as this? Yet this novice, who had not even attained the fruit, and who had a body subject to birth and destruction, dared to give up his body and his life for the sake of moral discipline. That is a great wonder!" Having proclaimed this, he invited the novice's teacher as well and had him teach the true Dharma to the great assembly. When all who had gathered in that assembly heard the Dharma taught, some asked to go forth and keep moral discipline, and some generated the mind set on unsurpassed awakening. All of them rejoiced and were delighted.

This is the sixteenth chapter, on the novice who kept his moral discipline.

At that time the Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada, together with a great saṅgha of monks, and was teaching the Dharma.

At that time in that land there was a great householder, rich and very wealthy, with gold, silver, the seven kinds of precious things, elephants, horses, cattle, sheep, male and female slaves, a great many workers, and a great many storehouses. He had no son, but he had five daughters, all of them lovely in appearance and endowed with intelligence. At that time, while the householder's wife was pregnant, the householder died.

At that time, under the king's law of the land, when there was no son, the king took all the wealth there was. The king accordingly ordered a minister to count all the man's property and livestock and whatever goods there were, and to take them for the crown. The daughters thought, "Whether what is in our mother's womb is male or female is not yet certain. If she gives birth to a girl, the king will simply take the property and livestock. But if a boy is born, he is the rightful heir." Having thought this, they petitioned the king: "Since our father has died and there is no son, the king is taking the property and livestock. Please leave it be for now, until what is in our mother's womb is known for certain. If it is a girl, it will be easy to take it then; but if it is a boy, he is the rightful heir." King Prasenajit, being upright and governing by the Dharma, did as the daughters requested.

Not long after that, when the nine months were complete, a boy was born. He had no ears, no eyes, no nose, no tongue, and no hands or feet; he was just a lump of flesh with only a male organ. So he was given the name Majibila. Then the daughters reported to the king that a boy had been born. The king thought, "It is not by having eyes, ears, nose, tongue, hands, feet and so forth that one becomes an heir; whoever has a male organ becomes the heir. This son too has a male organ, so he becomes his father's heir." Having thought this, he told the daughters, "All the property, livestock and goods belong to your brother. I will not take them."

At that time the eldest daughter was given in marriage to a man of matching family and lineage. That daughter showed respect to her husband: she spread his seat, made his bed, swept up, prepared his food, greeted him and saw him off, bowed to him, spoke gently to him and so on. In every task she was to do, she honored her husband just as servant women do the work of their mistress. So the husband said to his wife, "Being husband and wife is not something for us alone; it is the same for everyone. Why is it that you alone behave like this?"

The wife said, "My father had countless possessions and livestock. After he died, though there were five daughters, it was decided that the king would take all the wealth. But when my mother's months were complete, a brother was born from her womb. He had no eyes, no ears, nose, tongue, feet or hands; yet because he had only a male organ, he became the heir. Therefore, whatever faculties one may have, they cannot match the single mark of a male. That is why I honor you."

When the householder heard what his wife said, he was amazed. Together with his wife he went to where the Blessed One was, and when he arrived he said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, why is it that this householder's son, who has no eyes, ears, nose, tongue, hands or feet, became the master of the wealth in a very rich household as soon as he was born?"

The Blessed One said to the householder, "It is good that you have asked about these things. Listen very well and keep it in mind, and I will explain it to you." The householder replied, "I shall do so."

The Blessed One said, "In times long past, a householder had two sons. One was called Generosity and the other was called Moral Discipline. From the time they were small, the two brothers had a strong sense of conscience and shame, spoke the truth, always delighted in giving, and were protectors of the poor and destitute. Because they had such great faith and were so virtuous, the people of the land praised them, and the king appointed them as judges, to examine who was guilty and who was not."

At that time, under the king's law of the land, whenever one of the people lent something to another, they would come before the judge and appoint him as witness, and no other proof was required.

At that time a merchant who was going to sea borrowed a great deal of wealth from the judge's younger brother, called Moral Discipline, and used it for whatever he needed. Moral Discipline had an only son, who was still young and small. Moral Discipline took the son with him, and taking along the goods that were to be lent, went to his elder brother and said, "This merchant is borrowing this much wealth from me as a loan, and has undertaken to repay it whenever he comes back from the sea. Should I die, he has undertaken to repay it to my son. Elder brother, please take note of this." The elder brother said, "I shall do so."

Not long after that, the householder Moral Discipline died. The merchant's ship was wrecked at sea, and the merchant alone clung to a plank and made his way back to his own country. At that time the householder's son thought, "The merchant's ship was wrecked, his wealth was lost, and he has come back alone. Though he owes me this much in goods, he has nothing."

Then the merchant again borrowed from others, set out to sea once more, found many precious jewels, and came safely back home. He thought, "This householder's son did not press me for the debt before. Since he was very young, has he perhaps forgotten and does not remember? Or did he not press me for the debt because I had nothing? I will test him." Having thought this, he mounted a fine horse, put on costly, splendid clothes, and went to the market.

The householder's son saw the merchant wearing fine clothes and riding a fine horse, and thought, "This man has become prosperous; I will press him for the debt." Having thought this, he sent a man with the message, "Will you not now repay what you owe?" The merchant replied, "I shall do so."

Then the merchant thought, "I borrowed a great deal of wealth from this householder, so even if I hand over everything I have, it will not be enough to repay it. I shall devise a scheme." Having thought this, the merchant took a precious jewel and went to the judge's wife and said to her, "My lady, please understand. Some time ago I borrowed a little wealth from the householder Moral Discipline, and now the householder's son is pressing me for the debt. Please accept this precious jewel worth ten thousand taels, and ask the judge that when the two of us come to put the question to him, he say, 'I did not see it.'"

The wife said, "This husband of mine is extremely upright and does not lie, so it will certainly not work. Still, I will ask him." She took the jewel, and when her husband came back in the evening she told him in full what the merchant had asked.

The husband said, "I live by conscience and shame. Because I do not lie, the king made me a judge. If I were to tell a lie, I would no longer be of true lineage."

The next day the merchant came again, and the wife told him the whole of what had been said and gave the jewel back. The merchant then offered another precious jewel, worth twenty thousand taels, and said, "By saying a single word over a small matter, you will gain thirty thousand taels. The other party is a mere nobody; even if he were to become king, he would not get so much as a single coin."

She thought, "This is reasonable," and out of attachment to the precious jewels she agreed to carry out what the merchant had entrusted to her. In the evening, when her husband came back, she said, "If what I asked you yesterday seems acceptable, please do it." The husband said, "That is not right. It is because I do not lie that I was made a judge. If I were to tell a lie, in this life no one in the world would trust me, and in later lives too I would experience suffering for countless eons." So he told her.

At that time that householder had a son who could not walk, who could only crawl. The householder's wife said to the householder, "Since I became your wife, I would not expect you to refuse me even if I asked something that put your life at stake. Yet if you twist your words and turn me down over a small matter like this, I cannot go on living. If things do not go as I have asked, I will kill our son, and after that I will die too."

When the husband heard her say this, he thought, "Like a man who is choking, who can neither spit it out nor swallow it down: I have only this one son, and if he dies there will be no heir to the wealth. Yet if I do as the woman says, everyone in the world will talk and will not trust me, and in the future I will experience much suffering." Having thought this, he unwillingly agreed to do as his wife said. The wife was overjoyed and said to the merchant, "My husband has given his consent. Go ahead with it."

The merchant too was overjoyed and went back home. He adorned a great elephant with all kinds of jewels, put on costly clothes, mounted the elephant, and came to the market. When the householder's son saw this he was pleased, thinking, "Now this man has great wealth; I will press him for the debt." Having thought this, he went up to him, stepped forward, and said, "Householder, do you recall? Pay me the debts you owe." The householder pretended to be startled and said, "I do not remember owing you any debt. What debt have you seen me owe?"

The householder's son said, "In such and such a year and month you borrowed wealth from my father, and you took the wealth from my own hand; the judge knows it too. Why do you deny it?" The merchant said, "I do not remember borrowing any wealth of yours. If there is a witness, let us go before the witness and settle it." So they went before the judge, and the son said, "This man once borrowed this much wealth from my father, and you, uncle, saw it. Did this man not borrow wealth from my father?" The uncle said, "I am not certain." The nephew, startled, said, "The wealth he borrowed from me at that time: do you not remember, uncle, that you handed it over to this man with your own hand?" The uncle said, "There was no such thing."

Then the boy grew angry with his uncle and said, "Because you were upright, the king made you judge over everyone, and all the people of the land call you upright. If you treat even me, your own nephew, like this, what need is there to speak of others being wronged and cut off from a fair hearing? Whether this is true or false will become plain in time."

The Blessed One said to the householder, "If you wish to know who that judge was at that time, do not think it was anyone else. He is this Majibila, whose faculties are incomplete. Because he told a lie at that time, he was born in a great hell and experienced all kinds of suffering. When he was freed from hell, for five hundred lifetimes he was born as a lump of flesh with no faculties at all. Yet because at that time he delighted in giving, he was always born into wealthy households and became master of the wealth. Wholesome deeds and evil deeds, even after a long time has passed, do not vanish or perish. Therefore, all of you, be diligent, restrain body, speech and mind, and do no evil."

When the great assembly heard what the Blessed One had said, some attained anything from the first fruit to the fourth fruit, and some generated the mind set on unsurpassed awakening. They rejoiced and were delighted.

This is the seventeenth chapter, on the householder without faculties.

At that time the Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada, teaching the Dharma to the saṅgha of monks.

At that time in that land the wife of a householder gave birth to a daughter who surpassed other girls, fair of face, lovely to behold, and beautiful. When she was first born, she came out wrapped in soft white cloth. Her parents were amazed at this and showed her to a reader of signs. The reader of signs examined her and saw that she was endowed with great merit, and she was named Śuklā, the White One. As the girl Śuklā grew, the cloth also grew to the size of her body.

Later, when the girl had grown up, many people of the land asked for her as a wife. Her parents, intending to have the ornaments for sending their daughter off as a bride beaten out, engaged a smith and weighed out gold and silver. When the girl saw the gold and silver being weighed, she asked her parents, "What is this for?" They said, "This is to beat out the ornaments for sending you off as a bride." The girl said, "I have faith in going forth; I do not wish to go off as a bride."

Her parents, going along with their daughter's wishes, at once brought out cloth and set about sewing Dharma robes. The girl asked, "What is this cloth for?" They said, "We are sewing your Dharma robes." The girl said, "I already have Dharma robes to wear; there is no need to sew them. Take me to where the Blessed One is staying." Her parents said, "We will take you to where the Blessed One is staying," and they took her into the Blessed One's presence. When they arrived where the Buddha was, she bowed her head to the Blessed One and asked to go forth. The Blessed One said, "Welcome!" At that, her hair and the down on her face fell away of themselves, and the white cloth she wore on her body turned into the five robes. She was entrusted to the nun Prajāpatī. That nun applied herself with diligence and before long became an arhat.

Ānanda asked the Blessed One, "What merit did the nun Śuklā accumulate in the past, that she was born into a householder's family, had cloth on her body, and, after going forth, before long became an arhat?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "In times long past, a buddha called Merit Protector appeared in the world. When he went about with his attendants for the benefit of beings, all the people of the land made abundant offerings. At that time a certain monk, for the benefit of many beings, urged all the people of the land to listen to the Dharma from the Buddha and to make gifts.

"At that time this woman was called Ke, and she was utterly poor. She and her husband had only a single length of cloth between them for clothing. When the husband went out to seek a living, he wore the cloth, and the wife stayed naked inside the grass. When the wife went out to seek a living, she wore the cloth, and the husband stayed naked in the heap of grass.

"Later the monk who worked for the benefit of all came to their door and met the woman. He said, 'Go and pay homage to the Buddha. Make an offering.' He praised the virtues of giving and explained the karmic ripening of stinginess. He spoke at length about how rare it is for a buddha to appear in the world, how rare it is to hear the Dharma, and how rare it is to obtain a human body, and he urged her, 'Go and listen to the Dharma.' The woman said, 'Reverend, wait a little; I shall come back.' She went inside the house and said to her husband, 'At the door there is a monk who tells us, "Go and pay homage to the Buddha, listen to the Dharma, and make gifts." Because we did not give in our former lives, we have become poor like this. What shall we make into provisions for our next life now?'

"The husband said, 'It would be fitting to give to him, but we are poor and have nothing. What could we give?' The wife said, 'Because we did not give in our former lives, we have become poor like this. If we do not give now as well, where will we go in our next life? I am going to make a gift; please allow it.' The husband thought, 'She seems to have some hidden wealth,' and said, 'Make a gift.' Then the wife said, 'I wish to give this cloth.'

"The husband said, 'We two, husband and wife, have nothing but this cloth. With it we go out to seek a living and keep ourselves alive. If we give it away, are we both just to sit at home?' The wife said, 'For anyone who is born there is death. Even if we do not give this, we will die; and if we give it, we will die. If we are content to give and then die, there is hope for the next life. So it is better to die having given. You too, gladly let the gift be made.' Having told him this, the wife went outside and said to the monk, 'Reverend, please look away for a moment. I am going to make a gift.'"

"When she had said this, the monk told her, 'If you are going to make a gift, give it with your own hand, in person. I shall recite the verses of dedication for the gift.' The woman said, 'This cloth is all I have on my body. If I come out, I have no other clothing, and it is not fitting that you should see this filthy, stained body of a woman. I shall offer it from inside.' So she took off the cloth inside and offered it to the monk. The monk recited the verses of dedication, took the cloth, and went to where the Buddha was.

"When he arrived, the Blessed One said to the monk, 'Bring the cloth that the woman offered.' The monk offered the cloth to the Buddha, and the Blessed One accepted it with his own hand. Because the cloth was old and soiled with dirt, the king's retinue scoffed at his taking soiled cloth into his hand. The Blessed One, knowing the thoughts of the great assembly, said, 'As I see it, in this whole assembly there is no gift purer than this.' The great assembly was amazed.

"Then the king's queen was overjoyed. She took off the clothes and ornaments she wore on her body and sent them to the woman. The king too took off his clothes and ornaments and sent them to her husband, with the command, 'Let the husband and wife both come into the assembly.' The buddha Merit Protector taught the Dharma to the great assembly, and most of them attained liberation."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "The poor woman at that time was this nun Śuklā. Because at that time she gave the cloth with sincere intention, for ninety-one eons, wherever she was born, she was always born together with cloth, never in want, and prosperous just as she wished. Because she heard the true Dharma from that buddha and made the aspiration to be liberated, she has met me here and become an arhat. Therefore you too should be diligent in listening to the Dharma and in giving." When the Blessed One had spoken thus, the great assembly rejoiced at what he had said and were delighted.

This is the eighteenth chapter, on the poor woman who offered cloth.

At that time the venerable Mahākātyāyana was staying in the land of Avanti. In that land a wealthy householder, stingy and without compassion, had an old slave woman. Though the slave woman did all the menial work, she had no clothing to cover her body and never tasted food in her mouth, and she was constantly beaten and abused. The old woman could bear it no longer, and thinking, "I will go and throw away my life," she took a pitcher and went to fetch water, and on the bank of the water she cried out loudly and wept.

Kātyāyana came there and asked, "Old woman, why are you so unhappy?" "Noble one, on top of being old, my work is heavy, and I have nothing to wear and nothing to eat. I am so poor and destitute that I wonder why I do not die, and so I am unhappy and weep." Kātyāyana said, "Old woman, why do you not sell your poverty?" The old woman said, "How can poverty be sold? Who would buy poverty?" Kātyāyana told her three times, "Poverty can be sold." Thinking, "I will ask how this poverty can be sold," she asked, "How can poverty be sold?" Kātyāyana said, "If you truly wish to sell your poverty, do as I instruct you." She replied, "Noble one, I will do so."

Kātyāyana said, "First bathe, and after you have washed, make a gift." The old woman said, "I do not have even the makings of a patch; what could I give? This pitcher belongs to my mistress; I have no right to it." Kātyāyana said, "Fill this alms bowl with clean water and offer it to me. I shall recite the verses of dedication for the gift." Kātyāyana took the water, recited the verses of dedication, gave her the precepts, had her recite the Buddha's name, and praised his many kinds of virtues. Then he asked, "Old woman, do you have a place to stay?" The old woman said, "I have no place to stay. I sleep right where I grind grain, pound with the pestle, and do my work. Sometimes I sleep on the dung heap."

Kātyāyana said, "Do not feel regret or unhappiness at doing menial work; do your work diligently. After your mistress has gone to sleep, quietly open your mistress's door, spread clean grass in a clean corner of the doorway or the passage, and sit there. Without thinking of anything else, keep the Buddha in mind and recollect him."

Then the old woman did as she had been taught, and before long, one night while she was sitting there, she died and was born as a god in the Heaven of the Thirty-Three. Her mistress rose early in the morning, and seeing that the slave woman had died inside the house, she grew angry and cursed, "If this old woman was not allowed inside the house, why has she died here?" She tied a grass rope to her feet, dragged her off, and had her taken to the charnel ground.
