At that time there was a son of the gods who was surrounded by five hundred daughters of the gods and who delighted in play. A goddess had passed away, and the old woman was born in that goddess's place. Among those born as gods, those of sharp faculties know through what condition they have been born as gods, while those of dull faculties do not know. The old woman did not know why she had been born a god, and was simply attached to pleasure.

At that time Śāriputra, who was staying in the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, asked her, "Do you know through what condition you were born as a god?" She said, "I do not know." Śāriputra had her look with the divine eye, and made her see how she had become a god. Then she, together with some five hundred daughters of the gods, came down to the charnel ground, strewed divine flowers over the bones, and made offerings of incense. The light of the gods lit up the whole city with a great radiance. Her mistress and the others were astonished, and many people went to look. Seeing many gods making offerings to the old woman's bones, they said to the gods, "Why are you making offerings to these bones? Even when she was alive, everyone shunned her because she was filthy; how much more so the bones of one who has died!"

Then the daughters of the gods told at length how she had been reborn as a god, and went to where Kātyāyana was. Kātyāyana gave those daughters of the gods an extensive talk on the sublime Dharma, on generosity, moral discipline, rebirth in the higher realms, and so on, and taught them the teaching on impurity and the teaching on renunciation. Thereupon those daughters of the gods became free of dust and free of stain, their Dharma eye with regard to phenomena became pure, and they went back to the abode of the gods. The great assembly, too, having heard the Dharma taught, each attained the fruits, from the first fruit up to the fourth; they rejoiced and were delighted.

This is the nineteenth chapter, on the poor servant woman who sold her poverty to Kātyāyana.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time there lived in that land a householder who was very rich, of great wealth, his storehouses filled with jewels. He took a wife from a family of equal standing, and his wife bore a son whose color was like gold. A reader of marks examined him and gave him the name Golden God.

When the boy was born, a well appeared of itself in the boy's house, its water square and level, eight cubits across, and eight cubits deep as well. When water was drawn from that well, things came forth according to people's wishes: for one who wanted clothes, clothes came forth; for one who wanted food and drink, food and drink came forth; for one who wanted gold, silver, and jewels, gold, silver, and jewels came forth. Whatever anyone wanted, all of it came forth just as they wished.

The boy was nurtured and grew up, and he mastered the fields of knowledge. The householder was overjoyed and thought, "I will look for a wife for the boy." Intending to find for him a wife who, like the boy, was golden in color, fine and beautiful, surpassing the ordinary, he searched every land for a bride.

At that time, in the land of Campā, a very rich householder had a daughter called Golden Light. That girl too was golden in color and most radiant, her skin very fine and her bones very slender. When she was first born, a well had likewise appeared of itself at her home, eight cubits across and the same in depth, and from that well too there came forth of themselves whatever things were wished for: jewels of every kind, clothes, food, drink, and so on. That householder too thought, "This fine daughter of mine I will give to a young man like her, whose body is golden in color, most radiant, fine, and beautiful." The two who had these thoughts met; the boy heard about the girl and the girl heard about the boy, and the two householders agreed to make a marriage alliance.

Having agreed to hold the wedding feast, they brought the girl to Śrāvastī, to the house of Golden God. Golden God invited the Buddha and the Saṅgha, and the Blessed One came with the Saṅgha for the meal. He taught the sublime Dharma at length to Golden God and his wife, and Golden God and his wife, together with the householder and his wife, having heard the Dharma from the Blessed One, had all their blazing evil and non-virtue of two hundred thousand eons pacified. With purified minds they realized the Dharma and attained the fruit of stream-entry.

Then the Blessed One returned to the monastery, and Golden God and Golden Light, husband and wife, asked their parents for leave to go forth, and their parents granted it. The two of them went to where the Buddha was, and when they arrived they bowed their heads at the Buddha's feet and said, "We two wish to go forth." The Blessed One said, "Welcome!" Their hair and beards fell away of themselves, and they became monastics wearing saffron robes. Golden God entered the Saṅgha of monks, and Golden Light was entrusted to Prajāpatī. Before long both became arhats and came to possess every quality: the three knowledges, the six superknowledges, the eight liberations, and so on.

Then the monks were filled with doubt, and the venerable Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, what deeds did the monks Golden God and Golden Light do in the past, that from the moment they were first born they possessed such jewels and great wealth, and even from a well all their hopes were completely fulfilled?"

The Blessed One said, "Ninety-one eons ago, in the past, a buddha called Vipaśyin came into the world. After he passed into nirvāṇa, the monks who had entered his teaching wandered through the towns and the countryside working for the welfare of sentient beings, and all the householders of great and high families supplied them with robes and alms food without fail.

"At that time a poor householder and his wife thought, 'When our parents were alive, we had jewels and wealth, and our storehouses were completely full, yet we never met the noble Saṅgha. Now that we are destitute like this and own nothing at all, sleeping on a heap of straw and possessing not even a measure or a handful of grain, the noble Saṅgha has come, and all the rich householders are offering robes and alms food, while we have nothing at all to offer.' Thinking this, they were deeply unhappy and wept.

"The tears of the weeping husband fell onto his wife's hand, and the wife said to her husband, 'Why are you weeping?'

"'Do you not know? The noble Saṅgha has come to this town, and all the rich householders are making offerings to them, while we alone have nothing at all to offer. If we make no offering now, where will we go in our next life? That is why I am unhappy.'

"The wife said, 'Go and search in our old storehouse. It may be that a little something is left. If you find a few things, we will offer them.' When he searched the old storehouse, he found a single gold coin left there. The wife, for her part, had one mirror. The husband and wife bought a new vase, filled the vase with clean water, put the one gold coin inside it, covered its mouth with the mirror, and, carrying the water vase, offered it to the Saṅgha.

The Saṅgha accepted it. Some of them drank that water, some washed their hands and faces, and some washed their alms bowls. When the householder and his wife saw the Saṅgha use it in this way, they were overjoyed and delighted, and went back home. Not long afterward, struck by illness, husband and wife both died and were reborn as gods in the Heaven of the Thirty-Three.

"Ānanda, the poor husband and wife who at that time offered the water vase are Golden God and his wife. Because long ago they offered a vase full of water, one gold coin, and one mirror, in all their births they have been fine and beautiful, their complexion always radiant with the color of gold. So it has been for ninety-one eons. Because at that time they made the offering with a mind of the deepest faith, now in this life they have reached the end of saṃsāra and become arhats. Therefore, Ānanda, there is no deed belonging to the side of merit that one may leave undone. By giving a little, that poor man gained a measureless heap of merit such as this."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, Ānanda and the great assembly resolved to gather the accumulation of merit that comes from giving; they rejoiced and were delighted.

This is the twentieth chapter, on Golden God.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time in that land a rich householder took a wife from a family of equal standing, but because he had no son he was very unhappy, and he prayed to all the gods. After some time his wife became pregnant, and when her months were complete she bore a son. The boy was most beautiful and lovely, and his parents, relatives, and friends were all delighted and held a birth feast. They gathered on the bank of a river and ate the birth feast there.

While they were eating the birth feast on the riverbank, everyone wanted to look at the boy, so one person took him on his lap and then gave him onto another's lap. As he was passed in this way from one to the next, someone was careless, the boy slipped, fell into the water, and was carried off by the river. Through the power of the boy's merit, since his karma was not yet exhausted, he did not die: a fish swallowed him, and he stayed alive in the fish's belly.

At the lower end of that river there was another village, and in that village too there was a rich householder who had no son. A servant of that householder was fishing on the bank of the river and caught the very fish that held the child. When he cut open its belly, a boy came out of the belly, and the servant took him and offered him to his mistress. The householder's wife took the boy and looked at him, and saw that he was a fine boy endowed with the marks. She was overjoyed, thinking, "This is the sign that our prayers to all the gods have been answered," and she gave him to a nurse to rear.

Then the householder of the upper village heard that the householder of the lower village had found a son inside a fish. Thinking, "Without a doubt this is my son who fell into the water," he went to him and said, "This is my son, so give him back!"

The other replied, "This is not your son. Because I had no son, I prayed to all the gods, and this is a son given by the gods. I found him in the belly of a fish."

The two householders quarreled and appealed to the king for judgment. The boy's father said, "He is my son; he is the one who fell into the water at the riverbank." The other householder said, "I found the boy in the belly of a fish I caught; he is not your son." The king weighed the case of the two of them and decreed, "Raise this boy jointly, the two of you. When he is grown, each of you take a wife for your son, and whatever sons are born of the two wives, each of you take your own."

Then the two householders, just as the king had decreed in judgment, raised the boy jointly and saw that he lacked for nothing. When the boy had grown up, he said to his two sets of parents, "As soon as I was born I fell into the water, was swallowed by a fish, and underwent all sorts of suffering, yet I did not die. So please permit me to go forth." Because the boy's two sets of parents loved their son so dearly, they could not refuse him and gave him leave to go forth.

The boy went to where the Buddha was, and when he arrived he bowed his head at the Blessed One's feet and said, "I wish to go forth in the Buddha's teaching." The Blessed One said, "Welcome!" His hair and beard fell away of themselves, and he became a monk. He was given the name Two Families. When the Blessed One taught the sublime Dharma to the monk Two Families, he reached the end of suffering and became an arhat on the spot.

At this the monks felt doubt, and the venerable Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "What root of virtue did this monk Two Families accumulate in the past, that though he fell into the water and lay in a fish's belly, he did not die?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Listen well. Long ago a buddha called Vipaśyin came into the world and taught the Dharma to many people. At that time a householder came into that assembly, and when he heard the Blessed One teach at length on the merit of practicing generosity, the virtues of keeping moral discipline, and so on, he was overjoyed and delighted. Faith arose in him with a pure intention, and he took from that buddha three precepts of training, beginning with abstaining from killing, and offered him one coin. Because of that, wherever he was born he was endowed with wealth and jewels and never knew want.

"Ānanda, the householder at that time is this monk Two Families. Because he offered one coin to the Buddha, for ninety-one eons he has been endowed with great wealth, and in this present life too he has been endowed with riches and possessions in the homes of his two sets of parents. Because he took the precept of not killing, though he fell into the water and went into a fish's belly, he still did not die. Because he went for refuge to the Three Jewels, he has met me and become an arhat."

Then Ānanda and the great assembly rejoiced at what the Blessed One had taught and were delighted.

This is the twenty-first chapter, on Two Families.

The sixth section.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Vaiśālī, in the Mango Grove. At that time the Blessed One spoke these words to the venerable Ānanda: "One who has attained the four bases of miraculous power can remain in the world for an eon. I too have thoroughly cultivated the four bases of miraculous power; so now it would be fitting for the Tathāgata to remain in the world for as long as his lifespan lasts." He said this three times, but Ānanda, deceived by Māra, though he heard the Blessed One's words, stayed silent and said nothing.

Then the Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Go to a secluded place and reflect deeply." Ānanda rose from his seat and went into a grove. After Ānanda had gone, the king of the māras, the Evil One, came and spoke these words to the Blessed One: "Blessed One, you have dwelt in the world for a long time. You have tamed beings fit to be tamed, as many as the sands of the river Ganges, and accomplished the aim of turning them back from saṃsāra. Now that you are old in years, it is fitting that you pass into nirvāṇa."

Then the Blessed One took up a little earth, placed it on his fingernail, and said to Māra the Evil One, "Is there more earth in the great earth, or more earth on my fingernail?"

Māra the Evil One replied, "The earth of the great earth is vast indeed, while the earth on your fingernail is not much."

The Blessed One said, "The beings I have tamed are like the earth on my fingernail. Those not tamed are like the great earth." Then the Blessed One said to Māra the Evil One, "Three months from now I shall pass completely into nirvāṇa." When Māra the Evil One heard this, he was overjoyed and delighted, and went away.

At that time Ānanda had fallen asleep in the grove, and he dreamed of a great tree that covered the whole expanse of space, its branches, leaves, flowers, and fruit in full abundance, serving the benefit of all beings, a tree endowed with qualities of every kind, too many to describe. Then, not long after, a great wind rose, and its branches and leaves all broke and were scattered like dust, so that it vanished from its place of great strength, and all beings grieved deeply. Having dreamed this, he awoke uneasy and afraid, and thought, "I dreamed of a great tree endowed with every quality, serving the benefit of all gods and humans, and not long after a great wind scattered it and destroyed it. The Blessed One, like a great tree, is the refuge of all. Can it be that the Blessed One intends to pass into nirvāṇa?" Thinking this, he was terrified, and he came to where the Blessed One was, bowed his head at his feet, and spoke these words to the Blessed One: "While I was in the grove I dreamed a dream like this. Can it be that the Blessed One intends to pass into nirvāṇa?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "It is just so. It is as you have said: three months from now I shall pass into nirvāṇa. Whoever has attained the four bases of miraculous power can remain for an eon. I too have thoroughly cultivated the four bases of miraculous power, and I asked you whether it would now be fitting for the Tathāgata to remain for as long as his lifespan lasts. Though I asked you three times, you could not answer. After you had gone, the king of the māras, the Evil One, came and entreated me to pass into nirvāṇa, and I agreed to it."

When Ānanda heard the Blessed One say this, he was deeply unhappy and tormented by grief, so that his body could not bear it. The attendants told one another, and all of them, tormented by grief, went to where the Blessed One was. Then the Blessed One said to Ānanda and the attendants, "Who is there that is called permanent? Nothing at all is permanent. For your sake I have done everything that was to be done, and I have finished teaching the teaching. So do not grieve, but apply yourselves earnestly with diligence."

Then, when the venerable Śāriputra heard that the Blessed One was to pass into nirvāṇa, he was grief-stricken and said, "The Tathāgata's passing into nirvāṇa is far too soon! The whole world with its gods will be left blind and without a protector." And he spoke these words to the Blessed One: "I cannot bear to see the Blessed One pass into nirvāṇa. By whatever means, let me pass into nirvāṇa first." He asked this three times.

The Blessed One said, "If you know that the time has come, all noble ones, being at peace, pass into nirvāṇa."

Śāriputra, having received the Blessed One's word of permission, put on his patched robe, circumambulated the Blessed One about a hundred times, and praised him in many kinds of verse. Then, holding the Buddha's feet and hands, he touched his head to the ground three times, joined his palms, and said, "Even now, at the very last, I behold the Blessed One." With reverence he bowed and took his leave, and with his attendant, the novice Cunda, he went to Rājagṛha, the place where he had been born.

When they had arrived, he instructed the novice Cunda: "Go to all the towns, the market towns, the king's palace, the ministers' residences, and the homes of patrons and friends, and tell them that I bid them farewell." Then the novice Cunda, as his teacher had instructed, went everywhere and announced, "My preceptor Śāriputra has come here and is about to pass into nirvāṇa. If you wish to see him, all of you come!" Then King Ajātaśatru, the householders of great and high families, and the patrons, hearing the novice's announcement, were all unhappy and tormented by grief, and all with one voice spoke these words:

"The venerable Śāriputra, the great chief of the Dharma, who has become the spiritual friend of so many beings, is now to pass into nirvāṇa: this is far, far too soon!" Saying this, they quickly came to where Śāriputra was, bowed, and spoke these words: "Venerable Śāriputra, great chief of the Dharma, spiritual friend of so many beings, you are giving up your life entirely and passing into nirvāṇa, and so we and many other beings will be left without protector or ally."

Then Śāriputra said to the great assembly, "All things are impermanent. What is born ends in death; what comes together ends in separation. Since the three realms are filled with suffering, who could be happy in them? Through your past roots of virtue you have indeed met with a buddha who has come into the world; yet to hear the sūtras and the Dharma is very hard, and to gain a human body is also very hard. So strive in deeds that become an accumulation of merit and bring liberation from saṃsāra." With many kinds of skillful means he taught the great assembly at length, as one gives medicine to the sick. When he had taught the Dharma to the great assembly, some attained the fruits from the first fruit up to the third; some went forth and became arhats; and some vowed to attain the state of a pratyekabuddha. Having heard the Dharma taught, they bowed and each went his own way.

Then, in the last part of that night, Śāriputra set his body and mind upright and, with mind one-pointed, entered the first meditative concentration. Emerging from the first concentration, he entered the second concentration. Emerging from the second concentration, he entered the third concentration. Emerging from the third concentration, he entered the fourth concentration. Emerging from the fourth concentration, he entered the absorption of the sphere of infinite space. Emerging from the absorption of the sphere of infinite space, he entered the absorption of the sphere of infinite consciousness. Emerging from the absorption of the sphere of infinite consciousness, he entered the absorption of the sphere of nothingness. Emerging from the absorption of the sphere of nothingness, he entered the absorption of the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception. Emerging from the absorption of the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception, he entered the absorption of cessation. Emerging from the absorption of cessation, he passed into nirvāṇa.

At that time Śakra, lord of the gods (Indra), knowing that Śāriputra had passed into nirvāṇa, came to that place with many hundreds of thousands of gods in his retinue, each carrying offerings such as flowers and incense. They filled the sky above without any gap, shed tears like rain, and strewed flowers until they lay knee-deep, and they said, "The wisdom of the venerable Śāriputra was as deep and wide as the ocean; endowed with reasoning intellect, his eloquence was like a spring. Possessed of moral discipline, absorption, and wisdom, this great chief of the Dharma turned the wheel of Dharma far and wide after the Tathāgata. That he should pass into nirvāṇa today is far too soon!"

All the people living inside and outside that city, hearing that Śāriputra had passed into nirvāṇa, came carrying ghee, fragrant oil, incense, flowers, and articles of offering. Tormented by grief beyond what their bodies could bear, they made offerings, each with the articles he had brought.

Then Śakra, lord of the gods, commanded Viśvakarman, "Prepare a chariot adorned with many jewels; Śāriputra's body is to be laid on it." Then the gods, nāgas, yakṣas, the king, the ministers, and all the people of the land, with music playing, bore it to a pleasant, level place. Śakra, lord of the gods, commanded the yakṣas, "Fetch ox-head sandalwood from the shore of the ocean." The yakṣas brought sandalwood from the shore of the ocean and piled it up, laid the body on it, sprinkled it with ghee and oil, and set it alight. All that assembly bowed and went back.

After the fire had died down, the novice Cunda took his teacher's relics, his alms bowl, and his three robes, and went to where the Buddha was. He bowed his head at his feet, set his kneecaps on the ground, and said, "My teacher has passed into nirvāṇa. These are his relics, his robes, and his alms bowl."

When Ānanda heard what the novice said, he was deeply tormented by grief and spoke these words to the Blessed One: "If even Śāriputra, the great chief and master of the Dharma, has passed into nirvāṇa, with whom shall we take our stand, and in whom shall we take refuge?"

The Blessed One said, "Although Śāriputra has passed into nirvāṇa, his moral discipline, absorption, wisdom, liberation, and the vision of the wisdom of liberation, his Dharma body, have not passed into nirvāṇa. It is not only now that Śāriputra, unable to bear seeing me pass into nirvāṇa, has passed into nirvāṇa before me. In times long past too, unable to bear my death, he died before me."

Ānanda joined his palms and said to the Blessed One, "Please explain in full the matter of how, in a former life too, he died first and the Blessed One passed away afterward."

The Blessed One spoke these words to Ānanda: "Long ago, before countless, measureless, inconceivable eons had passed, in a great land here in Jambudvīpa there arose a king called Candraprabha, who ruled over Jambudvīpa, over eighty-four thousand vassal kings, and over eight hundred thousand cities in sixty thousand provinces. That king had twenty thousand queens. The senior queen was called Flower Giver, and the middle queen was called Great Eyes. That king had some five hundred princes. The king's eldest son, the regent, was called Excellent Discipline. The palace where the king lived was called Good Life; that palace measured four hundred yojanas square and was made of gold, silver, beryl, and crystal. In its pleasure garden there were four rows of trees of gold, silver, beryl, and crystal.

Some had branches of gold and leaves of silver; some had branches of silver and leaves of gold; some had branches of beryl and leaves of crystal; some had branches of crystal and leaves of beryl. The jewel ponds too were made of gold, silver, beryl, and crystal, and the floors of the ponds were spread with sand of the four kinds of jewels. Around the king's palace alone, for forty yojanas in every direction, the land was filled to overflowing with gold, silver, beryl, and crystal. The people who lived in that land were happy and content; the harvests were good, their wealth was abundant, and they possessed countless precious jewels.

At that time, as the king dwelt in his palace, this thought kept arising in him: "Those in the world who are powerful and great are honored by everyone." No sooner had he thought this than the finest enjoyments of the five sense pleasures came to him just as he wished. "That I have attained such a supreme result has come from the accumulation of merit I produced in the past. Farmers, for example, hope that if they sow much seed in spring, they will gather much in autumn. I am just the same: because I produced an accumulation of merit in the past, all this has now come to me. If I do not plant seed now, there will be nothing to hope for later." With this thought he said to his ministers, "Now, in order to give away all my jewels and wealth, pile them up at the city gates and at the crossroads of the marketplace, and let whatever I possess be given to beings as they wish. Further, I decree that all the treasuries of the eighty-four thousand vassal kingdoms be given to the multitude of beings." Having commanded this, he raised a golden banner and beat a golden drum, and had it proclaimed far and wide in every direction, so that all heard and knew.

Then the śramaṇas and brahmins dwelling in that land, the poor and destitute, the helpless, the old, and all who lacked means gathered like clouds. To those who wanted clothes he gave clothes; to those who wanted food, food; to those who wanted gold, silver, and jewels, gold, silver, and jewels; and to the sick, medicines and whatever else they needed for their illnesses, just as they wished. At that time all the beings living in Jambudvīpa, through the king's kindness, were happy and content; they sang songs, danced, and praised him in verse. The king's good name was proclaimed to the farthest borders, and there was no one who did not obey his command.

At that time, in the borderlands of the country, a minor king called Bhīmasena grew jealous of King Candraprabha. He could not lie down in comfort and could not sleep, and he thought, "So long as I have not done away with King Candraprabha, my name will never become renowned. I will summon the wandering ascetics who live in the regions of my land and find a way to have them accomplish this." With this thought he summoned the wandering ascetics who lived in his land, prepared food of a hundred flavors of every kind, and honored them respectfully for three months. Then he spoke these words to the wandering ascetics: "I am overcome with grief and have no peace day or night. You are practitioners of Dharma and worthy of offerings, so think of a way to rid me of my grief."

The brahmins said, "Tell us, O king, why you are distressed. We will do whatever we can so that the king is no longer distressed."

The king said, "King Candraprabha's virtues are renowned everywhere, and everyone serves and honors him, while I alone have no renown for virtue. I would be glad to have that king done away with. Tell me what means you have."

When the brahmins heard this command, they said these words to one another: "This King Candraprabha is endowed with compassion. His kindness pervades all beings, and to the poor and destitute he is like a father and mother. Even if it costs us our lives, none of us could harm him." So they said, and without regard even for their livelihood they went their separate ways.

When King Bhīmasena heard what they had said, he was all the more displeased, and he issued this decree: "Whoever cuts off King Candraprabha's head for my sake and brings it here, to him I will give half my kingdom and my daughter for a wife." When he had issued this decree, a brahmin called Raudrākṣa, up on a rocky mountain, heard it. He came to where that king was, overjoyed, and said, "I can accomplish what the king intends."

The king said, "If you are to accomplish this, on what day will you go?"

The brahmin said, "Give me provisions for the road, and I will set out seven days from now." For seven days the brahmin performed mantras to protect his body; then he took his leave of the king, set out on the road, and went.

At that time all kinds of evil omens appeared in King Candraprabha's land. The whole earth cracked, lightning flashed, stars fell, and day and night the land was shrouded in darkness; thunder roared, hail fell, and thunderbolts struck. Many birds cried pitifully in the sky and fell from the sky of their own accord. Tigers, leopards, wolves, beasts of prey, wild game, and other animals ran and leapt about, uttering harsh cries. The eighty-four thousand vassal kings dreamed that the great king's golden banner broke and his golden drum split. The minister called Mahācandra dreamed that a hungry ghost snatched away the king's golden diadem. All of them were overcome with grief beyond what their bodies could bear.

At that time the god who guarded the city gates realized that the brahmin was coming to beg for the king's head. He confused and bewildered him and would not let him in through the city gates, so that though the brahmin circled the city many times, he could not get inside.

Then a god of the Pure Abodes, knowing that if King Candraprabha gave away his head he would bring the perfection of generosity to completion, informed the king in a dream: "Great king, you have vowed to give gifts without going against anyone's wishes. Now a beggar has come to the city gate, and he has no way to get inside." Thus he informed him.

When the king awoke he was amazed, and he commanded the minister Mahācandra, "Go, and whoever comes to the city gates, do not stop him; let him in."

When Mahācandra came to the city gates, the god who guarded the city gates revealed his body and said to Mahācandra, "A brahmin has come from another land. With an evil mind he has come to beg the king for his head, and so I have not let him in."

The minister said, "If that is true, it is a great omen of harm. Even so, the king's command must not be broken." So he ordered, and then the god who guarded the city let the brahmin in.

The minister Mahācandra thought, "This brahmin will certainly beg for the king's head. I will have five hundred heads made from each one of the seven precious substances, and with these heads I will ransom the king's head." With this thought he had them made.

Then the brahmin came to the king's palace and said in a loud voice, "I have come from the borderlands of the country. From far away I heard of the great quality of the great king, that he gives away everything and does not go against the wishes of others, and so I have come here from a long road, wanting what I need."

When the king heard him say this, he was overjoyed and delighted. He paid homage to him and asked, "Are you not weary?" Then he said, "Whatever wealth you want, whether land, or a kingdom, or a city, or a wife, or a son, or a daughter, or jewels, or a vehicle, or an elephant, or a horse, or the seven precious substances, or a palace, or a male servant, or a female servant, or laborers, I will give you whatever you want."

The brahmin said, "A gift of external possessions brings much merit of giving, yet it does not become a great gift. The giving of the flesh and blood of one's own body is the highest merit, and so I have come this long road to ask for the king's head. If you will not go against my wish, please give it."

When the king heard him say this, he became incomparably joyful.

The brahmin said, "If you will give me your head, please declare when you will give it."

The king said, "Seven days from now I will give you my head."

Then the minister Mahācandra brought the heads made of the seven precious substances, and, prostrating himself on the ground before the brahmin, he said, "The king's head is flesh, blood and bone. It is impure; what would you do with it? Exchange that head for these heads of the seven precious substances. With these heads of the seven precious substances you will be rich for your whole life."

The brahmin said, "I do not need these. I need the king's head. Fulfill my hope!"

Although Mahācandra entreated him by every kind of means, the brahmin would not agree. Overwhelmed by sorrow, Mahācandra fainted, his heart burst into seven pieces, and he died.

At that time the king sent a minister mounted on an elephant that could travel a hundred thousand leagues to inform all the vassal kings: "Seven days from now King Candraprabha will make the gift of his head to a brahmin. All who wish to see it, come quickly!"

Then all of the eighty thousand vassal kings arrived at once. They came before the king, prostrated themselves on the ground, and said, "By the king's kindness all the beings of Jambudvīpa are happy and at ease. Since there is no harm, it is not right to abandon all beings for the sake of one man. Out of compassion for us, we beg you not to make the gift of your head."

The ten thousand ministers also prostrated themselves on the ground and begged, "Out of compassion for us, do not make the gift of your head."

The twenty thousand queens also prostrated themselves on the ground and said to the king, "Think of us with compassion, and do not give your head, we beg you."

The five hundred princes also wept and said, "We have no protector and no refuge. Think of us with compassion, and do not make the gift of your head. Consider that we too are still learning by following you."

Then the great king said to the great ministers, the queens and the princes:

"From beginningless time this body has circled in saṃsāra. For a long time, while dwelling in the hells, it died many times even in a single day, and so this body was wasted. In the hells of the river of ash, the iron couches, the boiling water, the vomit, the chariots of fire, the fire pits and the rest, it experienced suffering for a long time. Boiled and burned and so on, this body was wasted countless times, yet there was not even a little merit.

"When I was born in the animal realm, we devoured and chewed one another; I was killed by others and eaten by all; I died and rotted and decayed, countless times. So too this body was wasted, yet it did not become merit.

"When I was born in the realm of hungry ghosts, my body blazed with fire, and no sooner had a wheel cut off my head than I revived again; this I experienced countless times. So the body was wasted, yet it did not become merit.

"When I was born as a human, for the sake of wealth and women, with eyes glaring in anger and fury, we harmed and killed one another, and came to the battlefront and killed one another. So the body was wasted, and through attachment, aversion and delusion I cast away countless bodies, yet it did not become merit.

"I shall give this body away. This body of mine is an aggregate of all kinds of impurity. Before long it will perish and fall apart. Since it is impermanent, if this filthy head can serve a beneficial aim, why should I not give it? I will give this head of mine to that brahmin, and with that merit I will accomplish unsurpassed awakening. When I am fully awakened as a buddha I will be endowed with perfect qualities, and by my skill in means I will free you too from suffering."

"Through this giving that I make I shall become fully awakened. Do not hinder or obstruct my mind set on unsurpassed awakening."

When the vassal kings, the ministers, the queens and the princes heard him speak these words, they sat without saying anything.

Then the great king said to the brahmin, "Now the time has come to take my head. Take it!"

The brahmin replied, "Right now the king is surrounded by a large retinue of ministers and others. I alone do not have the power to cut off the king's head; it would not be borne. If you wish to give it to me, please go to the pleasure grove at the back."

Then the king commanded the vassal kings and the princes, "If you honor and serve me, do not make any trouble to harm this brahmin." Having so commanded, the king went together with the brahmin to the pleasure grove.

Then the brahmin said to the king, "The king is young, and in stature he is equal to a mighty wrestler. When the pain becomes unbearable, you may well regret it. So tie the king's head to a tree; only then will I kill you."

Then the king tied his head to a tree whose branches were firm and which was covered all over with leaves. He set his knees on the ground and said to the brahmin, "Cut off my head, then place it back in my hands, and take it from my hands. Through this merit of giving you my head, I do not wish to be Māra or Brahmā. It is not for the sake of wishing to be Śakra, or a wheel-turning king, or for the pleasures and enjoyments of the three realms. I wish for unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening, in order to protect many beings and establish them in the bliss of nirvāṇa."

At that moment, as the brahmin raised his sword to strike, the deity of the tree became deeply displeased and thought, "Why should this man do the deed of killing?" The deity struck the brahmin a single slap beside his ear, and he lost control of his hands and feet and fell to the ground.

Then the king looked up at the deity of the tree and said, "In times past I made gifts of as many as nine hundred and ninety-nine heads at this tree. Now with this head the gifts will reach a full thousand, and the perfection of generosity will be completely fulfilled. Do not hinder my mind set on unsurpassed awakening."

Then, when the deity of the tree heard what the king said, it restored the brahmin to his senses. The brahmin got up, took his sword, and cut off the king's head. As the head fell into his hands, the great earth shook in six ways. The palaces of the gods also trembled and swayed, and the gods became uneasy and were filled with amazement. When they all saw that the bodhisattva had made the gift of his head for the sake of all beings, they all gathered and assembled, and, crying "How wondrous!", with tears falling like rain they wept and said, "By making the gift of his head, King Candraprabha has completely fulfilled the perfection of generosity."

This became known everywhere, and when King Bhīmasena heard it, he was displeased; he fainted, his heart burst, and he died.

At that time, as the brahmin was going away carrying the king's head, the vassal kings, the ministers, the queens and the princes threw themselves on the ground and wailed. Some vomited blood and died. Some fainted, unconscious of anything at all. Some tore out all their hair. Some ripped all their clothes. Some scratched their faces all over with both their hands, wailing and rolling on the ground.

Then the brahmin, thinking, "The king's head is ugly and stinking," did not want it, and he threw it on the ground and went away. Some brahmins reviled him, saying, "You are utterly wicked! If you had no need of it in the first place, why did you ask for it?" As the brahmin set out on the road, everyone he met reviled him and would not give him food, and he became utterly wretched. On the road he heard the news that Bhīmasena too had died. Since things had not turned out as he had first hoped, he was crushed by sorrow; his heart burst into seven pieces, he vomited blood, and he died.

When King Bhīmasena and the brahmin died, they were both reborn in the Avīci hell. Those who died tormented by grief for King Candraprabha were reborn as gods in the higher realms.

"Ānanda, King Candraprabha at that time, on that occasion, is now myself. King Bhīmasena is Māra the Evil One. The brahmin at that time is now Devadatta. The tree goddess at that time is now Maudgalyāyana. The minister Mahācandra at that time is Śāriputra. At that time too, unable to bear seeing me die, he died first. In this present time too, unable to bear seeing me pass into nirvāṇa, he has passed into nirvāṇa first."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, the venerable Ānanda and the rest of the assembly praised him with one voice, saying, "The qualities of the Tathāgata are wondrous and marvelous!" Through earnest application to meditation, some attained the fruits of the ascetic life, and some attained unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. They rejoiced and delighted in what the Blessed One had said.

This is the twenty-second chapter, on King Candraprabha's gift of his head.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time King Prasenajit had a minister called Mṛga, who was very rich and had great wealth. He had seven sons.

Six of the sons had taken wives, but among them the one youngest son had not taken a wife, and his father thought, "I am old and frail. For this one son who has not taken a wife, I shall seek out an excellent, fine woman."

At that time the householder had a brahmin who was his close friend. Meeting him, he talked it over with him: "My youngest son has not yet taken a wife, and I do not know whom to ask. You travel through all lands; have you seen any girl who is good and beautiful, intelligent, and blessed with long life and good fortune? You must find one who will please this son of mine."

The brahmin said, "I shall do so," and as he wandered everywhere searching, in a land called Śiritita he saw five hundred girls together who, while at play, were picking fine flowers and making garlands to offer to the Buddha. The brahmin followed after them, watching where the girls went. As the girls were wading across a small stream, all the others took off their shoes, but one girl crossed the water without taking off her shoes. Then, going on and on, they crossed a great river; the other girls took off their clothes and entered the water, but that girl alone crossed the water without even taking off her clothes. Then they went into a grove; the other girls climbed up into the trees and picked flowers, while that girl alone gathered them on the ground, yet she found more than the others.

Then the brahmin went up to the girl and said, "If I ask you about something I wonder about, give me a good answer."

The girl said, "If you have a doubt, ask."

The brahmin asked, "Girl, when you crossed the water, the other girls took off their shoes to cross. You alone crossed the water without taking off your shoes. What other thought did you have?"

The girl said, "Your doubt is nothing very remarkable. When I walk on dry ground, my eyes can see what lies beneath my feet, so if I notice a thorn, or a stone, or a potsherd, or anything unpleasant, I step around it as I go. But the eyes cannot see what is at the bottom of the water. I did not take off my shoes for fear that if I stepped on thorns, or snakes, or venomous insects, my feet would be harmed."

The brahmin said, "Then why did you go into the water with your clothes on?"

The girl said, "A woman's body has good marks and bad ones. If one takes off one's clothes and goes into the water, others will see. If the marks are good, they will say nothing; but if the marks are bad, one will be mocked. So I did not take off my clothes."

The brahmin said, "Then why did you alone not climb the trees?"

The girl said, "If I climbed a tree, and a branch broke or I fell, my body would be harmed. So I did not climb the tree."

This girl was the daughter of Balamitra, the younger brother of King Prasenajit. The king's brother had once committed an offense, and so he was banished from that country and came to this one, where he took a woman called Divine Boundary as his wife; and this girl was born from her.

The brahmin said, "Girl, you are very wise and intelligent indeed. Do you have a father and mother?"

The girl said, "I do."

"Then let us go together to your house," he said, and when they reached her door the girl went inside and said to her parents, "At the door there is a brahmin who says he wishes to meet Father."

The girl's father came out to the door. They asked each other, "Are you well?" Then the brahmin asked, "Is that girl just now your daughter?"

He said, "She is my daughter."

"Then does she have a husband?"

He said, "She has no husband."

The brahmin said, "Do you know the minister called Mṛga in Śrāvastī?"

He said, "He is my friend."

The brahmin said, "That minister's youngest son is handsome in appearance and upright in mind. I ask for your daughter as his wife."

He said, "That family is great and high. If he wants my daughter, I shall do just as you think best."

When he had said this, the brahmin, since a companion was going back to Śrāvastī, sent a letter by way of that brahmin. When the letter arrived through the brahmin, horses, chariots and so on to welcome the bride were made ready, and then they set out for the land of Śiritita. As they drew near, they sent a man ahead to announce them, and the father too prepared a feast for sending off his daughter as a bride, and sent his daughter off as a bride.

When they had received the girl and were setting out for Śrāvastī, the girl's mother said to her daughter in front of many people, "From today on, always wear fine clothes and eat delicious food. Look in the mirror every single day without fail."

The girl said, "I shall do so."

Her father-in-law was displeased, and thought, "For anyone who is born, happiness and sorrow are impermanent. How could one always get fine clothes and delicious food? And to tell her always to look in the mirror is not proper either." With this thought, they took leave of one another and parted.

They all set out together on the road, returning to their own country. Along the way there was a fine, cool house, and those of them who had gone ahead stopped and settled in that house. When the new bride arrived there later, she said to her father-in-law, "It is not right to stay beneath this. Please go out onto the open ground quickly." As the bride had said, he came out onto the open ground. Some servants were unwilling to go and stayed on beneath the house. Before long an ox and a horse came there and rubbed against a pillar; the house collapsed, and those who were beneath the house perished.

The father-in-law thought, "It is this daughter-in-law who has saved me from losing my life," and he felt special affection for his daughter-in-law.

Then, going on and on, they stopped in a hollow that had grass and water. The daughter-in-law came later and said, "It is not right to stay in this hollow. Move quickly!" No sooner had they moved somewhere else than a violent downpour fell, and a great flood came into the hollow where they had first camped.

The father-in-law thought, "Twice now my daughter-in-law has saved me from losing my life." Setting out, they went on and reached their own country. Then the relatives arrived, spoke words of rejoicing, held a great feast, and made merry and celebrated for a whole day. After all the guests had dispersed, he called his daughters-in-law together and addressed them as follows.

"I am old in years and weary of affairs. Since I must hand over the household's wealth and goods to you, my daughters-in-law, which of you will keep the keys of the storehouse?"

When he asked this, all six of the elder daughters-in-law replied, "We cannot." The youngest among them replied, "I can."

Then the householder handed over all the keys of the storehouse, and the daughter-in-law managed it accordingly. In the morning she rose early, swept the whole house and sprinkled it with water. Then she cooked the food, and first served the meal to her father-in-law, her mother-in-law and the rest in order of seniority. After that she served the meal to the servants and the laborers. When she had set each of them to their work, only then did she herself eat. Because she always did things this way, her father-in-law thought, "This daughter-in-law of mine is not like the others; she is no ordinary person. Why, then, does the girl not do as her mother instructed?" With this thought he said to her, "When you set out from home, your mother said, 'Always wear fine clothes and eat delicious food; always look in the mirror.' What did those words mean?"

The daughter-in-law set both knees on the ground and replied, "When my mother instructed me to wear fine clothes, she meant: 'Always take care of the outer garments that are kept for wearing, and do not let them grow old. When guests or outsiders come, do not be poorly dressed.' When she said, 'Always eat delicious food,' she did not mean delicious and tasty food. She meant: 'If you eat later than everyone, then because you are hungry, all food is delicious.' When she said, 'Always look in the mirror,' she did not mean looking into a mirror of bronze or iron. She meant: 'Rise early and do your duties well: sweeping inside and out, sprinkling water, laying out the seats and cushions, and so on.' That is what my mother's instructions were."

At this he felt still greater affection for that daughter-in-law, and he entrusted all the work of the household to her, and everyone became happy and content.

At that time some birds were eating rice on an island in the ocean, and they flew off carrying ears of rice. When they came directly above the king's palace, some ears of rice slipped from them and fell right above the palace. Someone offered those ears of rice to the king. The king thought, "Such fine rice as this will surely be fit for medicine; it should be kept for seed," and he divided it among all the ministers, portion by portion, and commanded, "Sow it!"

At that time the householder too received a little of it. He came back home and told his daughter-in-law, "Sow this rice." The daughter-in-law, as she had been told, sent other servants, had a field plowed thoroughly and sowed it, and it grew well accordingly and yielded a great deal of rice. In the fields sown by others, because the work had not been done well, not a single one grew.

At that time the king's queen was struck by an illness, and the physicians examined her and determined, "If she is given rice from the island in the ocean, she will recover from this sickness." The king remembered that he had had the rice from the island in the ocean sown by each of them, and asked, "Was the rice from the island in the ocean that I had sown planted or not?" Some ministers said, "The rice that was sown did not grow." Some said, "Mice ate all the rice, and there is none."

At that time the householder went back home and asked his daughter-in-law, "Did the rice I had you sow earlier grow or not? The king's queen has been struck by an illness, and it is needed as medicine."

The daughter-in-law replied, "The rice that was sown has done very well. There is not merely medicine for one person; there is enough to restore the people of a whole country."

The householder offered a portion of that rice to the king, and the king had the queen treated with that rice as medicine. The queen recovered, and the king was overjoyed and gave him many rewards.

At that time the king of Śiritita and the king of the land of Śrāvastī were displeased with each other and were never at peace. At that time the king of Śiritita thought, "I shall find out whether or not the king of Śrāvastī has wise ministers, learned and intelligent." He sent a messenger to the land of Śrāvastī, and with that messenger he sent a mare and her offspring, the two alike as mother and young, of one coat color and indistinguishable, the mare and her foal paired together, with the message, "Distinguish which is the mother and which is the young."

The king and the ministers looked, but not one of them could distinguish them.

Then the householder came home, and his daughter-in-law asked, "What news have you heard?" He told his daughter-in-law in detail, "This is the talk going around."

The daughter-in-law replied, "This is very easy to discern. Take the mare and her young and give them some good grass together. The one that is the mother will push the grass toward the young one with her lips and feed it."

The householder told the king what his daughter-in-law had said. The king gave them grass and watched, and it happened just so; he recognized the mother and the young, and said to the messenger, "This is the mother, and this is the young." The messenger said, "It is just as you say; there is no mistake."

Then the messenger went to his own country and reported this in detail to his king.

Then the king sent another messenger, sending two snakes alike and equal in thickness and length, with the command, "Distinguish which of these two snakes is male and which is female."

King Prasenajit and the ministers gathered and examined them, but not one of them could tell.

Then the householder asked his daughter-in-law at home, and the daughter-in-law explained: "Spread out a soft cloth and place the snakes on it. The one that is female will lie without moving at all. The one that is male will be the sort that cannot bear it and will not stay still. How can this be seen? The one that is female delights in what is soft and light. The one that is male, being by nature irritable, cannot bear softness and will not lie still. By this one will know."

The householder told the king accordingly, and when they did as the daughter-in-law had said, they distinguished clearly which was the male and which was the female. They told the messenger, "This is the male, and this is the female," and he said, "Just so; there is no mistake."

Then the king was overjoyed and gave him many precious jewels as a reward.

Then he also sent a log about a fathom long, equal in thickness throughout, without any distinguishing features, with no knots and no marks of axe or adze, with the command, "Distinguish which is the top and which is the bottom of this log."

The king and the ministers gathered and examined it, but not one of them could tell. The householder again asked his daughter-in-law, and she said, "This is very easy. If you want to know the top and bottom of this log, put it in the water. The root will sink downward, while the top will rise up."

The householder told the king accordingly, and when they did as the daughter-in-law had said, they recognized the top and the bottom, and they told the messenger, "This is the top, and this is the root." The messenger said, "Just so; it is true."

Then the king was overjoyed and gave him many rewards. The messenger went back to his own country and reported in detail to his king, and that king sent the messenger with many jewels, saying, "That great king has wise and intelligent ministers. Offer him these, and from now on let us be at peace."

King Prasenajit was overjoyed that things had turned out this way, and he said to the householder, "How did you work these things out and know them?"

The householder replied, "It was not I who worked this out. I have an intelligent daughter-in-law, and it was she who worked it out."

When the king heard him say this, he was overjoyed and declared, "From now on the householder's daughter-in-law shall be as a sister to me."

Later the householder's daughter-in-law became pregnant, and when nine months were complete, thirty-two eggs came forth, and from within each egg came forth a boy, handsome in appearance and lovely to behold. When the boys grew up, their courage was unequaled; each one of them was a match for a thousand men. Their father and mother loved those boys dearly, and all the people of the land held them in awe. Later each took a wife from a great and high family of matching lineage in that land.

At that time Divine Boundary, having a mind of faith, invited the Buddha and the Saṅgha. When the Blessed One taught the Dharma, all the people of the whole household attained the fruit of stream-entry; only the youngest of the boys did not attain the fruit.

That boy rode out on an elephant to play outside the city. Outside that city flowed a great river, and over it there was a bridge. As the boy came onto the bridge, the son of a chief minister, sitting in a chariot, was coming in from outside, and they met on the bridge. Both were proud of their noble birth, and neither would give way to the other. Divine Boundary's son became angry, and still mounted on his elephant, he threw the chief minister's son, chariot and all, off the bridge.

The minister's son, injured in his body, hands and feet, came home weeping and said to his father and mother, "Though I did nothing wrong, Divine Boundary's son did me this harm, and my whole body is hurt."

The chief minister was very displeased and thought, "Those sons are extremely powerful, and on top of that they are close to the king through their mother, so it is hard to contend with them. Even so, I shall devise a trick against them." With this thought he had thirty-two staffs made of the seven precious substances, placed a sharp-edged sword inside each staff, and gave one to each of the thirty-two boys, saying, "You have reached the prime of youth, and it is the time for play. I have had these staffs made for you to play with. Carry them and enjoy yourselves." The boys were delighted, took the staffs, and each carried one.

By the king's law, no one was permitted to carry weapons when meeting the king. As they sat attending on the king carrying the staffs, the minister slandered them to the king, saying, "Those thirty-two sons of Divine Boundary are young and in their prime. In strength each of them is a match for a thousand men, and they are intending to do the king some harm."

Even so, the king did not believe it of them, and the minister said again, "What I said before is true; it is not a lie, it is always so. In each of these staffs that the boys carry in their hands there is a sword, so it is evident that they mean to do harm."

When, as the minister had said, they examined the boys' staffs, the weapons were there. They called them one by one, had them seized by strongmen, and killed them all. They cut off the boys' heads, put the thirty-two heads of the boys into a box, sealed it well, and sent it to the king's sister.

At that time the boys' mother had invited the Buddha and the Saṅgha, and when the box sent by the king arrived, she thought that inside it there might be some contribution for making offerings, and she was about to open the box. The Blessed One said, "Do not open the box for now. Open it after the meal has been served."

Then, after the meal had been served, the Blessed One taught the Dharma to the boys' mother: "This body is impermanent, suffering, empty and without self. It has no essence and does not last long. It is bound and fettered by the afflictions and tormented by suffering. Through sorrow at being parted from what one loves, this body is wasted, and that is of no benefit. Whoever is learned and wise will understand this point."

Divine Boundary understood the point and attained the fruit of once-returning. Overjoyed and delighted, she joined her palms and said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, out of compassion for me, please grant these four requests of mine. Of my four requests, the first is to provide sick monks with medicine for their illness, food and drink as they wish; the second is to supply food and drink as they wish to those who attend on sick monks; the third is to provide the requisites for visiting monks; and the fourth is to provide provisions and requisites for monks setting out on a journey."

"Why is this? Sick monks who lack the medicine they need for their illness, and food and drink, are hard to cure, and it may even cost them their lives. The monks who attend the sick, for want of food, also go for alms. But almsgoing has its fixed time, and if they are late or do not obtain what is wanted, the sick monk grows angry and becomes hard to care for; so I wish to offer food to them as well. Monks who come from far away do not know the country and have no friends, so they go for alms. When they go for alms, if they are harassed by dogs or by ruffians, anger arises and their patience is impaired; so I wish to offer food to them as well. Monks setting out on a journey, even when they have companions, may lack provisions and be parted from their companions; there are many beasts of prey or savage bandits on the road, and if they go on alone, that too may endanger their bodies. So I wish to provide for their needs as well."

Then the Blessed One, having heard the four requests Divine Boundary had made, praised her, saying, "Excellent, excellent! The vast merit of these four requests you have made is no different from making offerings to the Buddha." Having declared this, he returned with the Saṅgha to the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta.

After the Blessed One had gone, she opened the casket and looked inside. She saw that it held the heads of her thirty-two sons, yet because she was free of desire, she did not become distressed. She said, "Alas! For anyone who is born, life also comes to an end, and it is not long in coming. Now, in which of the five realms will they experience suffering?"

When the relatives on the mother's side of the thirty-two boys heard what had happened, they were deeply displeased, and anger arose in them. They said, "Though they pleased him so, the great king has killed every one of them. We will go and make war on him." They gathered a military force and surrounded the king's palace. The king was terrified and fled to where the Buddha was. Hearing of this, they surrounded the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta with their army as well.

Then Ānanda heard that King Prasenajit had killed the thirty-two sons of Divine Boundary and that those belonging to their mother's family had come to take revenge. He set his knee on the ground, joined his palms, and said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, through what cause and what condition were these thirty-two men killed by the king all at once?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "It is not only now that the thirty-two sons of Divine Boundary have been killed by the king all at once. I shall explain to you in detail why these thirty-two men lost their lives all at once. Listen well and keep it in mind." Ānanda replied, "I shall do so. I wish to hear it."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "In times long past, these thirty-two men were fond of one another, friends and intimates. By common agreement they stole a cow belonging to someone else. At that time there lived in that country a poor old woman with no children at all. The men who had stolen the cow led it to that old woman's house and were about to slaughter it there, and the old woman, delighted, gathered firewood and the like and prepared the things for cooking the meat. When the men were about to kill the cow there, the cow understood that it was to be killed and made a resolve, saying, 'Because you are now killing me, in the future, even if you should attain the fruit, I will not let you go: may I come to kill you!' As soon as it had made this vow, the men killed the cow. Some boiled the meat and some roasted it, and they ate it. The old woman too was sated with the cow's meat and greatly delighted, and she said, 'Guests like these have come before, but none has ever been of more help than today's.'

"The cow of that time was King Prasenajit. The cow thieves of that time are these thirty-two sons of Divine Boundary. The old woman, the mistress of the house, at that time is their mother. By that karmic maturation, for five hundred lifetimes they were always killed. And the old woman of that time, merely through rejoicing and being glad, was always their mother and was tormented by that much grief. Now, having met me, she has attained the fruit."

Again Ānanda joined his palms and said to the Blessed One, "What merit did they make, that they came to be of a great and high family, possessed of wealth, brave and endowed with strength?"

The Blessed One said, "In times long past, when the buddha Kāśyapa had appeared in the world, there was an old woman with great faith in the Three Jewels. She would buy a great deal of incense, mix it with butter, and continually anoint a stupa with it. On one occasion, as she was anointing the stupa, thirty-two men arrived there and helped the old woman anoint it. The old woman said to those thirty-two men, 'By the merit of helping me anoint the stupa, wherever you are born, may you have fine fortune and be endowed with power and strength!' They too, greatly delighted, did the work of anointing the stupa and said, 'Through this old woman we have brought about meritorious deeds.' And they made this aspiration: 'Wherever we are born, may we be of high and mighty family, rich, and possessed of wealth; may this old woman always be our mother and we her sons; may we never be parted from the Buddha and from hearing the Dharma; and may we quickly attain the fruit!' The old woman too said, 'May it be so!' And so for five hundred lifetimes they were of high and mighty family.

"The old woman of that time is now the mother of these boys. The thirty-two men of that time are these boys."

Then, when the soldiers heard what the Buddha had said, their angry minds were all pacified, and they said, "Let the great king be forgiven. This is the karmic maturation of these men's former deeds: merely from killing a cow it has come to this. This King Prasenajit is our lord.

Why should we bear a grudge against him and think of harming him?" Having said this, they threw down their weapons, went before the king, and confessed their offense, and the king for his part let them go without bringing any charge.

Then the Blessed One, taking this account of what had happened in the past, taught the sublime Dharma at length to the fourfold assembly. He taught that it is right to make diligent effort in virtuous actions and not right to commit nonvirtuous actions, and he also taught the Four Noble Truths at length. When the great assembly heard this, all of them attained the fruit, and they rejoiced in what the Blessed One had said and were delighted.

This is the chapter on the seven sons of the minister Mṛga, the twenty-third.

Fascicle seven.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time the king of that country was called Prasenajit. To its south lay a country called Golden Land, whose king was called Kapphiṇa. That king's son was called Mahākapphiṇa. When his father the king died, the son, as crown prince, ascended the throne. He was by nature upright of mind and clear of intellect, brave, and possessed of great might and prowess. He had thirty-six thousand battalions of troops, and wherever those troops descended, like a great wind, there was no foe they could not overcome. He had no dealings with the kings of the Middle Country.

Later some merchants went to Golden Land and presented that king with four bolts of fine, soft cloth. The king accepted them and asked the merchants, "Where does such a fine treasure come from?" The merchants replied, "It comes from the Middle Country." The king said, "What is this place called the Middle Country? What is the name of the Middle Country?" The merchants replied, "The Middle Country is called Rājagṛha, and it is also called Śrāvastī; and there are many other names of countries there besides." The king said, "Why do those kings not come to pay homage to me and offer tribute?" The merchants replied, "Those kings each hold their own kingdoms and manage the affairs of their own lands, so they do not come here."

The king thought, "By my might and majesty I have subdued everything under heaven. Why then do these kings not come to pay homage to me and offer tribute? Now I shall bring them down by my might and majesty." And he said to the merchants, "Among the kings of the Middle Country, which king is the greatest?" The merchants said, "Among them the king of Śrāvastī is the greatest."

Then that king sent an envoy to Śrāvastī with this message for King Prasenajit: "My might and majesty hold sway over all that belongs to Jambudvīpa, so why do you not even exchange envoys with me? Now I have sent an envoy to you. When you hear my name, if you are lying down, you should sit up. If you are sitting, you should rise. If you are eating, you should spit the food out without taking time to swallow. If you hear it in the midst of bathing, you should tie up your hair at once. If you are staying still, you should run. Within seven days from now, come here to meet me. If you do not, I shall lead my army and wipe out your country."

When King Prasenajit heard this message, he was displeased and alarmed. He went to where the Blessed One was and told him all of it in full. The Blessed One said, "Your Majesty, tell that envoy, 'Among kings I am not great; there is a king greater than I.'" So King Prasenajit, as the Blessed One had instructed, ordered him, "In this world there is a king of magic who dwells not far from here. Go there yourself and do just as your king's message says." And the envoy set out from the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta.

At that time the Blessed One transformed himself into a wheel-turning monarch, emanated Maudgalyāyana as his great minister, and sat endowed with the seven precious things. He also transformed the park into jewels: outside the park there was a moat of jewels in seven tiers, and between the moats were dense trees of the seven kinds of jewels, with countless lotuses of every color shimmering and glittering. Within the park, in a palace made of various jewels, he conjured a king of exceedingly great majesty seated there.

The envoy came to the conjured palace, and at the mere sight of the great king he was seized with terror. He thought, "My king's majesty and merit cannot match this. Nevertheless, I shall present the letter he sent," and he presented the letter. The king of magic took the letter and placed it under the sole of his foot, and said to the envoy, "I am a great king who holds sway over the four continents. Why should I obey that foolish king of yours? Go back quickly and tell him that I have commanded as follows: 'As soon as your envoy returns and you hear my command, if you hear it while lying down, you should rise. If you hear it while sitting, you should stand. If you hear it while walking, you should run. If within seven days from now you do not come here to pay homage, and so break my command, you will be dealt with according to the law.'"

The envoy went back as he had been commanded, and on reaching his own country he told the king of Golden Land in full all that he had seen and the message he had been sent with. When the king heard that message, he became remorseful and thought, "I shall go to the great king together with all the vassal kings under my rule. But it is not proper to go unannounced; I shall consult him once more." So he first sent an envoy ahead, saying, "When I come to pay homage to the great king, since there are three hundred sixty thousand vassal kings under my rule, should they all come, or should only half of them come? Please give your command."

The great king replied, ordering him, "Come with half of your vassal kings." So the king of Golden Land, together with eighteen thousand vassal kings, arrived in Śrāvastī before the conjured king and paid homage to him, and he thought this:

"This great king surpasses me in appearance, but in might and strength he is no match for me." As he thought this, the king of magic said to his precious minister, "Give your bow to the king of Golden Land." The king took it, but when he tried to lift it, he could not. Then the king of magic took it, strung the bow with his little finger, and said to the king of Golden Land, "Draw this bow." When the king of Golden Land took it, he could not so much as move the bowstring. Then the king of magic took it and drew it, and merely by the twang of the bowstring the great thousandfold world system quaked and shook.

Then he took up an arrow and shot it, and the moment it was shot it became five arrows. From the tips of those arrows came countless rays of light, and on each there appeared a lotus as large as a chariot wheel, and on each lotus there was a wheel-turning monarch endowed with the seven precious things. Those rays of light illuminated the great trichiliocosm, and there was no being of the five realms whom they did not benefit.

In the abodes of the gods, that light was seen and the voice teaching the Dharma resounded, and their bodies and minds were wholly purified. Some attained the fruits from stream-entry up to arhatship. Some generated the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Some came to dwell on the ground of irreversibility.

The beings of the human realm who saw the Buddha's light and heard the Dharma he taught were overjoyed and delighted. Some attained stream-entry, once-returning or non-returning, and some went forth and attained the fruit of arhatship. Countless beings generated the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening and came to dwell on the ground of irreversibility.

Those of the realm of hungry ghosts were touched by the Buddha's light and heard the Dharma, and so all were sated with food. Free of torment in body and mind, they were content, and in all of them a compassionate mind arose. They paid honor to the Buddha, were freed from the state of hungry ghosts, and were born as gods and humans.

The beings in the animal realm, by seeing the Buddha's light, had their minds of desire and anger wholly pacified, and those blinded by delusion were awakened. All of them rejoiced in the Buddha and had faith in him, and so they were freed from the animal state and were born as gods and humans.

For the hell beings, when the Buddha's light touched them, the cold became warm and the heat became cool. Their torment of suffering was cut off, and because they were overjoyed in body and mind, they had faith in the Buddha, escaped from hell, and were born as gods and humans.

At that time King Mahākapphiṇa and the vassal kings of Golden Land, having seen such miracles, were filled with nothing but the deepest faith, and through that faith they became free of dust and free of stain, and the Dharma eye regarding phenomena was purified in them; the minds of the eighteen thousand vassal kings were satisfied.

At once the Buddha withdrew his miraculous display and showed himself once more in his own Buddha form, seated and surrounded in front and behind by the Saṅgha of monks. Then the king of Golden Land asked the Blessed One for permission to go forth, and when the Blessed One said, "Welcome!" his hair and beard fell away of themselves, and he became a monk wearing saffron robes. By taking the Dharma to heart he exhausted the defilements and became an arhat.

Then Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "What merit did this king of Golden Land make in the past, that he is of such great and high family, eminent in noble qualities, and that upon meeting the Buddha, the Blessed One, he exhausted the defilements at once?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "The fruits of beings' actions ripen in accordance with their conduct. In times long past, the buddha Kāśyapa appeared in the world. After that buddha had passed into nirvāṇa, a certain householder built a stupa, made a dwelling for the Saṅgha, and provided the four requisites for living. After a long time had passed, the stupa fell into ruin, and the requisites for living, seats, clothing and food, were also cut off.

"Then that householder's son, who was a monk, taught the Dharma to many people, restored the stupa once more, and provided requisites such as clothing, bedding, food and drink, and seats. In harmony of mind with those people, he provided the requisites for living, and they made this aspiration: 'In the future may we be mighty and rich and long-lived, may we meet a buddha who has appeared in the world, and having heard the Dharma, may our fruit not be an ordinary one!'

"Ānanda, the householder's son, the monk of that time, is Mahākapphiṇa, king of the country of Golden Land. Those who, when he taught the Dharma, restored the stupa and supplied the requisites for living, are the eighteen thousand vassal kings."

When the Blessed One had said this, many in the assembly generated the irreversible mind, and they rejoiced and were delighted.

This is the chapter on Mahākapphiṇa, the twenty-fourth.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. King Prasenajit had died, and the crown prince Vaiḍūrya had ascended the throne. Because he did not rule in accordance with the Dharma, countless people were trampled under the hooves of elephants and killed.

At that time some women of great and noble family, seeing such things, lost all delight in household life, and all of them went forth and became nuns. Of those who went forth, some were of the Śākya clan and some of the royal caste; incomparably graceful and lovely to behold, some five hundred women of great family gave up all their possessions and went forth. All the people of the country rejoiced in them and provided for their livelihood.

Those nuns said to one another, "Though we have gone forth, we have not tasted the nectar of the Dharma. Since it will not do unless we pacify desire, anger and delusion, let us go to Upanandā of the group of six and listen to the Dharma." Having said this, they went to where she was, and when they arrived they bowed and said, "Though we have gone forth, we have not tasted the nectar of the Dharma. Please teach us."

The nun of the group of six thought, "If I could turn these women back from the Dharma and from moral discipline and get hold of their robes and alms bowls, how fine that would be!" And she said this to them: "You are of great and high family. You lack nothing: the seven precious things, elephants, horses, menservants and maidservants, workers, fields, and the requisites for living. Why then have you given all of these up and entered the order of nuns, so as to take on the training of the Buddha, afflicted like this and bound up with suffering? You would do better to go back home, and, with husbands, sons and daughters, delight together as you please, enjoy yourselves and know happiness in this life as well, and give gifts as you wish. That would be good." Thus she advised them.
