The king wondered, "Has the Blessed One not tamed him by now?" and the Blessed One said this to the king: "This Aṅgulimāla has now gone forth and become an arhat. All the faults of his wrongdoing are exhausted, and he is now in his dwelling. If the king wishes to meet him, I shall have you meet him." So saying, he sent him to Aṅgulimāla's dwelling.

When the king reached the door of Aṅgulimāla's dwelling, he merely heard the sound of the monk Aṅgulimāla clearing his throat inside the dwelling, and remembering that he had formerly killed many people, he was so frightened and terrified that he fainted and fell to the ground. After a long while he got up again, went to where the Blessed One was, and asked the Blessed One what this meant. The Blessed One said this to the great king: "Great king, listen well. Long ago in the past, here in Jambudvīpa, in a land called Vārāṇasī, there was an exceedingly venomous bird. It ate nothing but venomous insects for food. Its body too was so venomous that one could not bear even to come near and touch it. Wherever it flew or went, all the beings beneath it fell to the ground in dread, and all were terrified. At that time that venomous bird went into a grove, perched in a tree, and let out a cry. At that time there was a king of elephants at the foot of that tree, and merely hearing the cry of the venomous bird, it fainted and fell to the ground. Great king, the venomous bird at that time, on that occasion, is the present Aṅgulimāla. That king of elephants was the king himself."

"Blessed One, this Aṅgulimāla, wrathful and fierce, killed so many beings, and the Blessed One has established him in virtue." The Blessed One declared, "Great king, it is not only now in the present time that the monk Aṅgulimāla killed many people and I tamed him. Long ago in the past, too, Aṅgulimāla killed all these people, and I tamed him, and he came to set his mind on virtue."

The king again asked, "Blessed One, please explain in detail how in the past, too, he killed these people, and the matter of his cruelty." The Blessed One declared:

"Long ago in the past, countless eons ago, here in Jambudvīpa, in the land called Vārāṇasī, there was a king called Balamadar. At that time the king went with his fourfold army to amuse himself in a forest. He came upon a deer and ran in pursuit of it, and so the king, alone and separated from his retinue, reached a dense thicket. Then, being weary, the king dismounted from his horse and sat down. In that dense thicket there lived a lioness. Tormented by desire, she was roaming about, and in the forest she came upon the king sitting alone. Desire arose in the lioness all the more strongly; she approached the king, raised her tail, and pressed herself upon him. The king, recognizing that desire had arisen in her, thought, 'This is a fierce beast of prey, and she has the power to kill me. If I do not do as she wishes, it will cost me my life.' So, though afraid, he had intercourse with the lioness. After the lioness had gone away, his attendants also arrived, met the king, and escorted him back to the palace.

"The lioness then became pregnant, and when her months were complete a son was born. His body and form were in every way like a human's; only his feet were speckled. The lioness, remembering that he was the king's son, carried him on her back and brought him to the king. The king, knowing him to be his own son, raised him, and because his feet were speckled he named him Kalmāṣapāda, 'Speckled Feet.' Raised to full growth, he became exceedingly brave and bold. After the king passed away, he obtained the royal power and took two queens: one queen was of the kṣatriya caste, and one was of the brahmin caste.

"Later Kalmāṣapāda went to stroll in the pleasure grove, and he gave this instruction to the two queens: 'Whichever of you two queens comes quickly after me, whichever meets me first, with her I shall play and take my pleasure for a whole day. The one who comes later shall not be allowed to meet me.' After the king had gone, the two queens adorned themselves with ornaments, had their mounts made ready, and set out at the same time. Along the way there was a shrine of a god, and the queen of the brahmin caste got down from her mount and paid homage to it. Though she then went on quickly, she arrived late. So the king, as he had said before, did not keep her company.

"Then anger arose in that queen, and she was furious with the god: 'While I was paying homage to you I was delayed, and the king was not pleased with me. If you, god, truly had any power at all, why did you not act as my protector and refuge?' Utterly furious and enraged, she resolved in her mind to do it harm. When the king came back to the palace, the queen made this request of him: 'Grant me the authority to do whatever I please for one day.' The king gave the order, 'So be it, it is granted.' She turned out a great many people, demolished the god's shrine, and leveled the ground as well. Then the god who guarded that shrine was exceedingly displeased. Enraged, it resolved to do harm to the king, and it went to the palace, but the god who guarded the palace did not let it in.

"At that time a sage was living on the mountain called Ṛṣipatana. King Kalmāṣapāda made offerings to that sage, so every day the sage flew through the sky and came to the palace. He did not want fine food; he ate only ordinary food and then went back. One day the god knew that the sage was not coming. Emanating itself in the body of the sage, it meant to go into the palace, but the god who guarded the palace recognized it and did not let it in. It called from the gate, 'They will not let me come inside!' The king heard this and commanded, 'The sage is calling from outside; let him in at once!' The god who guarded the palace, in keeping with the king's command, did not hold it back and let it in.

"When it had come inside and sat on the sage's seat, the king offered it food prepared just as the sage always ate. Then the emanated sage would not eat the food and spoke thus to the king: 'This food is extremely poor. I eat meat and fish.' The king replied, 'Great sage, until now you have eaten ordinary food, and so meat and fish were not prepared.' The emanated sage said, 'From now on I do not want this poor food. Prepare meat and fish!' And having said this, it left. Then the king, as the emanated sage had said, had meat and fish prepared, and the next day offered them to the true sage when he came.

"Anger arose in that sage, and he was furious with the king. The king said to the sage, 'Great sage, when you came yesterday, did you not command me to prepare food like this?' The sage said, 'Yesterday I was ill, so I abstained from food for the day and did not come. Who gave such a command? You have done this out of contempt for me. King, for twelve years may you eat nothing but human flesh!' And having said this, he went away.

"Then one day the king's cook had received no meat, and in that moment he could find no other meat. He came upon the corpse of a child and thought, 'This meat will do as well.' He cut off the head, hands and feet, cooked it, seasoned it with various spices, and served it to the king. The king ate that meat and found it far more delicious than the meat he had had before. He asked the cook, 'The meat until now had no flavor like this. This meat is especially delicious; what meat is it?' The cook was frightened, threw himself prostrate on the ground, and said to the king, 'If the king will not punish me, I will tell the truth.' The king commanded, 'Tell the truth, and I will not punish you.'

The cook said, "We were pressed for time and could find no other meat. I found the corpse of a small child who had died, cooked it and served it, and that is what Your Majesty has tasted." The king commanded, "This meat is especially delicious. From now on always prepare meat like this." The cook replied, "Before, I happened to find the corpse of a child who had died and cooked it; beyond that, none is to be found." The king commanded, "From now on, take them secretly, in a way that you can keep hidden and others do not notice. I have the authority." The cook, as the king had commanded, stole children secretly at night and killed them, and each following day he cooked them.

At that time most of the people of that city were wailing, and from one to another the cry went round, "A child is lost!" The ministers gathered and deliberated over why this was happening, and at night they set a watch at the crossroads of the streets. Watching, they caught the king's cook stealing small children and carrying them off. They bound him, led him before the king, and said, "Again and again, so many children have been lost!" The king said nothing at all. Though they petitioned him in this way three times, he said nothing, so they said, "We have now caught the one who keeps committing this crime. If his guilt is evident, a sentence ought to be passed; it is not fitting to say nothing." The king said, "I ordered it."

Then the ministers were displeased; they held it as a grievance and went their separate ways. When they had gone outside, they said to one another, "The king is an enemy who has eaten the children of all of us and of others. We cannot share one realm with a king who eats human flesh. An enemy who has become so harmful ought to be done away with." So, all agreeing in council, they plotted to kill the king.

Outside that city there was a pleasure grove, and in it a pond in which the king went to bathe every day. The ministers hid a body of troops in the pleasure grove, and when the king was bathing in the pond, the many troops surrounded and seized him to kill him. The king was frightened and said to the troops, "Why have you surrounded me and come to seize me?" The ministers said to the king, "One who is called a king is one who nourishes his many subjects. Because the king has eaten all his subjects' children, all the subjects have been left without security. Since this suffering is unbearable, we shall now kill you, king." The king said, "I was indeed at fault in acting this way, but from now on I will not do so. May I be released this time, or not?" The ministers said to him, "Even if black snow should fall from the sky, or a black snake grow on your head, there will be no release. Make your final vow!"

Hearing the words the ministers had spoken, King Kalmāṣapāda thought, "Now I shall certainly be parted from life," and again he said to the ministers, "Since you are now determined to kill me, let me set my mind at ease for just a moment. Wait a little first." Then the ministers waited a short while and let him collect his mind, and the king made this resolve in his mind: "By the power of whatever virtue I have done until now, of having ruled the kingdom in accordance with the Dharma, and of all the merit there is in my having given to the sage, may I become a rākṣasa who flies through the sky!" So he dedicated it. As soon as he had spoken these words and made the dedication, he became a rākṣasa, rose up into the sky above, and said to the ministers, "Though you conspired by your power to kill me, by my merit I have survived. From now on you will have to endure it: I shall eat all your dear and cherished wives and children." Having said this, he flew off and landed on a mountain, and from then on he came among humans to eat people. Many people, terrified, were constantly fleeing and hiding.

Then the rākṣasa ate people, and gathering many rākṣasas about him, he came to have a large retinue and many servants. The rākṣasas of his retinue said to the rākṣasa Kalmāṣapāda, "Since we have become your retinue and servants, give us a great feast." The rākṣasa Kalmāṣapāda agreed to this, and resolving to kill five hundred kings for the feast, he went about seizing kings and put them in a rock cave. He found four hundred and ninety-nine, and while one king was still lacking, those kings in the rock cave said to one another, "Now we are lost, and there is no one to be our refuge. If Sutasoma, who is endowed with strength, came here, we might perhaps escape. Let us ask the rākṣasa to bring Sutasoma here." Having agreed on this, they said to the rākṣasa king, "King, when you hold your feast, a feast made by killing only us human kings will not be a fine one. There is one called Sutasoma who has great virtues. If you get him, then it will be a great feast." The rākṣasa king said, "As you please," and flew through the sky to go and seize Sutasoma.

At that time Sutasoma was going outside the city with young maidens to bathe, and on the way he met a brahmin who was begging. He undertook to give to him, and said to the brahmin, "I am going to bathe; when I come back I will give it to you." Having said this he went to bathe, and while he was bathing the rākṣasa king came and carried him off to the rock cave. Then Sutasoma was unhappy and wept, and Kalmāṣapāda said to him, "It is said that you are the best of men, with virtues surpassing others. A great being fears nothing whatever, so why are you unhappy, weeping like a small child?"

Sutasoma said to the rākṣasa, "It is not that I am attached to my body, nor that I am unhappy from fear of a rākṣasa. From the very beginning I have never told a lie. But just now, on my way to bathe, I met a brahmin who was begging, and I told him, 'When I have bathed and come back, I will give you a gift.' I went to bathe, and you, king, have carried me off here. So I am unhappy, thinking that I have told a lie. It is not that I am attached to body and life. King, out of kindness to me, grant me a respite of seven days; when I have given the gift to that brahmin, I will come back again to die."

The rākṣasa said, "If I release you now, will you be able to come back?" He said, "Even if I do not come, you have the power to take me." Having said this, the rākṣasa let him go.

As soon as Sutasoma had returned home, he found the brahmin still there, and being overjoyed, he gave the brahmin a gift. The brahmin, knowing that before long Sutasoma would go to be parted from life, and that if he were attached to his realm on going back he would be unhappy, spoke these verses to him:

When the end of the eon arrives,
Even the sky and the rivers blaze fiercely;
Mount Meru the towering, and the oceans too,
All of them turn to ashes and dust.
Gods, nāgas and asuras
All perish and come to ruin;
When even heaven and earth cease to be,
How could a kingdom be permanent?
Of birth, aging, sickness and death,
The round of saṃsāra knows no ending.
When for a while one's desires are not fulfilled,
One is harmed by sorrow.
Desire is the greatest of faults;
The ill does not come from elsewhere.
Since the three realms are filled with suffering,
How could the rule of a land be relied upon?
What exists is from the very first nonexistent,
Coming about through causes and conditions.
Where there is flourishing, there is decline;
What is true will surely turn false.
Ordinary beings, from first to last,
All dwell within illusion.
Just as the three realms are empty,
So too is the rule of a land.
Consciousness has no form;
It dwells among four venomous snakes.
What is born of ignorance
One clings to as a place of bliss.
Form has no permanent self;
Mind has no permanent home.
When even body and mind are parted,
How could the rule of a land remain?

Then Sutasoma, having heard the verses spoken, rejoiced as he pondered their meaning in his mind. He installed the regent as king, took leave of the ministers, and made ready to go back as he had promised. The ministers said to him, "Do not fear King Kalmāṣapāda. We have considered it: if an iron house is built to protect the king and the king stays inside it, then however brave Kalmāṣapāda may be, he will be able to do nothing."

The king commanded the ministers and the many people, "Whoever is born a human being must have the conscience not to tell lies. If one tells a lie, there is no joy even in being alive. Better to die, as I first promised; I have no wish to live by telling a lie." So he spoke of the many kinds of good that come from having a conscience and the many faults of telling lies, and the ministers, overcome with grief, were left without words. All of them escorted the king, all of them wailed, and they fainted and collapsed.

While Sutasoma was on the road, the rākṣasa Kalmāṣapāda thought, "It is time for Sutasoma to come here." He came out onto the summit of the mountain and looked, and he saw Sutasoma far off on the road, coming to give up his life. Then, when he had come near, the rākṣasa looked and saw that he had a radiance even more joyful than before, and he said to the king, "What joy can there be for those who are facing the loss of their life? In giving up your living, do you not grudge even your life? What benefit did you find by going to your land, that you are so joyful?" So he asked.

Sutasoma said, "Great king, you were considerate and granted my life a long respite, so I did not go against what I had promised before. I also gave a great gift, and I heard the sublime Dharma and took it to heart. My wishes are fulfilled, so even if I give up my life here, I am more joyful than if I lived."

King Kalmāṣapāda commanded, "Tell me too what Dharma you heard." Sutasoma recited the verses just as he had heard them, and besides that, with skillful means he also spoke at length of the evil and the wrongdoing of the act of killing, and of the ripening of nonvirtuous actions. He also taught at length the virtues of not taking life, vast merit, and the mind of loving-kindness. The rākṣasa Kalmāṣapāda was overjoyed and delighted; he bowed with the crown of his head, and as he had been taught, from then on he gave up taking life. He also sent the princes he had put in the rock cave back to their own lands. Then Sutasoma arrayed his troops, welcomed King Kalmāṣapāda, and installed him as king once more. Since the twelve years of the curse the sage had cast on him were now complete, from then on he no longer ate human flesh, and he ruled as king as before.

"Great king, Sutasoma at that time, on that occasion, is now myself. That King Kalmāṣapāda is now Aṅgulimāla. The people whom Kalmāṣapāda ate during those twelve years are the people Aṅgulimāla has killed. These people, wherever they were born, were always killed by Aṅgulimāla, and I, in all those births, established them in virtue. If, as I clearly remember, I was able to turn him back from killing in the past, when I was still an ordinary person, then now that I have become fully awakened, perfect in qualities and with wrongdoing completely exhausted, what need is there to mention my taming these?"

The king again said to the Blessed One, "What did these people do in the past, that in all their births they were always killed by Aṅgulimāla?" The Blessed One said to the king, "Great king, listen well, and I shall explain. Long ago in the past, countless eons ago, here in Jambudvīpa, in the land of Vārāṇasī, there was a king called Baladar. That king had two sons, handsome, beautiful to behold, and skilled. His youngest son thought, 'If my father passes away, my elder brother will become king.'"

"'Since I am the younger, I will not obtain the kingship. If, born a human being, I do not obtain the kingship, what good is it to live as a common man? I shall go into solitude and seek the path of a sage.' Thinking this, he went before the king and said to his father the king, 'I wish to seek the path of a sage in solitude. If you hold me dear, do not turn me back from this intention.' He asked again and again, and the king granted it as he wished, and he went off into solitude.

"Then, after many years had passed, his father the king passed away, and the elder brother ascended the throne. The elder brother, having taken the throne, was not long after struck by illness and passed away, and there was no one to continue the line. The ministers gathered and deliberated over what to do, and one minister said, 'The great king had a younger son who asked leave of the king and is now living on a mountain as a sage. Let us invite him and install him as king.' The ministers agreed, 'If there certainly is such a one, let us invite him now and install him as king,' and they went to the mountain to look for the prince.

"When they met the prince, they said, 'We have no king. You must be our king, so let us go.' The sage said, 'These words are frightening. Here in solitude I am perfectly happy and have no sorrow at all. Worldly people are by their nature untamed, and they even kill their kings. I cannot do it.' The ministers said, 'The royal line has been broken and there is no one to continue the line. You alone, sage, belong to the royal line. All the people of the land have been left without a protector, so out of compassion for the many beings, we beg you to come and be king.' The sage could not refuse, and as the ministers asked, he came back to the land and became king.

"That sage had from his youth not been touched by desire, but while ruling the realm he gradually became involved with women, and so his desire grew strong. Day and night he gave himself recklessly to desire, and he issued this decree: 'When any of my subjects who have daughters give them in marriage, they are to send them as brides only after they have first met with me.' So he decreed. And he behaved improperly with every lovely and attractive woman in the land.

"At that time a certain woman came out naked before a great gathering of people and urinated. Everyone laughed and reviled her: 'What is this shameless woman doing?' The woman said, 'What shame is there in a woman urinating in front of women? If you are not ashamed to urinate in front of one another, what shame is there for me in urinating among others who are just like women?' The many people said, 'What do you mean by saying this?' The woman said, 'In this land the king alone is a man; all the other people are surely women. If you were all men, it would not be right for the king to do such improper things.'

"Then the many people were ashamed and embarrassed, and they deliberated, 'What this woman says is true. Since this is not right, this king must be done away with.' All agreeing in council, they resolved to kill the king when he went to bathe in the pleasure grove. Then they hid a body of troops in the pleasure grove, and when the king came to the pond, all of them turned against him, seized him and set about killing him. The king was frightened and said, 'Men, what are you doing?' The ministers said, 'The king has not acted in accordance with the Dharma. Intoxicated with desire, he has defiled the families of all the people of the land and brought them to disgrace. We can no longer bear it, so we will do away with you, and only then will we seek a wise king.'

"Hearing these words, the king was frightened and said to the ministers, 'It is true that I have done wrong until now, but if from now on I do not act this way, may I be let go?' The ministers said, 'Even if black snow should fall from the sky, or a black snake grow on your head, it will not be granted. Speak no more!' So they commanded. Hearing these words, the king thought, 'Now they will certainly kill me,' and in anger he said to the ministers, 'Before, I lived on the mountain, taking no delight in the world, but you forcibly made me king, and now you kill me. Wherever I am born, may I meet you, and may I kill you too!' So he vowed. But though he made this vow, they did not spare him, and they killed him.

"Great king, the king who was a sage, at that time, on that occasion, is now Aṅgulimāla. The many people who at that time agreed together and killed the king are these people who have been killed by Aṅgulimāla, and so they were always killed by Aṅgulimāla."

The king set his knees on the ground and said to the Blessed One, "Since this monk Aṅgulimāla killed so many people, will he experience the ripening of it even after attaining the fruit in this way?" The Blessed One declared, "Great king, when evil is done, its ripening is certainly experienced. Even now this monk, in his dwelling, has the fire of hell blazing from every pore of his body, and he is experiencing unbearable suffering."

Then the Blessed One, so that the great assembly would understand that when evil deeds are done their evil certainly ripens, said to a certain monk, "Monk, go and take a key, and put it into the keyhole of the door of the monk Aṅgulimāla." The monk, as he was told, took a key and put it into the keyhole of the door, and at once it melted. The monk was terrified, and he went back and came before the Blessed One and reported what had happened. The Blessed One said to that monk, "This is the ripening of evil deeds." When he had said this, the king and the assembly came to have faith.

Then the venerable Ānanda said to the Blessed One:

"What good did the monk Aṅgulimāla do in the past, that he came to have strength equal to the strength of a great wrestler, great skill, and swiftness like a bird? What good did he do, that he met the Blessed One and came to be freed from saṃsāra? Out of compassion for the great assembly, please explain this."

The Blessed One declared, "Listen well. Long ago in the past, in the time of the Buddha Kāśyapa, there was a monk who did menial labor. He had loaded up a burden of grain and was on his way when he was caught on the road by rain. There was nowhere to take shelter, and the grain, together with its containers, was all soaked. At that time the monk wanted to go quickly, but because the load had soaked up water his going was slow and he could not hurry, and so he made this aspiration: 'In the future may I have strength equal to a thousand men, and a body light and swift like a bird in flight. In the future, when the Buddha Śākyamuni comes into the world, may I meet him and be freed from saṃsāra!' So he made his aspiration.

"Ānanda, do not think it was anyone else. The monk who did menial labor at that time is the present monk Aṅgulimāla. Because at that time he had gone forth, kept moral discipline, and done the work of the Saṅgha, then in accordance with his aspiration, wherever he was born he was handsome and had great strength and skill. Because he aspired to meet me, he has been freed from saṃsāra."

Then Ānanda, the monks, and the king's great retinue, having heard the Blessed One speak of the fruit of karmic ripening, all aroused diligence, and by bringing the Four Truths to mind, some became stream-enterers, some once-returners, some non-returners, and some arhats. Some generated the roots of virtue of a pratyekabuddha. Some set their minds on unsurpassed awakening and came to abide on the irreversible ground. All of them aspired to virtue of body, speech and mind, and they rejoiced and delighted in what the Blessed One had said.

This is the chapter on Ahiṃsaka Aṅgulimāla, the thirty-sixth.

Section eleven.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, the park of Anāthapiṇḍada.

At that time there was in that land a woman called Upanandā, destitute and dependent on begging for her living. Seeing the king, the ministers and many people each making offerings to the Buddha and the Saṅgha, the woman thought, "What evil did I do in the past, that I have been born poor like this and of low birth? Now, though I have met a field of merit, I have no seed to sow." She was utterly dejected and filled with remorse. Begging for a whole day, from morning to night, she got no more than a single coin. Taking that one coin, she went to the house of an oil seller to buy some oil. The householder who sold oil said, "Buying oil with a single coin is far too little. What will you do with it?" Upanandā told him truthfully, and the oil seller, moved by compassion, gave her extra oil.

Upanandā, having obtained the oil, was overjoyed. She went to the vihāra, saying, "I shall offer it to the Blessed One," set up a lamp before the Buddha, and made this aspiration: "I am now destitute, and with this little lamp I make an offering to the Buddha. By this merit, in the future may I possess the lamp of wisdom and clear away the stain of darkness of all sentient beings." Having made this aspiration, she went back to her dwelling.

That night the other lamps burned out, but Upanandā's lamp alone burned until dawn. At that time it was Maudgalyāyana's turn to keep the time, and when dawn came he went to collect the oil vessels. One lamp was especially bright; its oil and wick were undiminished, as if it had just been newly lit. He thought, "There is no use in a lamp lit in the daytime; I shall put it out." He tried to put it out, but it did not go out. Then he tried to put it out by waving his hand, but it did not go out and was not diminished. Then he tried to put it out with his robe, and still it did not go out.

The Blessed One saw this and said to Maudgalyāyana, "Though you intend to put out this lamp, you will not put it out. It is not something a śrāvaka can move or shake. Even if you poured on it the waters of the four great oceans, or blew on it with a hurricane, it would not go out. For this lamp was offered by one who generated the great intention of accomplishing vast benefit for all people."

When the Blessed One had said this, Upanandā too came before the Blessed One and bowed her head at his feet. Then the Blessed One prophesied, "In the future, after two eons have passed, you will become fully awakened. Your name will be Light of the Lamp, and you will be endowed with the ten masteries." Then Upanandā, hearing the Blessed One's prophecy, was overjoyed. Setting both knees on the ground, she asked to go forth, and the Blessed One granted her going forth.

The venerable Ānanda and Maudgalyāyana, seeing that the poor woman had received a prophecy and gone forth, set both knees on the ground, joined their palms, and said to the Blessed One, "What deed did this Upanandā do in the past, that for so long she has been destitute and unable even to find a livelihood? What did she do, that she has met the Blessed One and come to be honored by the fourfold assembly?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Long ago in the past, the Buddha Kāśyapa came into the world. At that time the wife of a householder invited the Buddha together with the Saṅgha of monks, but a poor woman had invited him first, and he consented to go to her. That poor woman had attained the state of non-return. At that time the householder's wife, being powerful, rich, and of noble family, scorned the poor woman's earlier invitation and said:

"Why does the Blessed One not come to my invitation, which I made first, but go instead to the invitation of a beggar woman?" So she spoke harsh words, and because she scorned a noble one, for five hundred lifetimes she was born into poor and destitute families that depended on begging for their livelihood. Yet later that same day, because she made offerings to the Buddha and the Saṅgha with faith and rejoiced, she has now met the Buddha, gone forth, and received a prophecy."

At that time the many in the assembly were overjoyed at what the Buddha had said.

Then the king, the ministers, and many people heard that the poor woman had offered a lamp to the Buddha and had been prophesied to buddhahood. All of them honored her, and they prepared and presented to her, in full sufficiency, the four kinds of supreme and excellent requisites for living. Then all the youths and maidens of that land, both of high and of low birth, prepared lamps of fragrant scent and offered them to the Blessed One in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta. The many people who offered lamps filled the whole of the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta with lamplight, so that on every side it was like the stars appearing in the sky. They offered them day after day, and in this way they kept offering them without interruption for seven days and nights.

Then Ānanda, overjoyed and filled with wonder, praised him, saying, "So great are the qualities of the Blessed One!" and he said to the Blessed One, "Blessed One, what deeds did you do in the past, that you have come to have such immeasurable lamps as these?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Long ago in the past, here in Jambudvīpa, there was a king called Pasili who ruled over some eighty-four thousand lesser kings. The king's queen bore a son whose body was the color of gold, who was endowed with the thirty-two major marks and the eighty excellent minor marks, and on the crown of whose head was a jewel of brilliant color that dazzled the eyes of anyone who looked at it. A sign reader was summoned to examine the child's good and bad features and give him a name. When the sign reader looked at him, he took the marks to be wondrous, raised his hands, and exclaimed, 'Excellent indeed!' He said, 'This prince has no equal among gods and humans in the world. If he stays in the household, he will become a wheel-turning king. If he goes forth, he will attain complete awakening.' Then the sign reader asked the king, 'When this prince was first born, what wondrous and marvelous omens appeared?' The king replied, 'When this prince was born, there was an exceedingly brilliant jewel on the crown of his head.' And so they named the prince Ratnaśikhin.

"Later, when he had grown up, he went forth, applied himself earnestly to awakening, attained perfect buddhahood, and worked for the benefit of countless sentient beings. At that time the king, his father, invited the Buddha together with the Saṅgha of monks for three months and served them a feast of offerings. Among them was a monk called Āryamitra, who had undertaken to be the patron providing lamp oil for those three months. Day after day he went begging through the villages and into the city, and asked merchants, householders, and many people for butter, sesame oil, lamp oil, and wicks.

"Now that king had a daughter called Muni. From the upper story of the palace she saw the monk going begging through the villages every day and toiling, and she felt compassion for him. She sent a man to ask him, 'Venerable one, what are you trying to accomplish, that you are always toiling like this?' The monk replied, 'I have undertaken to be the patron providing the lamps for the Buddha and the Saṅgha for three months. That is why I go begging through the villages and collect from the lay followers butter, sesame oil, wicks for the lamp oil, and the other things needed to set up the lamps.' When this message reached the princess, she was overjoyed, and she sent word to the monk Āryamitra: 'From now on you need not keep going about like this. I will provide the requisites for the lamp oil; come and take them.' The monk said he would do so, and from then on she always prepared the lamp oil, the sesame oil, and all the requisites for the lamps and had them brought to the monastery.

"In this way the monk Āryamitra offered lamps day after day, and because he generated the vast aspiration to benefit all, the Blessed One gave him a prophecy: that in the future, after countless eons had passed, he would attain complete awakening, and that his name would be Dīpaṃkara; and he was prophesied as endowed with the thirty epithets. When the king's daughter Muni heard that the monk Āryamitra had received a prophecy, she thought, 'It was I who provided the lamps offered to the Buddha. That monk did no more than encourage it, and yet he has received a prophecy, while I have not.' With this thought she went to where the Blessed One was and told him plainly just how it was. The Blessed One prophesied, 'Princess Muni, in the future, after ninety-one countless eons have passed, you will attain complete awakening, and your name will be Śākyamuni,' and he prophesied her as endowed with the ten epithets. When the king's daughter had thus heard her prophecy from the Buddha, great joy arose in her. She bowed her head at the Buddha's feet and asked to go forth. The Blessed One allowed her to go forth, and she applied herself to diligence without interruption."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "The monk Āryamitra of that time and occasion was the past buddha Dīpaṃkara. The king's daughter Muni is I myself now. Because I offered lamps in the past, throughout countless eons I have enjoyed merit in the worlds of gods and humans, and my form has been finer and more excellent than that of others; and now that I have attained perfect buddhahood, I have come to have many lamps."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, among the many in the assembly some attained the fruits from the first fruit up to the fourth. Some generated roots of virtue toward becoming pratyekabuddhas. Some generated the aspiration for unsurpassed awakening. The venerable Ānanda and the many in the assembly rejoiced and delighted in what the Blessed One had said.

This is the thirty-seventh chapter, the chapter of the maiden Upanandā.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying in Rājagṛha, on Vulture Peak Mountain. At that time there lived in that land a householder called Śirici, who was extremely rich, possessed of great wealth, and whose treasuries were filled with the seven kinds of precious things. He took a wife from a family of equal rank, and some time later his wife became pregnant. When the months were complete she gave birth to a son, so lovely and handsome that he had no equal in the world. His parents loved him dearly and summoned a sign reader to examine his marks. When he looked at the marks, the sign reader said, "This boy is endowed with the marks of merit, and he will make the family flourish." The boy's parents were all the more delighted, and they said to the sign reader, "Give the boy a name." The sign reader asked, "Since this child came to be, what omen or marvel has occurred?" They replied, "There has been nothing else, except that the boy's mother, who was not eloquent before, has become eloquent ever since this boy came to be." And so they named the boy Vaśicira.

He was nourished and raised, and when he had grown up he was intelligent and accomplished in many skills. One day, as he was going about playing with his young friends, he saw in the house of a low-caste family a girl who was lovely, graceful, and auspicious in bearing. He became attached to her and thought of taking her as his wife. He came back home and said to his parents, "Take that girl as a wife for me." His parents said, "We are of a noble family, and that girl is of a low and base caste. How could we take her as your wife?" The boy was very unhappy, and said to his parents once again, "Do not look at caste; take that very girl as my wife, because I love her. If you do not take her as my wife, I will die." His parents could not refuse their son, and so they sent someone to ask for the girl.

The girl's parents said, "You are of a noble family, and we are of a low and base caste, so we are not a fitting match for you. Why do you ask for our daughter?" Yet the boy remained attached to the girl, and once again he sent someone to ask for her. Then the girl's parents said, "If the man who would be my son-in-law has a variety of skills, knows every kind of song, dance, and entertainment, and knows everything that pleases the king, then we will make the match." Though he was ashamed to train in the work of music and the like, the boy strove at it, and before long he had trained until he had mastered all of it.

Some time later the king held a festival. He gathered all the most skillful performers, and they put on all kinds of entertainments, such as tree climbing, leaping, and rope walking. The householder's son also came there, climbed onto the rope, and performed the rope walk, running along the rope. But the king was distracted and did not see it, so he commanded, "Do it again now." The boy, though he was weary, performed the rope walk again, and when he had gone about halfway along the rope he fell from it. Terrified, he cried out, "I have no refuge!"

At that moment Maudgalyāyana came there and caught him before he hit the ground, and said to him, "Will you now go forth, so that you do not lose your body and life? Or will you fall to the ground and take the girl as your wife?" He replied, "If only my life is not lost, I do not want a wife." Then Maudgalyāyana conjured up level ground in the sky and set him down safely. The boy was freed from fear, and since he had been set down safely on the ground, he was overjoyed and followed Maudgalyāyana to where the Blessed One was. When he arrived, he bowed his head at the feet of the Blessed One and made offerings to the Buddha.

The Blessed One then taught him at length: a talk on generosity, a talk on moral discipline, a talk on rebirth as a god in the heavens, on the impurity of sense desires, and that going forth is bliss. His mind was liberated, and he attained the first fruit. He asked the Blessed One for permission to go forth and asked to train in the sacred Dharma, and the Blessed One granted it. His hair and beard fell away of their own accord and he became a monk. Applying himself earnestly to the practice of a monk, he exhausted all defilements and became an arhat.

Then the venerable Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "Please explain what past karma connected this monk with that girl, so that he became attached to her to the point of risking his life; what root of virtue he generated toward Maudgalyāyana, so that his life was saved; and through what condition he finally became an arhat."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Long ago, immeasurable eons in the past, here in the land of Vārāṇasī, a son was born to a householder, lovely and good beyond compare. At that time a man came from the ocean to that householder's house and presented the householder with a single bird's egg. The householder took it in his hand, and before long the egg hatched and a single fledgling emerged. The householder gave the fledgling to his son, and as it grew up the two became attached to each other. Whenever the householder's son went off here and there to see the shows, he would sit on the bird's back, and the bird would carry him, flying through the sky. At nightfall he would come back home, and in this way they roamed about day after day.

"After a long time had passed, he heard one day that a festival was being held in the land of another king. The boy mounted the bird and went to that other land to see the shows. He left the bird on the top of a tree, and the boy went in to watch the shows. On the way to the shows he met the king's daughter and became attached to her. He slept with the princess, and afterward the secret came out. The king had him seized and ordered him to be killed. The householder's son then said to the king, 'What need is there to kill me with a weapon? I ask to climb a tree myself and to die by throwing myself down from the treetop.' The king granted this, and the boy climbed the tree, mounted the bird, and flew away. In this way the bird saved his life.

"Ānanda, the householder's son of that time and occasion was Vaśicira. The king's daughter was this girl. The bird was Maudgalyāyana. In the past, too, he saved him when his life was about to end because of desire; and now again, when his life was about to be lost under the power of desire, Maudgalyāyana saved him."

"As for how this monk came to be intelligent and to attain the fruit free of defilement: long ago in the past, here in Vārāṇasī, a pratyekabuddha came begging alms from a householder. The householder offered him alms and asked him to teach the Dharma. Then the pratyekabuddha said, 'I am not able to teach the Dharma,' and he tossed his alms bowl into the sky and flew away after it. Then the householder thought, 'That person has great miraculous power, yet he cannot teach the sacred Dharma.' And he made this aspiration: 'In the future may I meet a noble one, and may I surpass this person, becoming tireless in teaching the sacred Dharma.' Because of that condition he is intelligent and has attained the fruit of an arhat."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, the many in the assembly rejoiced. Some attained the fruit of stream-entry, of once-return, of non-return, and of arhatship. Some generated roots of virtue toward becoming pratyekabuddhas, and some generated the aspiration for awakening, and they rejoiced in what the Blessed One had said.

This is the thirty-eighth chapter, the chapter of Vaśicira.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time there lived in that land a brahmin called Piṇḍola. That brahmin's wife was very ugly and blind in both eyes. The blind woman had no sons but had seven daughters, and they were utterly destitute. The brahmin gave each of his daughters away in marriage, and later the sons-in-law would gather at the house. The brahmin's wife was an angry and fierce woman, and she grew furious when the daughters and sons-in-law came, so the daughters in turn abused their mother.

At that time the fields were ripe for harvest, so the brahmin borrowed an ox from a neighbor and went off to the field, but because he did not guard it well, it was lost. Then Piṇḍola thought, "What wrong did I do in the past? When I come home my wife always scolds me, and my seven daughters and the sons-in-law give me no place to sit. Now I borrowed my neighbor's ox and it has been lost, and though I search for it I cannot find it. What am I to do?" He sat there, stricken with grief and despair, and from a distance he saw the Tathāgata seated at the foot of a tree, his senses all at peace, dwelling in bliss.

The brahmin, leaning his chin on his staff, gazed at him across the distance of an earshot, and thought again, "The ascetic Gautama lives in perfect bliss. He has no wife to scold him, no wicked daughters or sons-in-law to torment him, no fields ripe for harvest, and no need to borrow an ox. He has no sorrow."

The Blessed One, knowing his thoughts with his mind, said to him, "It is as you have thought. I have no faults or wrongdoing at all. I have no wicked wife, let alone one who abuses and scolds me. I have no seven daughters to harm me, and no sons-in-law. I have no fields ripe for harvest, and I have no sorrow over a lost ox. Would you like to go forth?"

The brahmin said to the Blessed One, "Now I regard my home as no better than a charnel ground, and I think of my wife and daughters as no better than enemies. If the Blessed One permits me to go forth, I will go forth." The Blessed One said, "Welcome!" and his hair and beard fell away of their own accord, and he became a monk. Then the Blessed One taught him the Dharma suited to him, and right there all his stains were exhausted and he became an arhat.

Then Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "Excellent! The Blessed One's work for the benefit of sentient beings is inconceivable. What good did this brahmin do in the past, that he is free of all fault and wrongdoing, and, as clean white cloth easily takes a dye, so he has come to achieve what is beneficial and virtuous?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "It is not only now that I have helped this brahmin and brought him to happiness. In the past too he was freed from all his troubles, and I brought him to happiness."

Ānanda said, "Please explain in detail how you brought this brahmin to happiness in the past."

The Blessed One said, "Listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you."

Ānanda replied, "So I wish to hear," and the Blessed One spoke to Ānanda as follows: "Long ago, countless and immeasurable eons in the past, there was a king called Sundara, who ruled according to the Dharma. At that time there lived in that land a brahmin called Daṇḍin, who was utterly destitute, without food or clothing. He borrowed an ox from a householder, and after working it during the day he led the ox to that householder's house. There the householder was eating his meal. Daṇḍin let the ox into the house, but the ox went out by another door and was lost. When the householder had eaten his meal and got up, he did not see the ox there, and he asked Daṇḍin, 'Where is the ox?' He replied, 'I let it into your house.' The householder said, 'You have lost my ox; give it back to me.' Daṇḍin said, 'I did not lose it.' Then the two of them said, 'Let us go together before the king, and he will determine which of us is right and which is wrong,' and the two of them set off.

"On the way another man's mare ran off, and that man called to Daṇḍin, 'Don't let the mare get away!' Daṇḍin picked up a stone and threw it, and it struck the horse on the leg and broke its leg."

The man said, "You have killed my horse, so give me a horse." "Why should I give you a horse?" He said, "Come along. Let us go before the king, and he will come to a judgment on us." So saying, they went on. Then Daṇḍin tried to run away. He leapt over a wall, and at its foot a weaver was sitting weaving; Daṇḍin fell on top of him, and the weaver died. The weaver's wife seized Daṇḍin and said, "You have killed my husband, so give me a husband." He said, "How can I give you a husband?" She said, "Come along. Let us go together before the king, and he will judge us." So they went on.

Partway along the road there was a deep river, and a carpenter was coming toward them through the water, carrying an adze in his mouth. Daṇḍin asked him, "How deep is the water?" As he answered, "The water is deep," the adze fell into the water and could not be found. The carpenter seized Daṇḍin and said, "You have made me drop my adze into the water." Daṇḍin said, "I did not make you drop it." "Come along. Let us go before the king, and he will judge us," he said, and they went on.

Daṇḍin, weary in body, went into a tavern and asked for beer. The woman who sold beer had given birth to a son, and the child had been laid down covered with a cloth. Daṇḍin sat down on top of him, and the child lost his life. She too seized Daṇḍin and said, "You have killed my son, so give me a son." He said, "I did not kill him, so how can I give you a son?" She said, "Come along. Let us go before the king, and he will judge us." And they went on.

In another place a crow was sitting in a śakoṭaka tree. It saw Daṇḍin and asked, "Where are you going?" "I am not going; these people are taking me." "Where are they taking you?" "Before the king." "Then take a message from me to the king too. Say to him: 'In such and such a place a crow sits in a śakoṭaka tree. When I sit on top of other trees, my voice is not sweet, but when I come to the top of this tree my voice is wonderfully sweet. Why do I sound like that?'" Then, in yet another place, a snake saw him and, as before, said, "Present this message of mine: 'When I come out of my hole I am at ease, but when I go back in again I suffer. Why is it so?' Ask him that." As they went on, in another place a young married woman saw him and, as before, said, "Present my message too: 'When I am staying in my parents' house, I long for my in-laws' house; and when I am staying in my in-laws' house, I long for my parents' house. Why is that?' Ask him that."

So they went on, and when they came before the king, they bowed their heads at the king's feet and sat to one side. The king asked them, "Why have you come?" and they told him all about the dispute between Daṇḍin and the householder.

The king said to Daṇḍin, "Did you borrow the ox?" "I borrowed it." "Then did you give it back?" "I returned the ox where its owner could see it, but I did not hand it over with a word." The king declared, "Because Daṇḍin returned the ox but did not say so, cut out his tongue. And because the householder saw the ox arrive but did not tie it up, gouge out his eyes." The householder said, "Daṇḍin has, first, robbed me of my ox, and second, had my eyes gouged out. Rather than that, I would sooner let Daṇḍin win."

One man said, "Your Majesty, Daṇḍin killed my mare." The king asked Daṇḍin, "How did you kill the horse?" "I was walking along the road, and this man said, 'Don't let the mare get away!' I picked up a stone and threw it, and the horse died." The king said, "Because the horse's owner said, 'Don't let the mare get away,' cut out the horse owner's tongue. And because Daṇḍin threw the stone, cut off his hand." The man said, "First my horse was killed, and second my tongue would be cut out. Rather than that, I would sooner let Daṇḍin win."

The carpenter said, "Daṇḍin asked me how deep the water was, and the adze I was carrying in my mouth fell into the water." The king said, "Whatever goods one carries, they should be carried on the shoulder; since he carried it in his mouth, knock out the carpenter's two front teeth. And because Daṇḍin asked whether the water was deep, cut out his tongue." The carpenter said, "First my adze was lost, and second my teeth would be broken. Rather than that, I would sooner let Daṇḍin win."

The woman who sold beer said, "Daṇḍin killed my son." Daṇḍin said, "I was weary, and went to ask for beer. I sat down on the seat without looking at it, and did not notice there was a child underneath, and the child died." The king said, "Beer seller, you were at fault for laying your child on the seat and covering him with a cloth so that he could not be seen. Daṇḍin, you were at fault for sitting down without checking the seat. Daṇḍin, become her husband and give her a son." The beer seller said, "First my son was killed, and second this man would become my husband. Rather than that, I would sooner let Daṇḍin win."

The weaver's wife said, "Daṇḍin killed my husband." Daṇḍin said, "I have many enemies and was afraid, so I leapt over a wall to flee, and I did not see there was a man on the other side, and he died." The king said, "Go, and take this very man as your husband." She said, "First my husband was killed, and second this man would become my husband. Rather than that, I would sooner let Daṇḍin win."

So each of them was given judgment, and Daṇḍin was granted land and freed from all his troubles.

That king also had two women disputing over one son. The king, being wise, examined the matter and commanded the two women, "Each of you take one of the child's hands and pull, and whoever wins him, take him as your son." The one who was not the child's mother had no compassion for the child and did not fear that he would be hurt, so she pulled with all her might. The one who was the child's mother loved the child and feared that he would be hurt, so although she was strong enough to win, she did not pull hard. The king examined this and said to the one who had pulled hard, "This is not your son; he is the other woman's son. Tell the truth." And so it turned out that he was the son of the one who had pulled gently, and she took the child away.

Then again two men, disputing over one bolt of cloth, came before the king, and the king examined the matter and gave judgment as before.

Then Daṇḍin said to the king, "When these people had seized me and were leading me here, a snake gave me this message: 'When I come out of my hole I am at ease, but when I go back in again I suffer. Ask him why it is so.'" The king said, "Say this to that snake: 'When you come out of your hole you are at ease, because you are not angry and you are hungry. Once you have come out, you eat a great deal of food, and the birds harass you, so you become angry and swell up; and so when you go back in, you get stuck in the hole and suffer.' Tell him: 'If from now on you know the right measure of food and do not get angry, you will go in at ease, just as you come out.'"

Then Daṇḍin told him the young married woman's message, and the king said, "Say this to that young married woman: 'You have a lover at your parents' house. When you are staying in your in-laws' house, you long to go to your parents' house because you are attached to your lover. When you are staying in your parents' house, you grow tired of your lover and long to go to your in-laws' house because you are attached to your husband. Give up one place and hold well to the other, and you will be free of such trouble.'"

Then he told him the message of the crow sitting in the śakoṭaka tree in another place, and the king said, "Tell him: 'Your voice is sweet because there is gold under that tree. Under other trees there is no gold, so your voice is not sweet.'"

The king said to Daṇḍin, "Although you have done many wrongs, I release you. You are destitute; dig up the treasure of gold under that tree and take it." Daṇḍin delivered each of the messages as the king had instructed, dug the gold out from under the tree, and took it away. From then on his livelihood was established and he was troubled by nothing at all, and for the rest of his life he was rich and happy.

"Ānanda, do not think that King Sundara of that time and occasion was someone else. He was I myself now. The brahmin Daṇḍin of that time and occasion was the brahmin Piṇḍola. In the past too I freed him from suffering and gave him precious things and made him happy. Now that I have attained perfect buddhahood, I have again freed him from suffering and given him the inexhaustible treasure of the precious Dharma."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, the venerable Ānanda and the many in the assembly rejoiced and delighted in what the Blessed One had said.

This is the thirty-ninth chapter, the chapter called the householder Daṇḍin.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time there lived in that land a brahmin called Śincira, of noble family and wealthy, who had no son. He went to the six teachers and asked them, and the six teachers said, "You have no sign of a son." Then the brahmin went back home, put on soiled clothes, went into the room of mourning, and sat there unhappy, thinking, "I have no son, and if one day I die, the king will take possession of my house along with all my wealth and goods." With that thought he sat there in great unhappiness.

The brahmin's wife was a friend of a nun, and at that time the nun came to the brahmin's house. Seeing the brahmin so unhappy and tormented by grief, she asked his wife, "Why is your husband unhappy?" The brahmin's wife said, "We have no son. When we asked the six teachers, they told us, 'You have no sign of a son,' and so he is unhappy and grieving." Then the nun said, "The six teachers are not all-knowing, so how could they understand the causes and conditions of karma? The Tathāgata is dwelling in the world, who understands and knows all things and is unobstructed with regard to past and future. Why do you not ask him whether you will have a son or not?"

When the nun had left, the brahmin's wife told her husband this, and the brahmin rejoiced and understood. At once he put on new clothes and went to where the Blessed One was. When he arrived he bowed his head at the Buddha's feet and asked, "Blessed One, have I no sign of a son, no son of good fortune?" The Blessed One said, "Brahmin, you will have a son. He will be endowed with merit, and when he has grown up, he will delight in going forth." When the brahmin heard him say this, he was overjoyed and delighted, and said, "If only I have a son, then even if he goes forth, so be it; I will have no sorrow." He invited the Blessed One and the Saṅgha of monks to come for a meal, and the Blessed One consented by remaining silent.

The next day, when the time for the midday meal arrived, the Buddha went with the Saṅgha to the brahmin's house and sat on the seats laid out, and the brahmin and his wife respectfully served them alms food. When they had eaten, the Buddha and the Saṅgha set off back again. On the way there was a meadow, and in it a spring of very pure water. The Blessed One and the Saṅgha of monks rested there, and each of them drew water from the spring and washed their bowls, hands, and feet.

Then a monkey came there and took Ānanda's alms bowl. Ānanda, afraid it would break the bowl, would not give it, but the Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Give the alms bowl to the monkey." So Ānanda gave the bowl to the monkey, and the monkey took honey from the top of a tree, filled the bowl, and offered it to the Blessed One. The Blessed One said, "Remove the impurities from the honey," and the monkey removed the insect carcasses and the like and offered it. The Blessed One accepted the bowl of honey and said, "Mix the honey with water and give it to me," and the monkey mixed it with water and offered it to the Blessed One.

The Blessed One accepted it and shared it out among the Saṅgha of monks as well, and when it had been given, all of them were fully satisfied. Then the monkey, seeing all this, was overjoyed, and as it leapt and danced it fell over a precipice and died. Immediately it was reborn as the son of a brahmin. That brahmin's wife became pregnant, and when the months were complete she gave birth to a fine, handsome boy. At the very moment of his birth, every vessel in the house was filled with honey. The brahmin husband and wife were overjoyed, summoned a diviner of marks, and had the boy's marks examined. The diviner asked, "When this boy was born, what omen or wonder occurred?" They said, "At the moment of his birth everything was filled with honey," and so the boy was named Supreme Honey.

The boy was raised and nourished, and when he had grown up he asked his father and mother for leave to go forth. Because his parents were attached to their son, they would not let him go. The boy asked again and again and said, "If my father and mother obstruct me, I can bear to die, but I cannot bear to live amid the bustle of the household." Then the boy's parents discussed it together: "Long ago the Blessed One too foretold that he would go forth. If we hinder him now, he may well lose his life. We should give our consent." Having decided this, they told him, "Son, we permit you to go forth as you wish."

Then the boy, delighted and overjoyed, went to where the Blessed One was, bowed his head at his feet, and asked to go forth. The Blessed One said, "Welcome!" and his hair and beard fell away of themselves, and he became a monk. Then, when the Dharma of the Four Noble Truths was taught to him in full, his mind was completely liberated; all his defilements were exhausted and he became an arhat. Whenever he went about with the other monks for the benefit of sentient beings, if they were thirsty or weary, he would toss his alms bowl into the sky and it would fill with honey of itself, and all of them drank it together.

Then Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "What merit did the monk Supreme Honey make in the past, that not long after going forth he attained arhatship and the Dharma just as he had wished?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Ānanda, do you remember when the brahmin Śincira offered alms? Do you remember how, when you had finished your meal and were resting on the meadow, a monkey took the alms bowl from you, filled it with honey and offered it to me, and how the monkey, delighted, leapt and danced, fell over a precipice and passed from that life?"

He replied, "I remember."

"Ānanda, the monkey who offered the honey at that time is this monk Supreme Honey. Because he saw the Buddha, the Blessed One, and offered honey with a joyful mind, he was born as that brahmin's son, with a handsome form, and after going forth he became free of defilements."

Then Ānanda placed his right knee on the ground and said to the Blessed One, "What wrong did this monk commit, that he was born among monkeys?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Long ago in the past, when the Buddha Kāśyapa had come into the world, a young novice monk saw a monk leaping over a canal and said, 'You are so quick, you are just like a monkey.' The monk heard this and said to him, 'Do you know who I am?' He replied, 'You are a monk of the Buddha Kāśyapa; how could I not know?' That monk said, 'Do not mock me. I am not a monk in name only; I have attained the four fruits of a monk.' At these words the young monk was terrified, the hair of his body stood on end, he touched the ground with the five points of his body, and confessed. Because he confessed in this way, he was not reborn among the hell-beings. But because he had mocked an arhat, for five hundred lifetimes he was always born as a monkey. Because in the past he had also gone forth and kept moral discipline, he met me, and as though washed clean, all his suffering came to an end. Ānanda, the young monk of that time is now Supreme Honey."

Then Ānanda and the great assembly, having heard what the Buddha said, all said this: "One must not fail to guard the actions of body, speech and mind. Because this monk did not guard the action of his speech, such was the karmic ripening of his deed."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Ānanda, it is just as you say." Then he taught the Four Noble Truths in full, and with the actions of their body, speech and mind purified and free of every stain of mind, some attained stream-entry, once-returning, non-returning and arhatship. Some generated the mind set on unsurpassed, perfect and complete awakening. Some came to abide on the irreversible ground. The great assembly rejoiced in what the Blessed One had said and were delighted.

This is the chapter of the brahmin Śincira, the fortieth.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Rājagṛha, in the Bamboo Grove, at the Kalandakanivāpa. At that time, in the land of Kośala, there lived a householder named Damśincira, wealthy, powerful and of high family. He had no son, and so he made offerings to the gods, the nāgas and the rest, at the shrines of all the gods, and prayed for a son. Because the householder worshipped all the gods from his heart and prayed for a son, his wife later became pregnant, and when the months were complete a son was born, a fine, handsome boy, rare in the world. A diviner was summoned and examined his marks, and in accordance with the marks he named the boy Danbila. He was raised and nourished, and when he had grown up, his father died, and King Prasenajit installed the youth as king in his father's place. When he was thus consecrated king in his father's place, all his houses and dwellings turned into the seven kinds of precious things, all his many treasuries were filled to overflowing, and he came to have everything he could want.

At that time the king's son, named Vaiḍūrya, was stricken with a fever. A physician examined him and said, "As medicine, if his body is anointed with gośīrṣa sandalwood, he will live." So the king issued this proclamation to the people of the land: "Whoever among you has gośīrṣa sandalwood, offer it to the king! As a reward he will be given a thousand gold ounces." But although this was proclaimed everywhere, not a single person had any.

Then someone said to the king, "In the land of Kośala, in the house of the householder Danbila, there is a great deal of it." The king himself mounted a horse carriage and set out to ask for the sandalwood. When he reached the householder's gate, the gatekeepers at the householder's gate said, "King Prasenajit has come here and is now waiting outside." The householder was overjoyed, came out to the gate, and welcomed the king inside.

When the king went to the gate and looked, the gate itself was made of silver, and inside the gate a woman, lovely and beautiful, without equal in the world, sat on a silver seat spinning silver thread. Beside her ten fine young maidens attended on her. Seeing her, the king asked the householder, "Is this your wife?" He replied, "No, she is the slave woman who keeps the gate." "Then what are these fine young maidens for?" the king asked. He said, "They are the messengers who carry word between outside and inside."

Then he went inside through the next gate and looked. The gate was made of lapis lazuli (vaiḍūrya), and inside the gate sat a fine young maiden, more beautiful still than the first, with attendant maidens half again as many as before. Then he went on through yet another gate and looked. This gate was made of gold, and inside it sat a fine young maiden more beautiful by far than the one before, on a golden seat, weaving golden cloth. To her right and left were attendant maidens far more numerous than before. He asked the householder, "Is this one your wife?" and he replied, "This one is not either."

Then he went into the house and looked. The whole floor was made of lapis lazuli, so clear that it looked like the color of water. On the ceiling above were painted the forms of beasts of prey, makaras, fish and the like, and when they swayed as the wind stirred them, their reflections appeared on the floor. The king, thinking it was truly water, said, "Is there no water elsewhere? Why have you dug a pond inside the house?" Danbila said, "This is not water; it is lapis lazuli." And he took the bracelet off his arm and threw it down upon it.

Seeing this, the king entered the house, and in a chamber of the seven precious things a lapis lazuli throne was set out, and the king was invited to be seated. The householder's wife was also there, and tears came from her eyes. The king asked, "Why are tears coming from your eyes? Are you displeased that I have come?" The householder's wife replied, "We are glad that the king has graciously come here. But the king's clothing smells faintly of smoke, and so tears came from my eyes. It is not that I do not wish to see the king."

The king said, "Do you not even light a fire in your house?" She said, "There is no fire." "Then with what do you cook your food?" She said, "As for food, by merely thinking of it, food with a hundred flavors appears." The king said, "In the darkness of night, by what do you see?" She said, "It is lit by a precious jewel. Even when the doors are closed, the light of the precious jewel shines from the windows as if it were day."

Then Danbila placed both knees on the ground and said, "Why has the king troubled himself to come here?" The king said this: "My son Vaiḍūrya has been stricken with a fever, and the physician examined him and says that as medicine he must be anointed with gośīrṣa sandalwood. So I have come here to ask it of you." The householder was overjoyed and invited the king himself into his treasury, and showed him all his wealth, the seven precious things and the rest, and showed him also immeasurable heaps of sandalwood, saying, "Take whatever you wish." The king said, "I need no more than two ounces; give me that." And the householder offered him whatever he wished.

Then, as the king was going back, he told the householder, "Go and meet the Buddha." The householder said, "What is this one called the Buddha like?" The king said, "Have you not heard? The son of Śuddhodana, king of the city of Kapilavastu, weary of old age, sickness and death, went forth, and by striving for awakening he became fully awakened. His body is adorned with the thirty-two major marks and the eighty excellent minor marks, and his bases of miraculous power and his wisdom are beyond compare. Because he has become the chief of gods and humans, he is called the Buddha."

When the householder heard him say this, he felt deep faith, and he asked the king, "Where is the Buddha staying now?" The king said, "He is staying now at Rājagṛha, in the Bamboo Grove." As soon as the king had left, the householder too went to where the Buddha was staying. When he arrived and beheld the Buddha's body, he saw that it surpassed by far that of every king. Delighted, joyful and glad, he bowed his head and asked after his health. The Blessed One taught him the Dharma as suited him, and the householder attained stream-entry. He placed both knees on the ground and asked the Blessed One for leave to go forth. The Blessed One said, "Welcome!" and his hair and beard fell away of themselves, and he became a monk. Then, when the Dharma of the Four Noble Truths was again taught to him as suited him, the stains of his mind were completely purified, and he became an arhat.

Then Ānanda and the great assembly said this to the Blessed One:

"What merit did the monk Danbila make, that he was born among humans and possessed the riches of a god, yet was not attached to the household, delighted in going forth, and at once attained the fruit?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Listen very well. Long ago in the past, ninety-one eons ago, a buddha called Vipaśyin came into the world. After that buddha had passed into nirvāṇa, at the time when the Dharma was practiced before his image, about five hundred monks made this vow together: 'Let us seek a secluded place and meditate on the path of awakening.' They established a secluded dwelling in a grove where there was grass and a spring. Of one accord, those monks made this request of one monk: 'This dwelling of ours is far from the town, and if all of us go for alms everyone will be wearied. So that you may make merit, go and beg our alms and give them to us.' That monk said, 'I will do so,' and he went to the town constantly, taught the Dharma to many patrons, and begged alms.

"Among them, four men, their bodies at ease, meditated, and after ninety days they attained the fruit. Of one accord they said to the monk who begged the alms, 'Because of you we have been very much at ease, and we have attained the samādhi we hoped to attain. Tell us whatever you wish for, and we will fulfil your hope.' Then that monk too was overjoyed and said this. He made this aspiration: 'In the future, whether I am born as a god or a human, may I be wealthy and possessed of riches, and may everything I need arise of itself without my working for it. May I meet a noble teacher, hear the Dharma a hundred and a thousand times more than you, and with my mind completely purified, attain the fruit.'"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "The one who was the monk at that time is the monk Danbila. Because he provided alms for his four monk companions, for ninety-one eons he was born among gods and humans into noble families, and was never born into a poor and needy family. And now, by merely seeing me, he has gained faith."

Then Ānanda and the monks, having heard what the Blessed One said, each set about striving with diligence, and they attained the fruits from the first fruit up to the fourth. Some generated the vast mind and came to abide on the irreversible ground. When the Blessed One had spoken thus, each of them rejoiced and was delighted.

This is the chapter called the householder Danbila, the forty-first.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time, in the land of Magadha, a son was born to a certain householder, fine and handsome and endowed with auspicious marks. When the boy was first born, a marvelous golden elephant calf appeared of itself in the treasury. When the boy's parents saw this they were overjoyed and showed him to a diviner of marks, and the diviner saw that the boy had the marks of merit. He asked, "When the boy was first born, what marvelous omen occurred?" The boy's parents said, "When this boy was born, a golden elephant calf appeared of itself in the treasury." So the boy was named Hastipāla.

As the boy grew, so too the golden elephant calf grew. When the boy went somewhere the elephant calf went too, always keeping him company, and even the elephant calf's urine and dung turned into gold.

When Hastipāla had grown up, he always played together with about five hundred sons of householders, and they were fond of one another. One day the boys each talked about whatever marvels there were in their homes. Some said, "In my home the seats, cushions, beds and the rest are all made of the seven precious things." Some said, "My home, my pleasure garden and everything else are all jewels." Some said, "My treasury is always filled with the finest jewels." So each of them told of all the various wonders they had. Then Hastipāla too said, "When I was first born, a golden elephant calf appeared of itself, and just as I grew, the elephant calf grew likewise. I go wherever I please; riding the elephant, I go swiftly anywhere I wish, to watch shows and the like, and even its urine and dung turn into gold."

At that time Prince Ajātaśatru was also there, and hearing what Hastipāla said, he thought, "When I become king, I will seize that elephant." Later, when he had become king, he summoned Hastipāla and commanded him, "Bring your elephant!"

Then Hastipāla's parents said this to their son: "There is no one so wrathful, fierce and greedy as Ajātaśatru. He even killed his own father, so what need to speak of others? Now he means to seize your elephant, so you must give him the elephant." The son said, "No one at all can seize this elephant of mine." Then the two brothers mounted the elephant and came to the gate of the king's palace, and the gatekeepers informed the king. The king commanded, "Let the two brothers Hastipāla come inside without dismounting from the elephant." The two brothers Hastipāla came into the palace riding the elephant, and only then dismounted. Then they paid homage to the king and said, "May you be well." The king was overjoyed, had them sit on cushions spread out for them, and gave them food and drink. When they had talked thus, and afterwards said they would go back, he commanded Hastipāla, "Leave the golden elephant, and you go back."

Then Hastipāla, as the king commanded, left the elephant behind, and the two brothers went off on foot. They had barely reached the gate when, before long, the elephant sank into the ground right where it was and came up outside the gate beside Hastipāla. The two brothers Hastipāla mounted it again and went home, and Hastipāla thought this: "The king does not act according to the Dharma; he punishes even those who have done no wrong. If I do not give it to him, my life will be in danger because of this elephant. Now the Blessed One is in the world, working for the happiness and benefit of many beings. I will go to him, go forth from the household, and delight in the pure life." With this in mind he asked his parents for leave to go forth, and his parents gave him leave. He bowed to them in farewell, mounted the golden elephant, and went to where the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta was. Coming into the presence of the Blessed One, he bowed his head at his feet and told in full all that had happened. The Blessed One said, "Welcome!" and his hair and beard fell away of themselves, and he became a monk. The Blessed One taught him the Dharma of the Four Noble Truths as suited him, and he became an arhat.

Wherever the monk Hastipāla stayed with his fellow monks, the golden elephant was always beside him. At that time all the people living in Śrāvastī began to gather to look at the golden elephant, and the practice of yoga was much disturbed by the commotion. Those monks told the Blessed One, and for that reason the Blessed One said this to the monk Hastipāla: "Because many people are looking at your elephant, there is no seclusion. Get rid of it quickly." Hastipāla replied, "I too have thought from the beginning of getting rid of it, but it will not go." The Blessed One said, "Say this to the elephant three times: 'For me, the realm of birth is exhausted; from now on I have no need of you.' Say this, and it will disappear." Hastipāla, just as the Blessed One had said, told the elephant three times, "I have no need of you," and the golden elephant sank into the ground.
