The monks were amazed and said this to the Blessed One: "What root of virtue did this monk Hastipāla plant in the past, and in what kind of field of merit, that such a fruit has ripened for him?"

The Blessed One said this to Ānanda and the monks: "When any ordinary person plants even a small seed of virtue in the field of merit of the Three Jewels, he will obtain an immeasurable fruit. Long ago in the past, when the Buddha Kāśyapa had come into the world, the human lifespan was twenty thousand years. After that buddha had worked for the benefit of sentient beings and passed into nirvāṇa, many stūpas were built for his relics. At that time, inside one of the stūpas, there was also a stūpa of the elephant that served as the mount when the bodhisattva first descended from the abode of Tuṣita and entered his mother's womb. At that time the figure of the elephant had become a little damaged. A man came to pay homage there, and seeing the elephant a little damaged, he thought this: 'This is the mount the bodhisattva rode. Since it has become damaged like this, I will restore it.' So he mixed some clay and plastered it, painted it over with red pigment, and made this aspiration: 'In the future may I always be of a noble family and possess inexhaustible riches.' That was the aspiration he made. When that man died, he was born as a god in the heavens, and when he passed from among the gods he was born in the human world, always of a noble family, possessed of riches, of excellent and handsome appearance, and always guarded by a golden elephant."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "The man who restored the elephant at that time is now the monk Hastipāla. Ever since he restored the elephant then, he has had great riches among gods and humans. Because he showed reverence and honor to the Three Jewels, he has now met me, gone forth in the teaching, exhausted all the stains of his mind, and become an arhat."

When the venerable Ānanda and the great assembly heard what the Blessed One had said, all of them understood its meaning and took it to heart. Some attained stream-entry, once-returning, non-returning, and arhatship. Some generated the mind set on unsurpassed awakening and abode on the irreversible ground. The great assembly delighted in what the Blessed One had said and rejoiced.

This is the chapter of Hastipāla, the forty-second.

The twelfth fascicle.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time, when the Blessed One went for alms with Ānanda as his attendant, the robe the Blessed One was wearing had a small tear, and he intended this for the sake of training beings who were ready to be trained. When he had begged alms and was returning, a brahmin came, bowed his head to the Blessed One, and gazed at the Blessed One's marks, vivid and radiant. Seeing that the Blessed One's robe was a little torn, he thought to make an offering. He had a piece of white cotton cloth at home, and cutting a piece from it he offered it to the Blessed One, saying, "Please patch your robe with this piece of cloth." Out of love for him the Blessed One accepted it, and the brahmin was overjoyed, delighted and glad.

Out of love for him, the Blessed One gave him a prophecy: "Brahmin, in the future, when a hundred incalculable eons have passed, you will become fully awakened. Your name will be Excellent Higher Knowledge, and you will perfect the ten powers." When this prophecy had been given, the brahmin went away overjoyed.

At that time the merchants of noble family, the householders and others in that land thought this: "Why did the Blessed One give so very great a return for such a small gift?" With this in mind, each of them laid out white cotton cloth, made all kinds of robes for the Tathāgata, and offered them to the Buddha.

Ānanda asked the Blessed One, "What root of virtue did the Blessed One make in the past, that everyone offers him so many robes?" The Blessed One said this: "Listen well and keep it in mind, and I will explain it to you. Long ago in the past, beyond immeasurable incalculable eons, a buddha called Vipaśyin came into the world, and he dwelt together with a retinue of ninety thousand. At that time the king was called Bandhumat. At that time a great minister invited the Buddha together with the Saṅgha to stay for three months, and the Blessed One consented. The minister went back home and prepared and arranged the provisions.

"At that time the king too thought to invite the Buddha and the Saṅgha. He went to where the Buddha was, and when he arrived he said this to the Blessed One: 'Venerable Blessed One, please accept my offerings for three months, together with the noble Saṅgha.' The Blessed One said this to the king: 'I have already agreed to go at the invitation of the great minister. By the rule of great beings it is not right to go against a consent already given.'

"Then the king went back to the palace and commanded the great minister: 'In this land there is no one greater than I. The Blessed One is staying in my land, and I wish to make offerings first. Although you invited him first, wait for now; after I have invited him, you may invite him.' The minister said this to the king: 'Your Majesty, if you will guarantee that my body and life will come to no harm, that the Tathāgata will remain forever, and that the king's realm will always be happy and prosperous with no misfortune arising, then I too will wait for now.' The king thought, 'That cannot be,' and commanded, 'Let the two of us invite him on alternate days.' So one day the king made the invitation, and the next day the minister made the invitation, and the wishes of both were fulfilled.

"At that time the great minister offered the Tathāgata a set of three robes, and to each of the ninety thousand monks of the Saṅgha he also made and offered a seven-panelled robe. Ānanda, do not think that the great minister who at that time made robes and offered them to the Buddha and the Saṅgha was someone else. He is now I myself. Because in all my lives I never wearied of making merit, I have experienced the ripening of all of it, and none of it has been wasted."

When Ānanda and the great assembly heard what the Blessed One said, they became diligent in doing meritorious deeds, and they were delighted and rejoiced.

This is the chapter of the brahmin's offering of a patch, the forty-third.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time the monks, having come out of the summer rains retreat, went to where the Blessed One was. When they arrived they bowed to the Blessed One and asked after his health, saying, "Are you free of illness?" The Blessed One, with a mind of loving-kindness, gave them encouragement, and with a mind of great love and loving-kindness he said, "Monks, have you not been suffering?"

Ānanda said this to the Blessed One: "For how long has the Blessed One had this mind of love and loving-kindness toward the monks?" The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "If you wish to know, I will explain it to you. Long ago in the past, beyond inexpressible incalculable eons, two men who had done evil fell into hell. The hell guardians made them pull an iron cart, beat them with iron hammers, and made them run back and forth without respite. One of the men was weak, and unable to pull, he stopped. He was beaten with an iron hammer, died, and revived again. His companion, seeing such suffering, gave rise to a mind of loving-kindness and compassion, and said this to the hell guardian: 'I will pull this cart alone; let this man go.' The guardian, enraged, beat him with an iron hammer, and he died there and was born as a god in the Heaven of the Thirty-Three. Ānanda, the one who at that time, while in hell, gave rise to a mind of loving-kindness and compassion is now I myself. At that time, in hell, I first gave rise to a mind of compassion toward him, and from then until now I have held a mind of love and loving-kindness toward all sentient beings."

Ānanda and the great assembly rejoiced in what the Blessed One had said and were delighted.

This is the chapter of the Buddha's first generating a mind of loving-kindness, the forty-fourth.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada, together with a great Saṅgha of one thousand two hundred and fifty monks. At that time the Blessed One saw that the monks had become vain, attached to gain and fame, keeping many surplus possessions and hoarding without knowing contentment. Seeing this, the Blessed One spoke of these faults of attachment to gain to the monks.

"Whoever is attached to gain, as soon as the body breaks up and he passes from this life, falls into the three lower realms and experiences immeasurable suffering. Therefore, as I clearly remember, in times long past I fell through the power of desire and experienced much suffering."

Then Ānanda set his knee on the ground, joined his palms, and said to the Blessed One, "How was it that the Blessed One, in the past, fell through the power of attachment to desire?"

The Blessed One declared: "Long ago, countless, immeasurable, limitless, inconceivable and incalculable eons in the past, here in Jambudvīpa there was a king called Respectful, who ruled over the eighty-four thousand minor kingdoms of Jambudvīpa. He had twenty thousand queens and palace women, and ten thousand great ministers.

"At a certain time a swelling like an egg appeared on the crown of the king's head. It was clear and completely pure, and it gave him no pain. Little by little it grew larger and larger until it was the size of a gourd, and when it was cut open, out of it came a boy, beautiful to behold and endowed with the major marks, with dark blue hair and a body the color of gold. When the soothsayers examined him, they said, 'This boy has the marks of wondrous qualities. Without a doubt he will become a wheel-turning king and will rule over the four continents.' And they gave the boy the name Mūrdhaja, 'Born from the Crown.' When he had grown up, because he was endowed with good qualities, the king made him the minor king of one region.

"At a later time the great king was struck by illness. All the minor kings gathered, and although they nursed him and treated him, it did not help, and he passed away. Then the minor kings assembled and went to where Mūrdhaja was staying, and when they arrived they said, 'The great king has passed away. We beg you to become king.'

"Mūrdhaja said, 'If I have the merit and the power to become king, then let the Four Great Kings, Śakra (Indra), and the others make the request to me and come to empower me.' No sooner had he said this than the kings of the Four Great Kings descended, holding precious vases filled with scented water, and poured it on the crown of his head and empowered him. Śakra, lord of the gods, also descended, offered a jeweled diadem, placed it on his head, and praised his qualities.

"Then the minor kings begged him to go to the great central land, and Mūrdhaja again said, 'If I have the power to become king, then the land ought to come to me; I will not go chasing after the land.' The moment he said this, the great land, with the royal palace, the pleasure gardens, the bathing pools and everything else that was needed, appeared right there. The golden wheel, the elephant, the precious horse, the precious woman, the precious minister, the precious householder and all the rest came together, and he became a wheel-turning king ruling over the four continents and governed in accordance with the Dharma.

"Then, seeing many people digging the earth and plowing fields, he asked the ministers, 'What are these people doing?' They said, 'All that has life lives by food, so these people plow fields to make their living.' Then the king said, 'If I have the power of merit by which I became king, let food of a hundred flavors exist of itself without any need for labor, and let everyone be satisfied and filled and be free of hunger and thirst.' The moment he had said this, food and drink came to exist.

"Then again, as he went out walking, he saw some people spinning thread and weaving, and asked, 'What are these people doing?' They said, 'Food now exists of itself, but there is no clothing to adorn the body, so these people are making clothes to wear.' Then the king again said, 'If I have the power by which I became king, let clothes to wear exist of themselves, and may all the poor and destitute also have enough clothing.' The moment he had said this, clothes to wear of every various color grew of themselves from all the trees, and though all the people wore them, they never ran out.

"Then again, as the king went out walking, he saw people carrying musical instruments and asked, 'What are these?' They said, 'Since there is enough food and clothing to wear, people make a great deal of music; these are instruments played for their own enjoyment and amusement.' The king said, 'If I have royal power, let music and all fine implements exist of themselves.' The moment he said this, many musical instruments hung from the branches of all the trees, and whenever anyone wished for music, they played by themselves, sweetly, so that merely hearing them made people rejoice.

"Through the king's merit and great power, the seven kinds of precious things fell like rain from the sky and filled the whole land. The king asked the ministers, 'Whose virtue is it that this has come about?' The ministers said, 'It has come about through the king's virtue and the merit of beings.' The king then said, 'If it is through the force of the merit of beings, let these precious things fall everywhere. If it is through my power alone, let them fall within the palace.' Then the rain everywhere else ceased, and for seven days and nights it fell inside the palace alone.

"King Mūrdhaja enjoyed the five objects of desire in Jambudvīpa for eighty-four thousand years. Then, at a certain time, a yakṣa appeared at the palace gate and cried out in a loud voice:"

"'In the east there is the land called Pūrvavideha, the eastern continent. There too the happiness is incomparably excellent, so it is fitting that the great king should go there as well and enjoy himself.' When he said this, the king was glad to go there. The golden wheel rose into the sky, and the ministers, the seven precious things and the rest rose after it, and they went to the eastern continent of Pūrvavideha. All the minor kings who had not yet bowed to him now bowed, and in the continent of Videha he enjoyed the five objects of desire for eighty thousand years.

"Then the yakṣa again said, 'In the west there is the land called Aparagodānīya. There too is great happiness, so it is fitting that the great king should go there as well.' The king was glad to go there, and in western Godānīya he enjoyed happiness for ten thousand years.

"Then the yakṣa again said, 'In the north there is the land called Uttarakuru. The people who live there are prosperous, enjoy good harvests and possess every enjoyment, so it is fitting that the king should go to that land as well.' The king was glad to go, and in Uttarakuru he enjoyed the finest of the five objects of desire for eight thousand years.

"Then, at a later time, the yakṣa said, 'The abode of the god-kings of the realm of the Four Great Kings is so blissful that it is hard even to measure. It is fitting that the king should go there as well.' The king, together with his ministers and his fourfold army, rose into the sky and went. The Four Great Kings saw them from afar and, terrified, drew up their armies and came out, but there was nothing they could do, and they went back to their abodes. Mūrdhaja enjoyed the objects of desire there too for ten thousand years.

"Then he thought, 'I will go to the realm of the gods of the Thirty-Three.' With his retinue he rose up into the sky above and went toward the realm of the gods of the Thirty-Three. At that time some five hundred sages (ṛṣis) were living in a hollow of Mount Meru, the king of mountains. The urine and dung of the king's elephants and horses fell and struck the bodies of these sages, and they asked one another, 'What is this?' One wise and intelligent sage said to the other sages, 'They say that King Mūrdhaja has gone up to the realm of the gods of the Thirty-Three. This is without doubt the filth of his elephants and horses.' At that the sages grew angry, and binding them with secret mantras and mudrās, they made Mūrdhaja and his great retinue unable to move.

"The king, knowing and understanding that this was so, made this solemn declaration: 'If I have merit and power, may those sages come before me and pay homage to me.' Through the blessing and the force of the greatness of the king's virtue, all five hundred sages came there, became his servants, and went along to the realm of the gods of the Thirty-Three.

"Before they had arrived, they saw from afar the gods' palace called Delightful, shining very brightly, clear and radiant. The gods' palace Delightful had one thousand one hundred and fifty great gates. The gods, in fear and alarm, shut the gates and fastened them with triple iron bars. Mūrdhaja arrived there with his army without any obstruction. The king blew his conch, drew his bow and twanged the bowstring, and all the eleven hundred and fifty gates of the gods' palace opened of themselves at once. Śakra came out to welcome him, led him into the gods' palace, and had him sit on half of Śakra's own seat. The lord of the gods and the lord of men were alike in form and appearance, so that those who saw the two lords could not tell them apart, except that they could be distinguished by the quickness or slowness of the gods.

"In the gods' palace the king enjoyed the finest of the five objects of desire while thirty-six lords of the gods succeeded one another. The last of these lords of the gods was the bodhisattva called Kāśyapa. At that time the king of the asuras led an army against the gods and fought with Śakra. Śakra could not prevail, and he fled back to his abode with his army. Then Mūrdhaja came out, blew his conch and twanged his bowstring, and at once the whole army of the asuras scattered.

"At that time Mūrdhaja thought, 'The strength of my might is equaled by no one. What need is there to sit sharing with Śakra? Since there are two of us, it would be good to kill him and rule by myself alone.' The moment this evil thought arose, he fell from the sky and dropped down before the gate of his own former palace, so exhausted that he was all but parted from life.

"Then people came and asked him, 'If worldly householders ask how King Mūrdhaja died, what should we tell them?' He said, 'If anyone asks that, tell them this: King Mūrdhaja passed from life through the power of desire. He ruled over the four continents for four hundred million years, and for four days and nights precious things fell like rain as well. Though he went to two of the heavens, he did not know contentment, and so he fell from the sky and passed from life. Say that.'

"Therefore, monks, gain and fame are truly a great and grievous fault. By every means, renounce them far away, and in your minds strive earnestly for perfect awakening."

Then Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "What merit did that King Mūrdhaja make in the past, that he obtained so great a result?"

The Blessed One declared: "Long ago, countless and inconceivable eons in the past, a buddha called Puṣya came into the world and, together with his retinue, worked for the benefit of the world. At that time a brahmin's son was taking a wife. When worldly people take a wife according to custom, it is fitting to carry a handful of beans and scatter them.

"That young man took up beans and went off to scatter them, and on the way he met the Blessed One. His heart was glad, and he scattered the beans he held in his hand over the Blessed One. Four beans went into his alms bowl, and one bean stuck to the very top of his head. Through just that much cause and condition of merit he obtained great merit: because four beans went into the alms bowl, he came to rule over the four continents, and because one bean stuck to the crown of his head, he came to enjoy the riches of two levels of the heavens."

At that time, when the attendants heard what the Buddha had said, some attained the first fruit, and some the second, the third, and the fruit of an arhat. The great assembly rejoiced at what the Blessed One had said and were delighted.

This is the chapter of King Mūrdhaja, the forty-fifth.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time the householder Anāthapiṇḍada had a youngest daughter called Sumanā, lovely in form and beautiful to behold. Of all his children he loved this youngest daughter most dearly, and wherever he went he took her along.

At a certain time the householder went to pay homage to the Buddha, and the girl went along after him. When the girl saw the Buddha, an extraordinary joy arose in her, and she thought of anointing with perfume every place where the Buddha stayed. At that time the girl was holding a mango in her hand, and the Blessed One said, "Give it here." At once the girl offered it into the Blessed One's hand. The Blessed One took the mango, wrote on it the names of many kinds of fine perfume, and gave it back into the girl's hand.

Then the girl, together with her father and mother, came home and went to the market, bought many various kinds of the perfumes the Blessed One wished for, and went back again to the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta. Every day she ground the perfumes and smeared them over every place.

At a certain time a prince of the land of Takṣaśilā had been sent on a mission to Rājagṛha, and as he was traveling back he came in due course to the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta. He saw the girl grinding perfume, became infatuated with her, and thought of taking her as his wife. He asked King Prasenajit for the girl, and the king said, "Whose daughter is she?" He said, "She is the daughter of the householder Anāthapiṇḍada." The king commanded, "In that case it is no use asking me. Ask the girl's own father and mother."

In due course the prince took leave of the king, and the king commanded, "Do so." The prince sent all his servants and retinue back to his own land and kept back only a single elephant. At a certain time he went to the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, carried off the girl Sumanā, set her on the elephant and fled. Anāthapiṇḍada pursued them but could not catch up, and the prince went back to his own land and made her his wife.

Then that wife became pregnant. Later, when her months were complete, she gave birth to ten eggs. When the eggs were broken open, out of each egg came a boy, beautiful to behold and handsome, finer than all others. When the boys had grown up, they were brave and very skilled, and they came to delight in killing wild animals. The boys' mother commanded them, "Do not kill wild animals like this." The sons said to their mother, "There is no joy like hunting wild animals. Since our mother will not allow us to kill them, it seems you must hate us."

The mother said, "I do not hate you. Nor is it for lack of love for you that your mother does not let you kill wild animals. It is this: through the evil of taking life, beings suffer in hell for many hundreds of thousands of years. They always come to have the heads of deer, the heads of sheep, the heads of hares and so forth, the heads of every kind of wild animal, and the guardians of hell kill them for countless years. Even if they wish to be freed from that, they are not freed from their evil karma."

The sons said, "If we are to do as you instruct, tell us: did you hear this instruction from someone else, or did you think of it and say it yourself?" She said, "I heard these words from the Buddha." The sons said, "Tell us who this one called the Buddha is."

"Have you not heard? The son of King Śuddhodana, his father, was handsome and fine of body and would have become a wheel-turning king. But not wishing for old age, sickness and death, he went forth, and by cultivating the path he accomplished what he had aspired to. He attained the unsurpassed fruit, and his body, sixteen cubits tall, is fully adorned with the major and minor marks. He possesses the three knowledges and the six superknowledges. His knowledge of what is past is without limit, and his knowledge of what is to come is also without limit; the three times are to him like a fruit placed in the palm of the hand."

When the sons heard this, they were overjoyed and said, "How near or far from here does that Buddha now dwell? May he be seen?" The mother said, "He is now staying at Śrāvastī, and he may be seen." The sons said, "Then we will go to see the Buddha," and when their mother gave her permission, the sons went together to Śrāvastī.

When they arrived there, their grandfather Anāthapiṇḍada saw them and was overjoyed and delighted. He took his grandsons and went to the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta to see the Tathāgata. When the boys saw that the Buddha's marks were a thousand times finer than what they had heard before, they were filled with joy.

Then the Blessed One taught them the Dharma in accordance with their dispositions, and all ten boys at once obtained the pure Dharma eye regarding phenomena. They asked the Buddha to let them go forth, and the Blessed One said, "Boys, have your father and mother given permission?" They said, "We have not yet asked them." He declared, "Until your father and mother have given permission, I do not permit you to go forth."

Then Anāthapiṇḍada said to the Blessed One:

"I am the grandfather of these boys, and I too have authority over my grandsons, so I give them permission to go forth." When he had said this, the Blessed One also gave his permission, and their hair and beards fell away of themselves and they became monks. Because they generated diligence on the path of a monk, all of them became arhats.

The ten monks delighted in one another exceedingly: when they went, they went together, and when they stayed, they stayed together, and all the people of the land were glad and rejoiced in them.

Then Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "What merit did these ten monks make, that they were born into a noble family, are fine in form and appearance, and, having met the Blessed One, have reached the end of suffering?"

The Blessed One declared to Ānanda: "Long ago, ninety-one eons in the past, a buddha called Protector of All came into the world. Having worked for the benefit of all beings, he passed into nirvāṇa, and his relics were widely distributed and countless stūpas were built. At that time one stūpa had fallen into ruin, and an old woman was repairing it. Ten travelers came there and saw her, and they said to the old woman, 'What work are you doing?' The old woman said, 'This is a stūpa, a support for an object of worship. Repairing it brings exceedingly great merit. Therefore, wishing for the fruit of virtue, I am repairing it.' The young men were overjoyed at what the old woman said and said, 'We too will help.' When the stūpa had been repaired, all ten young men made this aspiration: 'May we be born as the sons of this old woman.'

"Since then, for ninety-one eons, they have been born together among gods and humans and enjoyed happiness, and in three things they have surpassed other people. What are the three? They are fine and beautiful in form, they are honored by all, and they are always long-lived; and throughout all that time they have not fallen into the hells. Now, having met me, they have become free of all dust and stain and have become arhats. The old woman at that time was the girl Sumanā. The ten young men are these ten arhats."

When the Blessed One had said this, some in the great assembly attained stream-entry, once-returning, non-returning and arhatship. Some generated the mind set on unsurpassed awakening and came to abide on the ground of irreversibility. They rejoiced at what the Buddha had said and were delighted.

This is the chapter of the ten sons of the girl Sumanā, the forty-sixth.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time in that land there was a wandering ascetic called Upagupta, upright of mind and clear in thought, learned in the past, the future and the present. He went to where the Blessed One was and said to the Blessed One, "I will go forth." He said, "If, once I have gone forth, I am equal to Śāriputra in wisdom and eloquence, I will go forth; if I am not capable of that much, I will go back home." The Blessed One declared, "You are not capable of that much," and so the wandering ascetic did not agree to go forth and went back home.

At a later time the Blessed One declared to the great assembly: "When a hundred years have passed after my nirvāṇa, this brahmin will go forth, will possess the six superknowledges and wisdom, and will work for the benefit of beings as numerous as the specks of dust."

Again, just as the Blessed One was passing into nirvāṇa, he declared to Ānanda: "After my nirvāṇa I entrust the whole Sūtra Piṭaka to you. Retain it, and spread it widely." After the Blessed One had passed into nirvāṇa, Ānanda retained the entire Piṭaka of the Dharma.

When Ānanda was about to give up his body, he instructed his attendant Yaśas: "After I have passed away, I entrust all the sūtras to you. Retain them." Having instructed him, he also said this: "In the land of Vārāṇasī there is a householder called Gupta who has a son called Upagupta. Ask for him and have him go forth. After you pass away, entrust the Dharma to him."

After Ānanda had passed away, Yaśas upheld the sacred Dharma and did much for the benefit of beings in the world. Then he went to the town of Vārāṇasī, made friends with that householder, went to his house again and again, and won his confidence. A son was born to the householder, and he was given the name Upagupta. While the boy was still small and had not yet gained his strength, Yaśas asked for him so as to have him go forth, but the householder said, "This is the only son I have, the one I longed for. I think of him as the one who will carry on the family, so it cannot be. If another one is born, then I will give him."

Later, at another time, another son was born, and he was given the name Nantan Kubata. Then Yaśas went there again and asked for the son, and the householder again said, "This elder son has to accomplish the work outside the house. Only if the younger son accomplishes the work inside the house will things go well." He would not give him, and said, "If yet another son comes later, then I will offer him."

Yaśas was one of the arhats possessing the three knowledges and was skilled in the faculties of beings. When he examined those two boys, it did not seem that they had the fortune to go forth, so he let them be without pressing.

Then another son was born to the householder, beautiful in form and lovely to behold. Again Yaśas went to the householder and asked for the son, and the householder said, "The boy is still small and cannot yet even serve, and since I am poor I cannot spare him. Wait a while; when he has grown, I will give him."

Then, when the boy had grown up and become endowed with wisdom, his father also put wealth and goods into his hands and had him engage in trade.

At a certain time Yaśas himself went to him, taught him the Dharma, and had him apply his mind one-pointedly. He instructed him, "When a virtuous thought arises, set down a white pebble, and when a nonvirtuous thought arises, set down a black pebble, and from time to time count which are more."

Upagupta did as he had been instructed, setting down pebbles as virtuous and nonvirtuous thoughts arose, and counting them. At first the black pebbles were very many and the white very few. Then gradually the white and black became equal. Then, because he did not let go of this attention, there were no black pebbles, only white ones. His virtuous thought was perfected, and he attained the first fruit.

At that time a courtesan of that city sent a maidservant to buy flowers from Upagupta. Upagupta, being honest by nature, gave the maidservant flowers generously, so that she was in no way dissatisfied. The maidservant took the flowers and offered them to her mistress, who was greatly astonished and said to the maidservant, "The price of the flowers you bought before and the price of those bought today are the same. Why were there so few before, and why so many now? Were you cheating me before?"

The maidservant said, "The man who sells flowers today is wonderfully honest, and since he is without guile I got a great many. That man is handsome and beautiful in form, and if my lady were to see him she would be delighted." The courtesan sent for him, but Upagupta would not come there. Though she called him again and again, he would not go.

At that time the courtesan consorted with a prince. Coveting the prince's precious clothes, she secretly killed the prince and hid the body. Then the king searched the houses, and the prince's body was found in the courtesan's house. Her hands and feet were cut off, her ears and nose were cut off as well, and she was tied to a stake and set up in the charnel ground.

Even after all this was done she was still alive, and Upagupta went to her. The courtesan said, "When I was lovely before, you would not come. Now that I have become hideous, why do you come?" Upagupta said, "I have not come here out of desire for your beauty. I have come out of compassion for you." Having said this, he taught her these four teachings on impermanence: "This body is impure, suffering, empty and without self. When examined one by one, it is not fit to be relied upon. Only fools steeped in delusion are attached to it." When the courtesan heard these teachings, she obtained the pure Dharma eye regarding phenomena. Upagupta attained the fruit of non-returning.

Then Yaśas asked the householder for the young man, saying, "I will have him go forth," and the householder offered him accordingly. Yaśas took him to the monastery and conferred the ten precepts upon him. Then, when he reached twenty years of age, he received full ordination. When he was fully ordained by the formal act consisting of a motion and three proclamations, he became an arhat. He possessed the three knowledges and the six superknowledges, and his speech and eloquence became inexhaustible.

He gathered many people and taught them the Dharma, and at that time Māra, the Evil One, rained down gold coins among the great assembly, so that all the people went after the coins and did not listen to the Dharma. On the following day he gathered a great assembly and taught the Dharma, and Māra, the Evil One, rained down garlands of flowers, so that everyone's mind was disturbed. On the following day he gathered a great assembly and taught the Dharma, and the king of the māras displayed a dark blue elephant from whose mouth came six tusks. On each tusk was a pool, in each pool were seven flowers, and in each flower were seven crystal maidens, and each of these maidens was playing a great deal of music. He showed the elephant walking slowly, its steps upon lotuses, and since all the people watched it, they had no interest in the Dharma.

On the following day he gathered the assembly and taught the Dharma, and the king of the māras conjured up an exceedingly beautiful and lovely young woman coming into the assembly. All the people gazed at her without blinking and forgot to listen to the Dharma. At once Yaśas transformed the conjured woman into a white skeleton. When the many people saw it, then at last everyone listened to the Dharma, and very many attained the fruits.

From the beginning those venerable ones had a dog. Every day, while the Dharma was being taught, it would lower its ears and listen, and when that dog died it was born in the sixth heaven of the gods and sat on the same seat as Māra, the Evil One. The king of the evil māras thought, "This god possesses great qualities and is my equal. I shall examine where he died and was born from to be born here." When he examined it, he saw that he had been born from a dog, and he thought, "The monk has shamed me and humiliated me."

From afar he saw the venerable one absorbed in samādhi, and he placed a jeweled diadem on the venerable one's head. When the venerable one rose from samādhi and noticed there was a diadem on his head, he wondered who had placed it there, and on examining it he realized that Māra, the Evil One, had placed it there. By the power of his miraculous abilities he summoned Māra, the Evil One, to that place, transformed a dog's carcass into a garland for the head, and said to Māra, the Evil One, "In return for your giving me a diadem, I give you a garland. Accept it." Māra, the Evil One, took it, put on the garland, and went back.

When he reached his abode and examined it, he saw that the garland placed on his head was a dog's carcass. Not wanting it, he tried to throw it off, but though he exerted all the might and power he had, he could not throw it off. Then he went to Śakra and said, "Throw off this filth!" Śakra said, "Only the one who did this can throw it off. I do not have the power to throw it off." Māra, the Evil One, said to all the gods, up to Brahmā, the king, "Throw off this filth of mine!" but they said the same as before: "We do not have the power to throw it off." There being nothing he could do, he came back to the venerable one and said this:

"The Buddha, the Blessed One, truly possesses great qualities: his loving mind was never one-sided. You śrāvakas are terrifying.

How is this evident? Long ago I, with an army of eight hundred thousand pretas and more, surrounded the bodhisattva and assailed him in order to make him fall away from awakening, yet he still felt compassion and did not regard me as an enemy. I played only a small prank on you, and you have repaid it like this."

Upagupta said, "What you say is true. If you compare the Buddha with me, I am not fit to serve as a comparison even a hundredfold or a thousandfold. It is like comparing Mount Meru with a white mustard seed, or the great ocean with the water in a cow's hoofprint, or the lion king with a fox: the difference in greatness cannot even be measured. I was born at the end of a degenerate age and never saw the Tathāgata. I have heard that by your magical power you are able to transform yourself into the form of the Buddha. Show it so that I may see it; I wish to behold it."

Māra said, "I will transform myself to look like him, but you must not bow down to me."

He said, "I will not bow down."

Then Māra, the king, transformed himself into the form of the Buddha: a great body sixteen cubits tall, golden in color, adorned with the thirty-two major marks and the eighty excellent minor marks, its rays of light brilliant and blazing, a manifestation surpassing even the sun and the moon. Upagupta was overjoyed and was about to bow down, when Māra, the king, changed back into Māra's body and said to Upagupta, "Venerable one, since you agreed not to bow down, why are you bowing?"

Upagupta said, "I am bowing to the Buddha; I am not bowing to you."

Māra, the king, said, "Venerable one, out of kindness to me, please take away this dog's carcass!"

Upagupta said, "Give rise to a mind of compassion and become a refuge for all sentient beings, and the dog's carcass will turn into a garland of jewels. If a nonvirtuous, evil thought arises in you, it will turn back into nothing but a dog's carcass." When he had said this, Māra, the king, was frightened, and from then on he always gave rise to virtuous thoughts.

After the venerable Upagupta had attained the fruit, he trained sentient beings, and the number whom he brought to attain the four fruits was this: each person who attained a fruit made one counting stick four finger-widths long, and with such counting sticks a house sixty cubits high and sixty cubits wide on each side was entirely filled.

Then the whole multitude praised and extolled him, saying, "Your qualities are vast, and the number of sentient beings you have trained is beyond measure."

Upagupta said, "Even when I was an animal I trained sentient beings and brought them to attain the noble fruit; so what need is there to speak of training them in the present time?"

The great assembly said, "Please tell us how you trained sentient beings in the past."

Upagupta said, "Long ago, in times past, five hundred pratyekabuddhas lived here at Vārāṇasī, on the hill of Ṛṣipatana. At that time a monkey made offerings to them every day and watched their conduct. Later those pratyekabuddhas went elsewhere, and about five hundred wandering ascetics came to live there. Some of those wandering ascetics worshiped the sun and the moon, and some worshiped the god of fire (Agni). Those who worshiped the sun and the moon stood on one foot facing the sun and the moon. The fire worshipers blew on the fire early every morning. Then the monkey, with one hand, took hold of those who were standing by the raised foot and pulled it down, and it put out the fire of those who were blowing on it; then it sat with its body upright and turned its mind to contemplation.

"Then those wandering ascetics said to one another, 'What this monkey means is, "You too should adopt conduct like this." Let each of us also straighten his body upright and examine the meaning.' As soon as they had done so, they understood and realized it in their minds, and they became pratyekabuddhas. The monkey at that time, on that occasion, is now I myself."

The assembly said, "Then why were you born in the body of a monkey?"

Upagupta said, "Ninety eons ago, in the past, the Buddha Vipaśyin came into the world, and his monks lived at Vārāṇasī, on the hill of Ṛṣipatana. At that time a monk who had attained the fruit went quickly up to the summit of the hill, and a young monk said of him, 'That monk is as quick as a monkey.' Because of that cause and that condition he became a monkey for five hundred births. Therefore the four assemblies fully restrain their speech and do not say what is needless."

When Upagupta taught this Dharma, some of the great assembly attained the fruit of a stream-enterer, of a once-returner, of a non-returner, and of an arhat. Some generated roots of virtue toward becoming pratyekabuddhas. Those who resolved on awakening and became irreversible were also beyond measure. They rejoiced in what had been said and were delighted.

This is the chapter on Upagupta, the forty-seventh.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying in the land of Vārāṇasī. At that time, in a grove with a grassy meadow, the Blessed One was teaching the sublime Dharma to gods, humans and the fourfold assembly. At that time about five hundred geese were flying in the sky above. Hearing the sweet voice of the Blessed One, they were delighted and filled with devotion, and they circled in the sky, wishing to alight before the Blessed One. At that time some hunters had spread a bird net in that place; the five hundred or so geese were caught in the net, and the hunters killed them. All of them were born in the realm of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, appearing on the laps of their fathers and mothers as children about eight years old, of fine form and lovely to look upon, lighting up everything around with their radiance, shining and pure like a mountain of gold. They thought, "Through what condition were we born as gods?" and they remembered their former existence. Knowing that they had been born as gods through hearing the sound of the Dharma being taught, they descended at once from the sky, carrying divine flowers, incense and other offerings, and came to the place in Vārāṇasī, in Jambudvīpa, where the Blessed One was staying.

With their divine light they made the whole place where the Blessed One was staying shine like jewels, and all together they bowed their heads at the feet of the Blessed One. Joining their palms, they made this request: "Merely by hearing the voice of the Blessed One teaching the Dharma, we were born in a sublime abode of the gods. Now, out of kindness to us, please teach us the sublime Dharma once more." When they had requested this, the Blessed One taught the Dharma of the Four Noble Truths. Those gods understood it and attained the fruit of stream-entry. From then on they would no longer fall into the three lower realms; they went back to the realm of the gods, and within seven births all their defilements were exhausted.

Then Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "Please tell us where those gods came from who last night lit up everything with their rays of light and came to bow at the feet of the Blessed One."

The Blessed One said, "Listen well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain. When I was teaching the Dharma to the fourfold assembly of gods and humans in a certain grove, about five hundred geese felt faith in the sound of the Dharma being taught. They felt joy toward me and were coming toward me, but before they reached me they were caught by hunters and all were killed. Through their mind of faith in the sound of the Dharma they were born in the realm of the gods of the Heaven of the Thirty-Three, and, remembering their former existence, they came to bow to me."

When Ānanda heard what the Blessed One had said, he was overjoyed, delighted and pleased, and he praised it, saying, "How wondrous and marvelous!" Then he said this: "The coming of the Blessed One into the world is wondrous and marvelous: there is no one who is not satisfied by the rain of the Dharma. If even lowly beings, such as those born as animals, gain merit by hearing the Dharma, what need is there to speak of humans? The fruit of the merit of those who retain it with a mind of faith is greater by a hundredfold and a thousandfold; it is beyond comparison."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Excellent, excellent! As you have said, the coming of a Blessed One into the world serves the benefit and happiness of all. He lets fall the rain of nectar everywhere and satisfies many beings. Therefore, apply yourself with a one-pointed mind to the teaching of the Buddha."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, Ānanda and the great assembly rejoiced and were delighted.

This is the chapter on the five hundred geese who were born as gods, the forty-eighth.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Rājagṛha, on Vulture Peak Mountain. At that time Devadatta, who constantly harbored ill will toward the Blessed One, wanted to do away with the Tathāgata and proclaim that he, Devadatta, was the Buddha. So he incited Prince Ajātaśatru to kill his father, saying, "It would be good if a new buddha and a new king governed the realm." Prince Ajātaśatru listened to him, killed his father, and took the throne himself.

At that time the people of the land came to feel ill will toward all the monks and did not even want to see them. Then, on another occasion, when the monks went into the town to beg for alms, the people were angry and would not even speak to them. The monks returned with empty begging bowls, went to Vulture Peak Mountain, and said this to the Blessed One: "Because Devadatta has committed evil, nonvirtuous deeds, all the patrons have come to feel ill will toward the monks."

When they had said this, the Blessed One spoke these words to Ānanda: "Whatever sentient being feels ill will toward a monk who wears saffron robes, that person feels ill will toward the buddhas, pratyekabuddhas and arhats of the past, and feels ill will toward the buddhas, pratyekabuddhas and arhats of the future. Know that he feels ill will toward the buddhas, pratyekabuddhas and arhats who are present now. Because he thus feels ill will toward the noble ones of the three times, the immeasurable results of evil and blameworthy deeds will ripen.

"Why is that? The saffron Dharma robe is the victory banner of the noble ones of the three times. Whatever sentient being shaves off his hair and beard and wears dyed robes will before long have his mind completely freed from all suffering, will attain undefiled wisdom, and will become a protector, a refuge and an ally for all beings. Whatever sentient being gives rise to a mind of faith toward one who has gone forth and wears the dyed Dharma robe will attain immeasurable merit. Ānanda, I too in the past felt faith toward one who had gone forth and wore the dyed Dharma robe, and because I showed him reverence I attained complete awakening."

Ānanda said, "Please tell us how the Blessed One in the past showed reverence to one who had gone forth and wore the dyed Dharma robe. We wish to hear it."

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Listen very well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain it to you. Ānanda, countless, immeasurable eons ago in the past, there was here in Jambudvīpa a great king called Debyi, who ruled over some eighty-four thousand minor kings. At that time the Dharma of the Buddha did not exist in the world, but there were pratyekabuddhas living in the mountains and groves, who traveled through the sky and worked for the benefit of sentient beings. At that time the wild animals served and honored them.

"There lived there a lion called Firm Vow, the hair of whose body was like the color of gold and gleamed brightly. For food it ate fruits and roots; it did not kill living beings. At that time a hunter saw it and was overjoyed, thinking, 'Now I shall find happiness! If I kill this lion and present its skin to the king, I will certainly be rid of my poverty.' On another occasion he shaved his head, put on a Dharma robe, tucked his bow and arrows under his arm, and went to where the lion was. At that time the lion was lying asleep, and the hunter shot it with a poisoned arrow. The lion woke up and was about to spring at the hunter, but seeing that he wore a Dharma robe, it thought, 'A man such as this does not remain long in the world; before long he will be completely freed from all suffering.'

"Why is that? This dyed Dharma robe is the victory banner of the noble ones of the past, the future and the present. If I harm him, I will be harboring ill will toward the noble ones of the three times." Thinking this, it gave up the intention to kill. Overcome by the poisoned arrow, at the point where body and life were parting, it spoke these words: "ya la la ba śa sa svāhā." At the words it spoke, all of heaven and earth shook, and rain fell from a cloudless sky. When the gods looked with their divine eyes, they saw that here in this world a hunter had killed a bodhisattva who had taken the form of a lion, and divine flowers fell like rain from the sky above, making offerings to the lion's corpse.

"Then the hunter flayed the lion's skin, came home, and presented it to King Debyi, asking for a reward. The king thought, 'In the writings and treatises it says that any animal whose fur is the color of gold is certainly a bodhisattva, a great being. Why then should I give a reward to this hunter? If I give it, I will be no different from the one who killed it.' When the hunter pressed him again, the king gave him a small amount and spoke to the hunter thus: 'When you killed this lion, did something extraordinary happen, or what omens appeared?' Asked this, the hunter said, 'From its mouth it spoke these eight syllables, and heaven and earth shook, rain fell from a cloudless sky, and the gods let flowers fall.'

"When the king heard him say this, he was deeply displeased, and, filled with faith, he gathered all his ministers and the learned and wise elders of former generations. When they examined the words the lion had spoken, not a single one could understand them. In a secluded place there lived a learned and knowledgeable sage called Śama. The king invited that sage and asked him, and the sage explained thus: 'The meaning of what is called ya la la is this: it says that those alone who shave their heads and wear the dyed Dharma robe are swiftly freed from birth and death. The meaning of what is called ba śa sa is this: it says that all who shave their heads and wear the dyed Dharma robe bear the mark of the noble ones, and are close to nirvāṇa. The meaning of what is called svāhā is this: if they shave their heads and wear the dyed Dharma robe, they should be honored by all, gods, humans and the whole world.'

"When the sage had said this, the king was overjoyed. He gathered the eighty-four thousand minor kings and the many others, and with a tall chariot made of the seven precious substances, he placed the lion's skin on top of it so that everyone could see it, and had them make offerings to it with incense and flowers and bow before it. Then he had a golden casket made, placed the lion's skin inside it, and built a stūpa. At that time all those many people who thought virtuously were born, after their deaths, as gods in the higher realms.

"Ānanda, at that time, on that occasion, because that lion gave rise to a virtuous mind toward a man wearing the dyed Dharma robe, for a hundred thousand eons he became a wheel-turning king and gave happiness to all sentient beings; having increased his meritorious deeds, he attained complete awakening. Do not think that the lion Firm Vow at that time, on that occasion, was someone else: he is now I myself. At that time, on that occasion, because King Debyi made offerings to the lion's skin, for a hundred thousand eons he was supreme among gods and humans and did every kind of virtue: he is the bodhisattva Maitreya. At that time, on that occasion, the sage was Śāriputra. The hunter was Devadatta."

Then, when the great assembly heard the Blessed One say this, they were overjoyed and said this: "Not knowing them to be noble ones, we harbored ill will. So that, out of the Tathāgata's kindness to us, it may not become an evil deed, we confess it with remorse." When they had said this, the Blessed One, with great compassion, taught the Dharma of the Four Noble Truths. In accordance with their former causes they obtained the fruits: some attained stream-entry, once-returning, non-returning and arhatship, and some resolved on unsurpassed awakening. Ānanda and the great fourfold assembly were delighted and rejoiced in what the Blessed One had said.

This is the chapter called the lion Firm Vow, the forty-ninth.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Rājagṛha, on Vulture Peak Mountain. At that time, not far from the city, there was a filthy pool of water, where many people threw their excrement and urine and all kinds of filth. Within that pool there was a great creature like a snake, with four feet. It ran about in the water; sometimes it came out, and sometimes it went along on top of the water. It lived there for many years and experienced immeasurable suffering.

On another occasion the Blessed One, surrounded by many monks arrayed before him, went to the edge of that pool. Seeing the creature, he said, "Which of you knows the former cause of this creature?" The monks thought about it, but not one of them could understand what that creature had done in the past, and they said, "None of us knows anything about it."

The Blessed One said, "Listen well and keep it in mind, and I shall explain the former cause of this creature. Long ago in the past, a buddha called Vipaśyin came into the world, worked for the benefit of sentient beings, and passed into nirvāṇa. Within the teaching of that buddha, ten thousand monks practiced the pure conduct of Brahmā, practicing it in complete purity. They lived on a mountain of hermitages, a most delightful place, where to the right and the left flowering trees and fruit trees grew in abundance, and between the trees there were springs and bathing pools. At that time the monks strove at their conduct and applied themselves diligently, so that all of them attained the fruits, from the first fruit up to the fourth, and there were no ordinary beings among them.

"On another occasion about five hundred merchants were setting out to the ocean to obtain jewels. Taking a turn on their way, they came to that mountain, and seeing how intensely the monks strove at their practice, the merchants consulted together and thought to make an offering. But because there were many patrons, they found no opportunity. Unable to do as they had intended, they bowed to the Saṅgha in leave-taking before setting out to the ocean, and said this: 'If we return safely, without mishap, we will make an offering.' The Saṅgha said nothing, and the merchants set out on the ocean.

"Those merchants found many jewels in the ocean and returned safely, and to that Saṅgha they presented a supreme and very precious jewel, as a provision for the Saṅgha's alms food. The Saṅgha accepted it and entrusted it to a monk who was the steward. Later, when the Saṅgha came to be short of alms food, they thought to use the jewel for alms food, and they instructed the steward monk, 'Bring the jewel the merchants presented.' That monk said, 'The merchants gave the jewel to me alone; they did not give it to all of you.' The Saṅgha told that monk, 'The merchants presented that jewel to the Saṅgha and had you keep it safe; they did not give it to you.' The monk became angry and said, 'Will you eat excrement? Do not say the jewel is anything but mine!' Then the members of the Saṅgha, seeing that the monk was angry and had given rise to ill will, dispersed each his own way.

"Because he thus spoke harsh words to the Saṅgha with ill will, when his body broke up and he died, he fell into the great hell of beings called Avīci, and for ninety-one eons he wallowed in filth. Released from there, he has now been born here in this pit of filth, and although many years have passed, he is not yet free.

"Thus, in the past, the Buddha Śikhin too came here with the Saṅgha of monks and showed him to his disciples, explaining what had caused his state. After that the Buddha Protector of All also came here with the Saṅgha of monks and explained what had caused his state. Then, when he died, he was born again in the hells, and after countless tens of thousands of years had passed he was later born again in this pit. After that the Buddha Krakucchanda also came here with the Saṅgha of monks and told his past story. After that the Buddha Kanakamuni also came here. After that the Buddha Kāśyapa also came here with his disciples and told his past story. Now I, the Buddha Śākyamuni, the last of the seven buddhas, have also shown you this creature here. In the same way, the thousand buddhas of the Fortunate Eon will come here with their disciples and tell the story of what this creature did in the past."

When the monks heard what the Blessed One had said, the hairs of their bodies all stood on end, and each of them guarded his actions of body, speech and mind with great care. The great assembly rejoiced in what the Blessed One had said and were delighted.

This is the chapter telling the story of the creature, the fiftieth.

Thus did I hear at one time. The Blessed One was staying at Śrāvastī, in the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta, in the park of Anāthapiṇḍada. At that time the venerable Śāriputra, three times each day and night, would look with his divine eye to see which sentient beings had the good fortune to be trained, and he would go wherever there was one fit to be trained.

On another occasion some merchants who were going to another country to trade took a dog with them. On the way the dog stole a merchant's meat, and the merchant, enraged, broke the dog's legs, abandoned it in an empty wilderness and went on. Then Śāriputra, looking with his divine eye, saw that the dog was starving. He put on his Dharma robe, took up his begging bowl, and went begging for alms; at once he flew through the sky and went to the dog's side, and gave rise to a mind of love and tender compassion. He fed the dog with the food. Having eaten the food, the dog was overjoyed, and Śāriputra taught the sublime Dharma to the dog. Then the dog died and was born in Śrāvastī as the son of a brahmin.

On another occasion Śāriputra went alone on his alms round and arrived at the brahmin's door. The brahmin said to Śāriputra, "Why do you go about alone? Have you no novice?" Śāriputra said, "I have no novice, so I go about alone. They say you have a son. Could you give him to me, or not?" The brahmin said, "I have one called Kyunte. He is still young and small and cannot do anything, so I will give him when he has grown up." When Śāriputra heard him say this, he kept it in mind and went back to the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta.

When seven years had passed, he went again to the brahmin's house and asked the brahmin for his son, and the brahmin gave him to Śāriputra. Śāriputra took the boy and led him to the Jeta Grove of Prince Jeta. He made him a novice, and when he taught him the sublime Dharma in its many forms, the boy's mind was liberated, he became an arhat, and he came to possess the six superknowledges and every good quality.

On another occasion, when the novice Kyunte examined with the power of his wisdom what he had done in his former life to obtain this body, meet his preceptor and attain the fruit, he saw and realized that in his previous life he had been a starving dog, and that through the kindness of his preceptor Śāriputra he had obtained a human body and attained the fruit. Overjoyed, he thought, "Through the kindness of this preceptor I have been freed from suffering. Now, for as long as I live, I shall remain a novice and not take full ordination, so that I may serve my preceptor."

Then Ānanda said to the Blessed One, "What evil deed did this novice commit in times past, that he came to have the body of a dog? And what root of virtue did he plant, that he came to be liberated?"

The Blessed One said to Ānanda, "Long ago in the past, in the time of the Buddha Kāśyapa, some monks were living together. Among them was a young monk with a beautiful voice, and when he chanted praises and melodies, everyone delighted to hear him. At that time there was also an old monk whose voice was not beautiful, yet he too chanted praises and verses with melody. That old monk was an arhat, possessed of all the qualities of a renunciant.

"On another occasion the young monk with the beautiful voice spoke words of contempt to the old monk whose voice was not beautiful: 'Your chanting is like a dog howling in pain.' Then the old monk asked the young monk, 'Do you know me?' He replied, 'I know you: you are an elder monk of the Buddha Kāśyapa.' The old monk said, 'I am an arhat, possessed of the qualities of a renunciant.' At this the young monk was terrified, and with remorse he confessed his fault to him. Although the old monk accepted his confession, because of the harsh words he had spoken he was born as a dog for five hundred births in succession. Because he had gone forth and kept moral discipline, he has met me here and come to be liberated."

When the Blessed One had spoken thus, Ānanda and the great assembly rejoiced and were delighted.

This is the chapter on the novice Kyunte, the fifty-first.

The Sūtra called the Wise and the Foolish is complete.

It appears to have been translated from Chinese.

Translator's notes

"The place of complete victory" renders dkyil 'khor rnam par rgyal ba, evidently the site of awakening (bodhimaṇḍa) in Magadha. The king's name Excellent Color (kha dog dam pa) and the being called the Reliever (ngal bso po), who takes the form of a yakṣa to test the king, are translated literally, as their Sanskrit forms could not be identified with confidence; in the parallel Chinese version the tester is Śakra. Kanaśinipali is a Tibetan transliteration, given as spelled. In the sentence "why now abandon," the Tibetan reads ji srid du ("as long as"), which makes little sense here; it has been read as a question ("why") as the context requires. The brahmins Leuduci and Leunguchu and King Byinglinggarli are Tibetan sound-spellings, apparently of the Chinese forms of the names (this sūtra was translated into Tibetan from Chinese); they are given as spelled since their Sanskrit forms cannot be fixed with confidence. "An elephant that could travel a hundred thousand leagues" renders ban glang rgyang grags brgya stong du phyin pa; the exact sense of ban glang is uncertain. The crown prince's name Damgam is given as the Tibetan spells it, apparently a sound-spelling of a Chinese form; its Sanskrit original cannot be fixed with confidence. The queen mother is called lha mdzes, literally "Beautiful Goddess"; as the Bodhisattva's mother she is identified as Queen Māyā, as in the parallel version. The sixth line of the verse is terse and literally reads "for others too, a dharma in accord with oneself"; it is rendered closely. The name of King Śibi's palace, de ba brta, is given as the Tibetan spells it, Devabarta; its Sanskrit original is uncertain. Śakra's "loss of the five excellences of his body" refers to the signs of decline that mark the approaching death of a god. The sentences from "The King Śibi of that time" to "pass into nirvāṇa" use humble speech addressed to the Blessed One and are taken as the close of Brahmā's narration of the former-life stories, followed by the narrative frame describing his praise. ལན་ཅིག་ཕྱིར་མི་ལྡོག་པ is an unusual form blending the terms for once-returner and non-returner; it has been rendered as "the state of no return" (anāgāmin), but "once-returner" is possible. རིགས་ངན་སྐྲག་སྟེ is taken as the low-caste executioners leading the terrified sons; the syntax is loose. The prince's name ལྷ་ཆེན་པོ is Mahādeva as a personal name here, not the god Śiva. The prince names follow the parallel tigress story (Mahāpraṇāda, Mahādeva, Mahāsattva). The name of the middle son, བ་མི་སུ་ཏྲ, is a phonetic transcription that I could not identify with confidence; it has been given as spelled, Vamisutra (it may stand for a name such as Vasumitra). The repeated ཡང་སྨྲས་སོ། །ཡང་སྨྲས་པ is treated as a duplication and rendered once. རྒྱུད་ལྔ is taken here as the five realms of rebirth, not tantras. The identifications name only the cubs, not the tigress, as the text has it. ཀུ་ཤུ is rendered "apple", its usual sense. ཀུན་ཏུ་རྒྱུ is taken as the non-Buddhist wandering ascetic (parivrājaka), which fits the wife's objection to "another teacher's" precepts. In the king's command to the minister, མི་སྙེད་དུ་མི་གནང is read as མི་རྙེད་དུ, "I will not allow you not to find it." སོ་ཆིས is obscure; from context it is rendered "fell on hard times". བྱང་ཆུབ in the closing of the fourth story is taken in the sense of the arhat's awakening mentioned earlier. མིའི་ཤིང་རྟ in the list of hell torments makes little sense as written ("human chariots"); it is read as མེའི་ཤིང་རྟ, "chariots of fire". ཆབ་རོམ and ངན་སྐྱུགས are rendered loosely as "molten metal" and "vile filth". བཀྲ་ཤིས་མ is taken as the goddess of good fortune (Śrī). "Parted from the five qualities" (yon tan lnga dang bral) refers to a god's loss of his divine qualities or pleasures as death approaches, akin to the signs of a god's impending death. The phrase ram bu btegs, rendered "took up the refrain," is uncertain; it seems to mean that the man joined in or answered the women's song loudly. "Unharmed" renders mnyes par, literally "pleasantly," describing his safe return from the water. The queen's name is written Barli in this Tibetan; it is taken here as the Tibetan form of Mallikā, Prasenajit's chief queen, who is the mother in parallel versions of this story. The Tibetan rje'u rigs is rendered "vaiśya class." The words immediately describing the householder (khyim a ro / khong khro 'dra langs gi med la / bdag des) are garbled as written; they are taken as "the householder, who had no anger in him," reading khong khro ... med for "without anger" and bdag des as the end of khyim bdag des. The phrase kun gyis te, rendered "with the others," is unclear. The names Golden Treasure (gser dbyig) and Divine Flower (lha'i me tog) are translated by meaning, as their Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence. The text places the buddha Kanakamuni ninety-one eons ago, and that is kept as written. The names Divine Flower (lha'i me tog) and Divine Jewel (lha'i rin chen) are translated by meaning, as their Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence. The sage's name, literally "Patient One," is given in English because the Tibetan here is not the usual rendering of Kṣāntivādin, the figure in the well-known version of this story; the English also keeps the wordplay of his answers "I am the Patient One." King rtsod byung ("Arising of Strife") is taken as Kali (Kalirāja), the king in the Kṣāntivādin story. Chu bo 'od srung is taken as Nadīkāśyapa ("River Kāśyapa"), one of the three Kāśyapa brothers, whose group together made up the thousand monks. "From a place where they could not be seen" translates bden pa mi snang ba'i gnas, which literally reads "a place where truth does not appear"; it may mean a hidden spot. King byams pa'i stobs is given as Maitrībala, the usual Sanskrit name of this jātaka king. rDzogs byed is taken as Pūraṇa (Pūraṇa Kāśyapa), the first of the six non-Buddhist teachers. "He was obstructed by wrong views" reads glags pa as bgags pa ("blocked"); as written the word is unclear here. The other five of the six teachers are identified as follows: phyugs lhas kyi bu ma ska ri as Maskarin Gośālīputra, rnam par smra ba'i bu kun nas rgyal ba as Sañjayin Vairaṭṭīputra, skra'i lwa ba can mi pham pa as Ajita Keśakambala, kA tyA ya na nog can as Kakuda Kātyāyana, and gnyen gyi bu gcer bu pa as Nirgrantha Jñātiputra. "Miracles of superhuman qualities" translates mi'i bla ma'i chos kyi cho 'phrul (Skt. uttaramanuṣyadharma prātihārya). Several names here are transcriptions that cannot be identified with confidence, so they are written as the Tibetan spells them: the land Vargi (war gyi, perhaps Vṛji), its king Śuncida (shun tsi da), the land Tikicaśiri (ti ki tsa shi ri), and its king Indravami (in dra ba mi, perhaps Indravarman). The king of Kauśāmbī is spelled Udrayana here (u dra ya na), not the usual Udayana. "Eighty thousand" renders stong phrag brgyad khri, which is irregular as written. "Tooth stick" takes phyag dar, which usually means "sweeping," as the tooth stick (Skt. dantakāṣṭha) that the next sentence calls tshems shing. Two of the lotus kinds are uncertain: ལེ་བརྒན is taken as an orange-red or vermilion color, and མར་གདལ is rendered tentatively as "butter-colored." The word མངད in the description of the lotus fragrance is read as ངད, "fragrance, scent," which fits the context. The householder ཙིན་ད is a transliterated name that I cannot identify with confidence; it is given as "Cinda" following the Tibetan spelling. The king's name ཤིང་རྟ་ནི་མོ is half translation, half transcription; it is taken as Rathanemi ("chariot" plus "nemi," rim), which is likely but not certain. The queen's name སུ་ལི་པ་ལ is a transcription I cannot identify with confidence and is kept as "Sulipala." ཤེས་རབ་འོད is rendered Prajñāprabha, with its meaning "Light of Wisdom." The names of the two kings, མ་ཧ་ཤ་ཀུ་ལི and ལི་ཤི་པ་ཙ, are transcriptions I cannot identify with confidence and are given as spelled, Mahāśakuli and Liśipaca. The prince's name སྡོང་དུམ is translated by its meaning, "Stump," since no Sanskrit original can be identified with confidence. དམག་པ, "soldier," in the description of the bridegroom is read as མག་པ, "bridegroom," which the context plainly requires; and སྡོང་དུ་མ is read as སྡོང་དུམ. The prince's new name སུ་ལན་ཤན is a transcription I cannot identify with confidence and is given as spelled, Sulanśan. སྐྱེ་དགུའི་བདག་མོ is rendered Prajāpatī, following the Tibetan, even though she is named here as the prince's wife. The number of the six teachers' followers is ཁྲི་ཕྲག་དགུ་བཅུ, nine hundred thousand, as the text states here, although an earlier passage gave ninety thousand. The pratyekabuddha's name, Drangsong Ri, is rendered literally as "Sage Mountain"; no Sanskrit form could be identified with confidence. The coin the oil presser says the pratyekabuddha cannot earn is a zho, a small unit of weight and money. The Buddha's illness, bser ma'i snyun, is a disorder associated with cold wind; "chill ailment" is an approximation. The "measures" are srang, a unit of weight (tael). In the list of fruits, phyir mi ldog pa stands for the non-returner, while phyir mi ldog pa'i sa is the irreversible ground of bodhisattvas. The beast's name, Kunta, is a phonetic form in the Tibetan; it is kept as written, since its Sanskrit original could not be identified with certainty. The verb bsngo in "constantly reviles the Blessed One" is read in its colloquial sense "to scold, revile" rather than "to dedicate." The householder's name, dpal skyes, is given as Śrījāta, the regular Sanskrit equivalent of the Tibetan, though the Indic original of this name in this story is not certain. In the list of fruits, phyir ldog pa stands for the once-returner and phyir mi ldog pa for the non-returner. The bandit named Asika is kept as the Tibetan spells it; his Sanskrit identity is uncertain. The "sweeper who throws away filth" is taken as a separate person from Upāli, the former barber. The disciple named Nabun is kept in the Tibetan form; his Sanskrit identity could not be established with confidence. The husband's name, sar phag chen po, is not securely identifiable; it may be a phonetic rendering of an Indian word such as sārtha (caravan) and is given here in phonetic form as "Great Sarphak". The cold hell chab brom is likewise not a standard name and is given phonetically as "Chabdrom". The phrase de'u re bral in Śrījāta's thought is obscure; it is taken as "lest I become separated". The first question about who delights to be born in the hells has no named speaker in the Tibetan. The monk's name ལི་ཏ is not securely identifiable and is given phonetically as "Lita". The king's name ཆོས་ཀྱི་འཕགས་པ is taken as Dharmodgata, its usual Sanskrit equivalent. The maidservant's phrase dge slong gis ma yogs pa'i lhag ma is obscure; it is taken as "the leftovers the monk has not finished". In the monk's reply, ཚངས་པར་གྱུར་ཅིག is read as "may you be purified," answering the young monks' confession (mthol tshangs), not as the god Brahmā. The phrase ལུས་སྨད་ལྔ is taken as the usual five-point prostration (lus kyi yan lag lnga). The country name ཨན་ཏ is given phonetically as "Anta"; its Indian identification is uncertain. Here རྡོ་རྗེ is the substance vajra (the ocean's floor and rim), not a personal name. The phrase "in four ways" introduces the four kinds of monks compared to mangoes. The word ཀེའུ་ལེ, the substance with which the feast seal is stamped on the wrist, is not identified; from the story it is something sweet, and it is kept as "keule". The brahmin who licks off the seal is then called a wandering ascetic (kun tu rgyu, parivrājaka), as in the Tibetan. The examples the novice recalls allude to well-known stories of monks who kept their precepts at the cost of life, among them the monks bound with grass by bandits, the monk who saw a goose swallow a jewel and kept silent though suspected and beaten, and the monk who gave his plank to a senior monk in a shipwreck. The word rendered "goose" (bya gag) denotes a goose or similar waterbird. The bones (rus bu) of the novice are rendered "remains," since the body has just died. The name of the limbless boy, ma 'ji byi la, could not be identified with a Sanskrit form and has no clear meaning in Tibetan; it is given as a phonetic rendering, Majibila. In the wife's speech, nor ba dag is read as nor bdag, "heir, master of the wealth," as the context plainly requires. The brothers' names, sbyin pa and tshul khrims, are rendered by their meanings, Generosity and Moral Discipline. The phrase rendered "stepped forward" (sar 'phags pa) is obscure; it may instead mean that he raised himself up or spoke loudly. The merchant's remark that the other party would not get a single coin "even if he were to become king" is taken literally; it may mean "even if the case went before the king." The buddha's name, rendered literally as Merit Protector (bsod skyabs), could not be identified with a Sanskrit form with confidence. The poor woman's name, Ke, is given as written. The nun's name dkar mo, "White One," is rendered Śuklā. The phrase rendered "the five robes" (chos gos smad lnga) is unusual and may refer to the set of robes of a nun. The place name a pa de is taken as Avanti, Mahākātyāyana's home region, where the parallel accounts set this story. In the line about the slave's rebirth, the Tibetan says a goddess (lha'i sras mo) had passed away and the old woman was born in her place, among the five hundred companions of the son of the gods. "The teaching on renunciation" renders འབྱུང་བའི་ཆོས, read as short for the standard formula's ངེས་པར་འབྱུང་བ (renunciation), which follows the teaching on impurity in this stock sequence. The well's description ཆུ་ཞེང་གབ་པ is obscure; it is rendered loosely as "square and level." The names Golden God and Golden Light are translated literally, since their Sanskrit forms in this text cannot be identified with confidence. The monk's name, རིགས་གཉིས་པ, is translated literally as "Two Families," since a Sanskrit form cannot be identified with confidence. In དད་ཀྱི་གནོང་ཤིག the verb is read as གནང་ཤིག, "please permit." ཁྱོད་རྟག་པར་ཉོན་ཅིག, literally "listen constantly," is rendered as the usual exhortation "listen well." The novice ཀྱུན་ཏེ is identified as Cunda, Śāriputra's novice attendant in the canonical accounts of his death; the earlier spelling ཀུན་ཏ (rendered "Kunta") may refer to a different figure. The grove ཨ་མྲའི་ཚལ is rendered "the Mango Grove." Ānanda's request, literally "in the Blessed One's former life too he died first and afterward [the Blessed One] passed away," is read as referring to Śāriputra dying first. The names of the two queens (Flower Giver, Great Eyes), the prince (Excellent Discipline) and the palace (Good Life) are translated literally, since their Sanskrit forms cannot be identified with confidence. ཆུ་ཞེང་གབ་པ is taken, as in an earlier passage, to mean "square." The brahmin's name, spelled ལེའུ་ཏེ་ཙ in the Tibetan, is identified as Raudrākṣa, the brahmin who begs King Candraprabha's head in the Indian versions of this story. ཀུན་ཏུ་རྒྱུ, "wandering ascetics" (parivrājakas), are then addressed as brahmins in the text. The princes' plea "Consider that we too are still learning by following you" renders the obscure phrase bdag cag kyang mi'i rjes su bslabs par dgongs; the sense is uncertain. "Leagues" renders the Tibetan dpag tshad (yojana). "It would not be borne" renders the unclear thugs mi thub bo in the brahmin's reply. The king's name here is spelled gsal thub rather than the usual gsal rgyal; it is taken as Prasenajit. The minister's name ri dags means "deer" and is given as Mṛga; in parallel versions of this story the minister is known as Mṛgadhara. Śiritita is a phonetic rendering of the Tibetan sound-spelling of the place name, which could not be identified with confidence. Balamitra is reconstructed from the Tibetan stobs grogs po ("strength-friend"). The mother's name, lha mtshams ma, could not be identified and is translated literally as "Divine Boundary". "Perished" renders the unusual kha dag.
